# 2:61: 78 questions

## 2:61/q01 Who is addressed and how are later Israelites included?  [وَإِذْ قُلْتُمْ · referent]
turns on: Whether the speech addresses ancestors directly or includes descendants through shared identity and approval.
- 2:61/q01/p1 Israel across generations is meant — TAB-FULL (من قال إنها مصر فرعون): Those arguing for Pharaoh's Egypt cite the statement that Israel inherited Egypt's gardens and goods, reasoning that inheritance would be of no benefit unless some Israelites went there. TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd identifies the people punished here as the Israelite Jews, explicitly distinguishing them from Egypt’s Copts. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Although Jews in Muhammad’s time did not personally kill the prophets, God calls them killers because they supported the earlier killers; the same collective attribution occurs in 5:81. TABRISI: The addressees of the recollection are the Israelites' forebears. TABRISI (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Hurayra reports the Prophet saying that Israelites divided 500 years after Moses, when captive-born offspring had become numerous, and divided again 200 years after Jesus. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم الأصفهاني): Abu Muslim argues that God bequeathed Egypt to the Israelites, so they could not be forbidden from entering it; he cites the report that God made them inherit it. BAYDAWI-FULL: Alternatively, it was one kind of food; though it suited people of luxury, the Israelites were farmers who longed for familiar fare. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو هريرة): A Prophetic report through Abu Hurayra says the Israelites split five hundred years after Moses when children of captives multiplied, and split again a hundred years after Jesus. IBNASHUR-FULL: The emphatic negator لن conveys the Israelites’ extreme vexation by making their refusal seem to extend through all time. IBNASHUR-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says the Israelites were agricultural people who yearned for their former habits. IBNASHUR-FULL: The third-person pronoun follows the apparent sense because these later Israelites are not the addressees; this is not التفات. IBNASHUR-FULL: Jeremiah was stoned after repeatedly rebuking and advising the Jews; Ibn ʿĀshūr notes disagreement about this account. [12 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد,من قال إنها مصر فرعون); WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); TABRISI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم الأصفهاني); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو هريرة); IBNASHUR-FULL(الحسن)]
- 2:61/q01/p2 The stated interpretation — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari answers that Israel's inheritance of Egypt's property made them its owners but did not mean they returned there; their homes were in Syria. WAHIDI-BASIT: Alternatively, the general wording may intend only a particular subset of people. IBNATIYYA-FULL: They bore God's wrath: baʾtu means to bear something, illustrated by Muhalhil's address to Yahya ibn al-Harith ibn ʿAbbad. IBNKATHIR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet defines arrogance as rejecting truth and despising people; Ibn Kathir uses this to characterize Israel's rejection and violence. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Israel's disbelief and killing of prophets brought God's irresistible punishment and disgrace in this world, joined to disgrace in the next as fitting recompense. BIQAI-FULL: Their address to Moses by his personal name shows a lack of deference. [6 notes: TAB-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNATIYYA-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:61/q01/p3 Later Israelites addressed through ancestors — WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Although Jews in Muhammad’s time did not personally kill the prophets, God calls them killers because they supported the earlier killers; the same collective attribution occurs in 5:81. WAHIDI-BASIT: The accusations do not apply to every Jew across all generations: some died earlier, some had only some traits, and those who approved or followed the perpetrators share their guilt; collective attribution can target only offenders and their supporters, as in 9:30. TABRISI: The addressees of the recollection are the Israelites' forebears. BIQAI-FULL: The narrative telescopes events from Moses and Joshua through Israel's later decline, aggression, and killing of prophets, presenting worldly authority and wealth as the reason for preferring the present life. BURSEVI: The verse recalls an earlier Israelite offense and ingratitude, addressing their descendants as the same people because of their shared identity. IBNASHUR-FULL: The third-person pronoun follows the apparent sense because these later Israelites are not the addressees; this is not التفات. [6 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); TABRISI; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q02 What provisions surrounded the wilderness episode?  [لَن نَّصْبِرَ عَلَىٰ طَعَامٍۢ وَٰحِدٍۢ · reports]
turns on: Different reports identify salwā as bird or honey and describe other wilderness gifts.
- 2:61/q02/p1 Manna was a sweet substance like honey — IBNHISHAM (ابن هشام): Manna was a sweet substance falling on the Israelites’ trees at dawn; they gathered and ate or drank it like honey. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari explains maghāfīr as a sweet substance resembling honey that descends from the sky and settles on trees. [2 notes: IBNHISHAM(ابن هشام); TAB-FULL]
- 2:61/q02/p2 Garments and a guiding light were supplied — MUQATIL: When asked about clothing, God made the Israelites' garments grow with their children, remain on adults, and neither tear, wear out, nor become dirty; a pillar of light illuminated their journeys at night without moonlight. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 2:61/q02/p3 Manna and salwā were satisfying foods — IBNHISHAM (أعشى بني قيس بن ثعلبة): Al-Aʿshā invokes manna and salwā as satisfying provisions, supporting their place as foods in the Israelites’ account. [1 notes: IBNHISHAM(أعشى بني قيس بن ثعلبة)]
- 2:61/q02/p4 Salwā was a bird, perhaps quail — IBNHISHAM (ابن هشام): Salwā is described as a bird, with singular salwāt; one reported identification is the quail. [1 notes: IBNHISHAM(ابن هشام)]
- 2:61/q02/p5 Salwā could mean honey — IBNHISHAM (خالد بن زهير الهذلي): Ibn Hishām also reports that salwā can mean honey, citing a line by Khālid ibn Zuhayr al-Hudhalī. [1 notes: IBNHISHAM(خالد بن زهير الهذلي)]
- 2:61/q02/p6 Clouds gave shade — TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says a cloud shaded the Israelites in the wilderness when they received manna and quail. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة)]

## 2:61/q03 Why did the Israelites ask for other food?  [لَن نَّصْبِرَ عَلَىٰ طَعَامٍۢ وَٰحِدٍۢ فَٱدْعُ لَنَا رَبَّكَ يُخْرِجْ لَنَا مِمَّا تُنۢبِتُ ٱلْأَرْضُ مِنۢ بَقْلِهَا وَقِثَّآئِهَا وَفُومِهَا وَعَدَسِهَا وَبَصَلِهَا · interpretation]
turns on: Former habits, monotony, desire for settlement, social dependence, or appetite and ingratitude.
- 2:61/q03/p1 They wanted other produce — AKHFASH: Alternatively, من can be treated as extra, as in “I saw no one” or “did a man come to you?” TAB-FULL: Kufr is covering or concealing; God’s signs are His proofs, evidences, and indications of His oneness and His messengers’ truthfulness. TAB-FULL: The charge means they denied God’s proofs of His oneness and the messengers’ truth, rejected their force, and called them lies. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفرزدق): His line to Muʿāwiya uses بؤت to mean ‘I would have acknowledged it,’ as if taking the place of one who confesses it. JISHUMI: Wāḥid can mean one, the first number, or what cannot be divided; God is one through uniqueness in His praiseworthy attributes. KASHSHAF-FULL: The requested greens are choice vegetables eaten by people, such as mint, celery and leek. TABRISI (الفرزدق): Al-Farazdaq's image of a spider's web spread over someone illustrates the idiom of something being laid upon them. TABRISI: Anger means the will to inflict harm; when attributed to God it means His will to punish the one under wrath. TABRISI: Wāḥid is the first number; God is one because He is distinguished by His most beautiful attributes. RAZI-FULL: Abu Muslim's side answers that ownership normally entails unrestricted use, and barring entry contradicts that default without evidence. QURTUBI-FULL: Asking someone to feed one a report means asking them to tell it; an imam's being asked to be fed means prompting him when he loses his recitation. QURTUBI-FULL: Second, God's gift came with a command to eat it, so continuing in obedience and gratitude brought afterlife reward, unlike their request. NASAFI-FULL: The requested greens are the earth's vegetables, especially choice herbs such as mint, celery and leek. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Wāḥid means indivisible or something to which no second is added; waḥada/yahidu means to be alone. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The pronoun could instead refer to God, supported by the ensuing command and promise. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبي): Ubayy reads أتبدلون; the wording is figurative because God alone effects the change, but their request makes them seem to change it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: A commentator mistakenly split Zuhayr al-Kisa'i into two people when attributing the anomalous reading; Zuhayr al-Kisa'i and al-Farqabi are one man. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The verb باء is used with good outcomes as well as evil ones, contrary to the claim that it occurs only for evil. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The definite wording can instead emphasize totality: no right existed, whether recognized by Muslims or anyone else. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الخليل بن أحمد): Al-Khalil ibn Ahmad subsisted on barley flour and fresh water, yet had an exceptionally bright mind and strong body, campaigning one year and making pilgrimage the next. SAMIN-DURR (أبو البقاء): Abu al-Baqa regards the indefinite-relative analysis as weak because the request refers to a specific thing, not an unspecified one. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says cities rarely exhibit extraordinary breaks in ordinary order; ascetics therefore seek travel and withdrawal from inhabited places to experience provision by God's word without the burden of His established order. BURSEVI: The clause beginning “He said” is a new sentence answering an implied question about what God or Moses replied. ALUSI-FULL: Calling the requested items simply ما avoids naming them directly as a sign of disdain. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): One reply distinguishes the prophets who were killed from the messengers promised help. ALUSI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas is reported to say that God decreed that seventy thousand people would be killed for each prophet and thirty-five thousand for each caliph; the author finds this answer unsound. IBNASHUR-FULL: The بدل family means putting one thing or quality in place of another, or compensating one thing with another. IBNASHUR-FULL (أبو الطيب): Abū al-Ṭayyib’s couplet offers a parallel reproach: if this year pleases you, return to Homs next year. [28 notes: AKHFASH; TAB-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفرزدق); JISHUMI; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(الفرزدق); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبي,الخليل بن أحمد); SAMIN-DURR(أبو البقاء); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(ابن عباس,قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو الطيب)]
- 2:61/q03/p2 Weariness and desire for variety — TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says they tired of their food and recalled the way of life they had before, which prompted their request for produce. IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatada says they tired of their food and recalled the life they had before. WAHIDI-BASIT (سعيد بن المسيب): Sa'id ibn al-Musayyib says the people had grown tired of their way of life. BAGHAWI-FULL: The Israelites grew weary of eating manna and quail. KASHSHAF-FULL: The people had been farmers; returning to their old habits made them weary of their blessing and desirous of misery. TABRISI (قتادة): Qatada says the people were shaded by clouds and given manna and quails in the wilderness, tired of them, recalled life in Egypt, and asked Moses for its produce. QURTUBI-FULL: The request occurred during the wandering, after they tired of manna and quail and remembered their former life in Egypt. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: They may have sought other foods to leave the wilderness, where those foods were unavailable; variety also improves appetite and digestion compared with a single repeated food. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says they had tired of their wilderness food and recalled the life they had before, prompting their request to Moses. BURSEVI: In the wilderness they tired of unchanging manna and quail, remembered their former agricultural life in Egypt, and longed for familiar habits. [10 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); WAHIDI-BASIT(سعيد بن المسيب); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(قتادة); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; DURR-FULL(قتادة); BURSEVI]
- 2:61/q03/p3 Longing for former food and habits — TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says they tired of their food and recalled the way of life they had before, which prompted their request for produce. TAB-FULL (قتادة): In another report, Qatada says that after reaching Syria they lacked their former foods and asked Moses for crops. IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatada says they tired of their food and recalled the life they had before. THALABI (الحسن): The Israelites were people accustomed to leeks, onions, and lentils; they reverted to their bad former habits and craved familiar foods, so they rejected manna and quails. KASHSHAF-FULL: The people had been farmers; returning to their old habits made them weary of their blessing and desirous of misery. TABRISI (قتادة): Qatada says the people were shaded by clouds and given manna and quails in the wilderness, tired of them, recalled life in Egypt, and asked Moses for its produce. QURTUBI-FULL: The request occurred during the wandering, after they tired of manna and quail and remembered their former life in Egypt. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says they had tired of their wilderness food and recalled the life they had before, prompting their request to Moses. BURSEVI: In the wilderness they tired of unchanging manna and quail, remembered their former agricultural life in Egypt, and longed for familiar habits. ELMALILI: While making a request, they should remember past deprivation and seek increase through gratitude and an elevated spirit. [10 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); THALABI(الحسن); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(قتادة); QURTUBI-FULL; DURR-FULL(قتادة); BURSEVI; ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q03/p4 Desire for settled life — IBNABIHATIM (السدي): Al-Suddi says that after leaving the wilderness, the manna and quails were withdrawn and the Israelites ate vegetables. KASHSHAF-FULL: The wilderness extended from Jerusalem to Qinnasrin, twelve farsakhs by eight. TABRISI (قتادة): Qatada says the people were shaded by clouds and given manna and quails in the wilderness, tired of them, recalled life in Egypt, and asked Moses for its produce. NASAFI-FULL: The things they requested are available in cities, not in the wilderness. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: They may have sought other foods to leave the wilderness, where those foods were unavailable; variety also improves appetite and digestion compared with a single repeated food. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Moses' point is that the requested produce is common in any city, so it is too lowly and plentiful to warrant asking God for it. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines asking as seeking what need calls for and what suffices, and says the requested goods are found in cities where God has established worldly wisdom, not in the wilderness. BIQAI-FULL: Israel denied the great gifts of manna, quail, and water by growing dissatisfied and requesting other food; their wilderness wandering was near at hand. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says they had tired of their wilderness food and recalled the life they had before, prompting their request to Moses. BURSEVI: In the wilderness they tired of unchanging manna and quail, remembered their former agricultural life in Egypt, and longed for familiar habits. [10 notes: IBNABIHATIM(السدي); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(قتادة); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(قتادة); BURSEVI]
- 2:61/q03/p5 Desire for social differences — QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): Another interpretation is that they could not bear universal prosperity, which left no one needing help; the passage says they were the first to use slaves and servants. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q03/p6 Appetite and ingratitude — SAMIN-DURR: طعم with fatḥa can mean the verbal noun, something desired as food, or what taste perceives, as in sweet and bitter taste. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:61/q04 What does their claim that they cannot endure mean?  [لَن نَّصْبِرَ · meaning]
- 2:61/q04/p1 Restraint or endurance of the self — TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the command means a town; he takes it to be the holy land God assigned to Israel, citing 5:21. JISHUMI: Ṣabr is restraint of the self from what it desires, opposite to panic; the cited poem contrasts patience with panic. JISHUMI (الحسن وأبو العالية والربيع والأعمش): Another view identifies the destination as Pharaoh’s Egypt; another identifies it as Jerusalem, where they could obtain the plants they wanted. JISHUMI (قيل): Another explanation says they had grown tired of the wilderness and were too ashamed to state that plainly, so they hinted at it indirectly. BAGHAWI-FULL: The familiar reading omits the hamza; one explanation is that it was dropped for ease because the word is used frequently. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash permits inflecting the name because of its lightness and resemblance to Hind and Daʿd; Sibawayh does not permit this. IBNATIYYA-FULL (غير الأخفش): Another explanation says the word is inflected because it denotes a place. TABRISI: Another explanation says they sought produce so the poor could assist the rich, rather than having everyone equally rich and independent. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Baqalat and abqalat are both eloquent forms for land sprouting vegetation; ibtaqalat describes camels grazing. RAZI-FULL: Their request may have been driven by weariness with the wilderness and a desire to reach settled lands, rather than by the foods themselves. RAZI-FULL: An objection says the command to enter the Holy Land may be recommendatory and therefore need not bar entry into Egypt; Razi invokes the principle that an imperative is presumptively obligatory. RAZI-FULL: Even if entering the Holy Land were only recommended, allowing the prophets to abandon a recommended duty would be improper. RAZI-FULL: A reply says do not turn back can mean do not disobey the command or deny the Holy Land's superiority, or the prohibition may apply only for a specified time; Razi finds that limitation contrary to the apparent sense. RAZI-FULL: Razi says the context makes do not turn back refer to the immediately preceding order to enter the Holy Land, while restricting the prohibition to a particular time departs from its apparent meaning. RAZI-FULL: An objection says owning a house does not always permit entry, as when a person observing mosque retreat owns a home; perhaps Israel's inheritance meant authority while the Holy Land command barred entry. QURTUBI-FULL (عكرمة عن ابن عباس): ʿIkrimah transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's explanation: a town among these towns. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): The tanwin may still indicate an unspecified city, specifically one among the cities of the Holy Land, as supported by the command to enter the Holy Land. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another explanation treats the phrase as emphatic because no prophet ever brought anything warranting death; those killed were hated, and their killing raised their rank. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: One explanation takes definite الحق as an allusion to the established rule in the Prophetic hadith that Muslim blood is protected except in three cases. SAMIN-DURR: On the near-in-this-world interpretation, the superior food's benefit is obtained in the afterlife through patience. SAMIN-DURR: Alternatively it may be an accusative circumstantial phrase describing the omitted pronoun returning to ما; من remains explanatory. SAMIN-DURR: The Prophet's ban on saying راعنا is analogous: the Jews could exploit that word as an insult in their language; alternatively, it encouraged choosing the most eloquent language. BIQAI-FULL: لن نصبر means they would not endure the food for long. BURSEVI (قيل): An alternative explanation is that they could not bear everyone being self-sufficient, since mutual aid would disappear; they were also early adopters of servants and slaves. BURSEVI: This community would not have spoken like Israel because of its brighter lights and penetrating spiritual insight; it is the balanced community of 2:143. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another explanation confines the prohibition to early Islam, when Jewish subterfuge was prevalent, as with replacing راعنا by انظرنا. IBNASHUR-FULL: The episode shifts from favors to Israel's poor choice of appetite, which may still be lawful, but their words show discourtesy toward Moses and the benefactor: ‘we cannot endure’ casts the given food as hateful. [27 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); JISHUMI(الحسن وأبو العالية والربيع والأعمش,قيل); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الأخفش,غير الأخفش); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(عكرمة عن ابن عباس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI(قيل); ALUSI-FULL(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q05 Was their request sinful?  [فَٱدْعُ لَنَا رَبَّكَ · law]
turns on: Whether the request rejects God's choice, and whether rebuke or a grant establishes sin.
- 2:61/q05/p1 Sinful ingratitude — IBNABIHATIM (الحسن): Al-Hasan says they were ungrateful, recalled their former life, and requested the vegetables and foods they used to eat. WAHIDI-BASIT: They called it one food because manna with quail was eaten together like one dish and presented as one color, although made from different ingredients. JISHUMI: Some say the request was not sinful because both foods were permitted; others say it was sinful because they resented God’s choice, which the author regards as stronger. TABRISI: The more apt view is that the request was sinful because they were reproached for refusing what God chose. RAZI-FULL (الظاهريون من المفسرين): Most literalist commentators hold that the request for another kind of food was sinful, but Razi rejects this view. Against — RAZI-FULL: Razi argues that manna and quail had been permitted rather than commanded, so asking for another food was permissible, including asking through Moses because that seemed likelier to be answered. Against — RAZI-FULL: Razi says the rhetorical rebuke may concern losing a greater worldly benefit or a greater afterlife benefit, rather than sin in the request itself. RAZI-FULL: An objection reads their inability to endure one food as proof they disliked the manna and quail, making the request sinful. Against — RAZI-FULL: Razi replies that they expressed desire for another food, not dissatisfaction with what they had; the future negation in lan does not establish resentment toward the present food. RAZI-FULL: A counterargument says guaranteed food disliked by their tastes was harder to consume than desired food obtained with effort; Razi answers that certain present benefit still outweighs uncertain distant benefit. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Abū Ḥayyān replies that saying they could not endure one food shows a desire for other foods, not necessarily dissatisfaction with what God provided; a rebuke may concern forfeiting worldly or afterlife benefit. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another interpretation says they meant they could not endure everyone being wealthy and no one needing to serve another; the one food represents their common status in the wilderness before social differences returned. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Because replacing one food kind with another is a reasonable aim and the Qurʾān does not say they were forbidden to ask, the request cannot be treated as sinful. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الحسن البصري): Al-Hasan says they showed ingratitude, would not endure the provision, and recalled the diet they had lived on before. ALUSI-FULL: The author endorses al-Salikuti's judgment that al-Razi's inference is weak: granting the request does not make their ungrateful demand virtuous. ALUSI-FULL: Although inability to endure one food might imply wanting an addition, their aversion and ingratitude imply a wish to remove the blessing and replace it. ELMALILI: Monotony may bore human desire, but seeking variety is not itself sinful. IBNASHUR-FULL: This sentence continues the preceding pattern of recounting God's favors, whether pure favors or favors mixed with ingratitude and subsequent pardon, so its content should also be a favor. IBNASHUR-FULL: A reading transmitted through al-Fakhr and explained by ʿAbd al-Ḥakīm says asking to replace manna and quail was sinful ingratitude: ‘patience’ implies dislike, and the rebuke confirms it; the favor was pardon and the concession to go down. IBNASHUR-FULL: God's answer withdraws special care: instead of providing food, water, and shade as before, He orders them to go to a town and procure their own, a disciplinary rebuke for their attitude rather than punishment for a sinful request. IBNASHUR-FULL: Worldly deprivation and loss of spiritual gifts are not consequences of a legal sin under divine command; they resemble causal ordering in the world's system. Only afterlife punishment proves the act was sinful, resolving the interpretive difficulty. IBNASHUR-FULL: The jussive يخرج replaces an expected أن يخرج, presenting the result as certain if Moses asks and pressing him to act for their benefit. IBNASHUR-FULL: The واء rather than فاء makes the punishment an independently asserted report of what their ingratitude merited, not merely a consequence attached to Moses’s rebuke. [19 notes: IBNABIHATIM(الحسن); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI; RAZI-FULL(الظاهريون من المفسرين); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(الحسن البصري); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABRISI+; RAZI-FULL-; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:61/q05/p2 The rebuke censures their choice — WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو العباس): He derives the root from dwelling or a dwelling place: to bear a sin is to come to occupy the position of the guilty, and the other senses of carrying, returning, and acknowledging converge on settling in a place. RAZI-FULL: Razi says the rhetorical rebuke may concern losing a greater worldly benefit or a greater afterlife benefit, rather than sin in the request itself. RAZI-FULL: The phrase that was because they disobeyed restates the cause in different words, like a master listing a servant's past offenses with varied rebukes. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Abū Ḥayyān replies that saying they could not endure one food shows a desire for other foods, not necessarily dissatisfaction with what God provided; a rebuke may concern forfeiting worldly or afterlife benefit. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: They wearied of the provision and asked Moses to intercede for familiar foods; he rebuked them for trading precious, effortless manna and quails for laborious, harmful foods that stole worship time and impaired body and perception. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Moses rebukes the Israelites for seeking lowly foods while they enjoyed abundant, pleasant, beneficial provision. ALUSI-FULL: The interrogative rebukes them, and الاستبدال means exchanging one thing for another. IBNASHUR-FULL (موسى): One attribution makes the rebuke beginning أتستبدلون Moses’s speech. IBNASHUR-FULL (الله): Another attribution makes the rebuke beginning أتستبدلون God's speech. IBNASHUR-FULL: The question is a severe rebuke, stripped of persuasion and warning, and expresses astonishment at their foolish choice. [10 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو العباس); RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(الله,موسى)]
- 2:61/q05/p3 Permissible request for lawful variety — JISHUMI: Some say the request was not sinful because both foods were permitted; others say it was sinful because they resented God’s choice, which the author regards as stronger. RAZI-FULL: Razi argues that manna and quail had been permitted rather than commanded, so asking for another food was permissible, including asking through Moses because that seemed likelier to be answered. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Their request was permissible: the earlier eat-and-drink command was permission, so people allowed different foods may reasonably ask for another kind directly or through a messenger. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The response, descend to a town and you will have what you asked, confirms that the request was granted; granting a sinful request would be impermissible for prophets. Against — ALUSI-FULL: The author endorses al-Salikuti's judgment that al-Razi's inference is weak: granting the request does not make their ungrateful demand virtuous. ELMALILI: Monotony may bore human desire, but seeking variety is not itself sinful. [5 notes: JISHUMI; RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ELMALILI; ALUSI-FULL-]
- 2:61/q05/p4 Lawful but discourteous request — IBNASHUR-FULL: God's answer withdraws special care: instead of providing food, water, and shade as before, He orders them to go to a town and procure their own, a disciplinary rebuke for their attitude rather than punishment for a sinful request. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q06 How is the final rāʾ of نَّصْبِرَ treated at pause?  [نَّصْبِرَ · readings]
- 2:61/q06/p1 Pause treatment depends on rāʾ and preceding sound — NASHR: A final rāʾ that is open, including نصبر, is stopped on with sukūn only; the author sets out parallel categories for still, transferred-kasra and other final rāʾs. NASHR: When final rāʾ has a permanent lexical or inflectional kasra, pause permits rawm or sukūn; a nominative ending permits rawm, ishāmām, or sukūn. NASHR: At pause with sukūn or ishāmām, tarqīq is the established rule when the preceding sound is kasra, sukūn after kasra, silent yāʾ, imāla, or a soft rāʾ; otherwise the rāʾ is tafkhīm. NASHR: Some soften a rāʾ with its own kasra at pause on the ground that pause makes the preceding state temporary; the author notes a possible distinction between temporary and permanent kasra. NASHR: With rawm, a kasra softens rāʾ for all; a ḍamma is softened only by Warsh through al-Azraq after specified triggers, while other cases are tafkhīm, with some distinction for a kasra ending. [5 notes: NASHR]

## 2:61/q07 What was the 'one food,' and why was it called one?  [طَعَامٍۢ وَٰحِدٍۢ · referent]
turns on: Whether one describes a repeated regimen, mixed meal, food class, single item, or shared prosperity.
- 2:61/q07/p1 Manna and quail together — TAB-FULL (ابن أبي نجيح): Ibn Abi Najih says their one food was manna and quail, which they sought to replace with vegetables and the other listed crops. IBNABIHATIM (أبو العالية): The Israelites had quails for food and manna for drink, and said they could not endure that single fare. THALABI: Although manna and quail are two foods, the Arabs can describe two things with the singular “one”; he cites the pearls and coral verse, although they come only from the salt sea. THALABI (عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): They kneaded manna and quail together, making one food that they ate. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says manna was their food and quail their drink, but the two were combined when eaten and therefore counted as one food. BAGHAWI-FULL: Although manna and quail are two foods, Arabs may refer to two as one and one as two; the pearl-and-coral verse is an example, though only the salt sea produces them. BAGHAWI-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): ʿAbd al-Rahman ibn Zayd ibn Aslam says they kneaded manna with quail, making one food. KASHSHAF-FULL: The single food means manna and quails, called one because they did not vary; even a varied dailymenu could be called one food if unchanged. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Atiyya says all of these grounds together establish the superiority of manna and quail. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says manna and quail are superior in value, since the requested vegetables have little worth. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The Israelites spoke in the wilderness after tiring of manna and quail and recalling their former life in Egypt; the two foods are called one food because they ate them together and repeatedly received the same pair. TABRISI: They say they cannot endure one food: manna and quails are called one because the same daily food repeated from day to day, even if it offered varieties. TABRISI: A further interpretation contrasts farmed and crafted food with manna and quails given freely. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: The Israelites said this while in the wilderness; by one food they meant manna and quail. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (محمد بن القاسم): They counted manna and quail as one food because the manna was eaten with quail and the quail with manna. QURTUBI-FULL: Fifth, manna and quail were certainly lawful as gifts from God, whereas dealings in crops could involve usurpation and doubt. QURTUBI-FULL: They called manna and quail one food though they were two because they ate one together with the other. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): Another view says the two foods repeated daily, so they were called one regular fare, like a person constant in one practice. BAYDAWI-FULL: The single food they could no longer bear was manna and quails from the wilderness. NASAFI-FULL: The one food was manna and quail; it was called one because it did not change, as a person may call a daily unchanging menu one food despite its variety. NASAFI-FULL: Alternatively, they considered manna and quail one kind because both were refined foods, while they wanted the vegetables and grains familiar to an agricultural people. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the Israelites mixed manna and quail, making them like one color that combines several things. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Some take the one food to mean quail, since manna was a drink or sweet accompaniment and only quail counted as food to them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Others compare calling two foods one to calling one source two, as in the verse about pearl and coral coming from two seas although they come only from the salty sea. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another view says they spoke while only one of the two foods was being sent down. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī explains one food as the unchanged manna and quail, which they called one because it did not vary from day to day. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Another explanation is that manna and quail were one class of luxurious fare, while the Israelites as agricultural people wanted familiar varied crops and vegetables. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزجاج): The first explanation measures superiority by value: vegetables are of little worth beside the more valuable manna and quails. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): A second explanation says manna and quails were God's gift and commanded food, so continuing to obey and thank Him earned reward in the afterlife, unlike the requested food. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): A fourth explanation values freedom from toil: manna and quails came without labor, while crops required cultivation and care. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): A fifth explanation values lawful purity: manna and quails came from God free of the defects, usurpation, unlawful elements, and doubts that can affect crops. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): A sixth explanation says manna and quails are superior in the kind of nourishment and benefit they provide. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The request refers to the vegetables and grains they chose over manna and quails. SAMIN-DURR: Although manna and quails are two foods, واحد can mean one unchanging, nonvarying kind. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): They may call the two items one because both are luxury foods, whereas they wanted their familiar varied crops. BURSEVI: The paired manna and quail counted as one food because eaten together; alternatively, “one” means an unvaried diet even if it has several dishes. ALUSI-FULL: It is even less plausible that “one food” meant only quails because manna was a drink or sweetener and therefore supposedly did not count as another food. ALUSI-FULL: Their declaration that they could not endure one food signals aversion to the manna and quails and failure to thank God for an effortless provision. ALUSI-FULL: One food can mean a single, unchanging regimen, even if it consists of the two foods manna and quails. ALUSI-FULL: Another explanation is that the Israelites sought familiar agricultural produce because manna and quails were luxurious foods unlike their usual fare. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): A reported explanation says they cooked manna and quails together, making one dish. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): A reported view says they explicitly sought replacement, and the reply signals that receiving the requested food would mean losing manna and quails rather than combining the foods. IBNASHUR-FULL: The author reads lan as extending negation throughout every time of ‘endure’: they announce they will no longer eat manna and quail, pressuring Moses to ask at once and implying they have made acceptance impossible. IBNASHUR-FULL: They call manna and quail one food because it recurred every day, although it consisted of two things. [44 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن أبي نجيح); IBNABIHATIM(أبو العالية); THALABI(عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن زيد); BAGHAWI-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الزجاج); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(محمد بن القاسم); QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن زيد,الزجاج,الزمخشري,قيل); SAMIN-DURR(قيل); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL+]
- 2:61/q07/p2 The wilderness fare is called one food — ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة): The Israelites tired of their single food and remembered their former life, so they asked Moses for produce from the earth. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari recalls that patience means restraining and holding the self back, and interprets the Israelites as saying they could not endure one food. TAB-FULL (بعض أهل التأويل): Some commentators identify the single food God provided in the wilderness as quail. TAB-FULL (وهب بن منبه): Wahb ibn Munabbih instead says the single food was fine bread with meat. TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada glosses the requested food as worse than the food God had given them. IBNABIHATIM (السدي): Al-Suddi says they became sated or weary of that food and said they could not endure one fare. IBNABIHATIM: The transmitters explain their phrase as being sated or disliking the food. THALABI: The good and evil may refer to kinds of food, or to God’s choice for them as against their own choices. BAGHAWI-FULL: Another view is that they ate one food together with the other, making them a single meal. RAZI-FULL: A monotonous diet can weaken appetite and digestion; variety can strengthen appetite and increase pleasure, making a change of food reasonable. QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj argues that the people would not request food without grain, the foundation of nourishment. QURTUBI-FULL: Fourth, God's food came without effort, while their requested food required cultivation, agriculture, and toil. QURTUBI-FULL: In ordinary usage, a food market means the place where food is sold, not every place selling something edible or drinkable. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The author counts nine reported explanations for one food, including the two explanations attributed to al-Zamakhsharī on the preceding page. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: This sequence of five foods follows their differing temperamental qualities. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another interpretation takes the request to mean their reliance on managing their own livelihoods and food supplies. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): Another explanation is that they ate one food together with the other, or ate both at the same time. SAMIN-DURR: طعم with ḍamma means the tasted food; Abu Khirash's poem uses الطعم first for the food itself and then for its desirability. BIQAI-FULL: Their request that God bring food is made even if Moses himself is not attending to that matter. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines food as what sustains the eater and becomes part of him, linking the word to the command to look at one's food. BURSEVI: Believers’ recoil from disobeying God resembles one’s recoil from poisoned food. ALUSI-FULL: The author calls it a weak account that the request preceded the descent of the quails. IBNASHUR-FULL: The author finds al-Fakhr's proposed favor very remote because the text never establishes that they actually received the food they requested. [23 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(بعض أهل التأويل,قتادة,وهب بن منبه); IBNABIHATIM(السدي); THALABI; BAGHAWI-FULL; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR(قيل); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q07/p3 An unchanging daily regimen — JISHUMI: They call it one food because their daily diet did not change; alternatively Arabic can use the singular for a pair and vice versa, as in the verse about pearls and coral. KASHSHAF-FULL: The single food means manna and quails, called one because they did not vary; even a varied dailymenu could be called one food if unchanged. TABRISI: They say they cannot endure one food: manna and quails are called one because the same daily food repeated from day to day, even if it offered varieties. RAZI-FULL: One food means a single unchanging menu or manner of eating, not a single food species. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): Another view says the two foods repeated daily, so they were called one regular fare, like a person constant in one practice. BAYDAWI-FULL: Its oneness means it did not vary or change, as in the expression that an emir's table has one food because its colors do not change. NASAFI-FULL: The one food was manna and quail; it was called one because it did not change, as a person may call a daily unchanging menu one food despite its variety. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī explains one food as the unchanged manna and quail, which they called one because it did not vary from day to day. SAMIN-DURR: Although manna and quails are two foods, واحد can mean one unchanging, nonvarying kind. BIQAI-FULL: Their complaint about one food means they did not want to remain with an unchanging provision, even though it was varied, noble, and effortless. ALUSI-FULL: One food can mean a single, unchanging regimen, even if it consists of the two foods manna and quails. IBNASHUR-FULL: They call manna and quail one food because it recurred every day, although it consisted of two things. [12 notes: JISHUMI; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q07/p4 Quail alone counted as food — IBNABIHATIM (أبو العالية): The Israelites had quails for food and manna for drink, and said they could not endure that single fare. THALABI: Although manna and quail are two foods, the Arabs can describe two things with the singular “one”; he cites the pearls and coral verse, although they come only from the salt sea. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says manna was their food and quail their drink, but the two were combined when eaten and therefore counted as one food. BAGHAWI-FULL: Although manna and quail are two foods, Arabs may refer to two as one and one as two; the pearl-and-coral verse is an example, though only the salt sea produces them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Some take the one food to mean quail, since manna was a drink or sweet accompaniment and only quail counted as food to them. Against — ALUSI-FULL: It is even less plausible that “one food” meant only quails because manna was a drink or sweetener and therefore supposedly did not count as another food. [5 notes: IBNABIHATIM(أبو العالية); THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن زيد); BAGHAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); ALUSI-FULL-]
- 2:61/q07/p5 A single mixed or jointly eaten meal — THALABI (عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): They kneaded manna and quail together, making one food that they ate. BAGHAWI-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): ʿAbd al-Rahman ibn Zayd ibn Aslam says they kneaded manna with quail, making one food. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the Israelites mixed manna and quail, making them like one color that combines several things. BURSEVI: The paired manna and quail counted as one food because eaten together; alternatively, “one” means an unvaried diet even if it has several dishes. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): A reported explanation says they cooked manna and quails together, making one dish. [5 notes: THALABI(عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم); BAGHAWI-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن زيد); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q07/p6 One class of luxury foods — RAZI-FULL: They may have tired of eating one kind of food for forty years and wanted something different. NASAFI-FULL: Alternatively, they considered manna and quail one kind because both were refined foods, while they wanted the vegetables and grains familiar to an agricultural people. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Another explanation is that manna and quail were one class of luxurious fare, while the Israelites as agricultural people wanted familiar varied crops and vegetables. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): They may call the two items one because both are luxury foods, whereas they wanted their familiar varied crops. ALUSI-FULL: Another explanation is that the Israelites sought familiar agricultural produce because manna and quails were luxurious foods unlike their usual fare. [5 notes: RAZI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); SAMIN-DURR(قيل); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q07/p7 Arabic singular can cover two items — THALABI: Although manna and quail are two foods, the Arabs can describe two things with the singular “one”; he cites the pearls and coral verse, although they come only from the salt sea. JISHUMI: They call it one food because their daily diet did not change; alternatively Arabic can use the singular for a pair and vice versa, as in the verse about pearls and coral. BAGHAWI-FULL: Although manna and quail are two foods, Arabs may refer to two as one and one as two; the pearl-and-coral verse is an example, though only the salt sea produces them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Others compare calling two foods one to calling one source two, as in the verse about pearl and coral coming from two seas although they come only from the salty sea. [4 notes: THALABI; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q07/p8 Only one food had yet been sent — TABRISI: An alternative account says only manna first descended; they tired of it and then quails were sent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another explanation says they received manna alone for a period, became tired of it, and then received quail alone and tired of that. Against — ALUSI-FULL: The author calls it a weak account that the request preceded the descent of the quails. [2 notes: TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); ALUSI-FULL-]
- 2:61/q07/p9 A shared condition of abundance — SAMIN-DURR (قيل): Calling it one food may allude to shared wealth: they did not want everyone to share one thing, since that would leave no one serving another; they were said to be the first to use servants and slaves. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(قيل)]

## 2:61/q08 What does ṭaʿām mean here and in Arabic usage?  [طَعَامٍۢ · meaning]
- 2:61/q08/p1 Food or nourishment, including what is eaten or drunk — NAHHAS: يا موسى is a singular vocative; لن makes نصبر subjunctive, على makes طعام genitive, and واحد is its adjective. JISHUMI: Ṭaʿām means food or nourishment; ṭaʿm with ḍamma means eating, ṭaʿm also means taste, and food can denote nourishing substance. TABRISI: Ṭabrisī defines food as nourishment, ṭaʿm as eating, and food also as a physical substance perceived by taste. QURTUBI-FULL: An unnamed poet describes ostriches at Wajrah as tasting sleep only while fasting, illustrating the idiom. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو خراش): Abū Khirāsh's poetry uses ṭaʿm first for food and then for its savor or desirability. QURTUBI-FULL: Ṭaʿām can mean anything eaten or drunk; Qur'anic 'taste' can include drinking wine, and honey as salwā would also be a drink. QURTUBI-FULL: Food can conventionally mean grain or dates specifically; Abū Saʿīd's report on zakāt al-fiṭr contrasts a measure of food with barley. QURTUBI-FULL: With fatḥah, ṭaʿm can mean what taste conveys or something desirable; with ḍammah it means food. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Ṭaʿām means whatever is eaten, analogously to ʿaṭāʾ for whatever is given, and is a generic noun. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): One explanation follows the singular grammatical form of ṭaʿām, not the fact that it refers to two foods. SAMIN-DURR: لن نصبر على طعام واحد is a subject–predicate clause, and the whole clause is an object of the verb of saying. SAMIN-DURR: The phrase ما يطعم النوم means does not sleep; a poem uses it for ostriches that sleep only standing. SAMIN-DURR: طعام can mean anything eaten or drunk, as in 2:249, but sometimes refers specifically to certain foods such as grain or dates; a charity hadith distinguishes a measure of food from barley. SAMIN-DURR: طعم can also mean giving; the prophetic report إذا استطعمكم الإمام فأطعموه means prompt the imam when he asks for a cue. [14 notes: NAHHAS; JISHUMI; TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو خراش); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:61/q09 How is فَٱدْعُ pronounced and parsed?  [فَٱدْعُ · readings]
turns on: The usual imperative vowel versus the Banū ʿĀmir dialect and its grammatical explanation.
- 2:61/q09/p1 Kasra in the Banū ʿĀmir form — NAHHAS: فادع is a request functioning like an imperative, so its wāw is dropped; Banū ʿĀmir pronounce its ʿayn with kasra at the consonant junction. IBNATIYYA-FULL: In the dialect of Banu Amir, the ʿayn of فادع is pronounced with kasra. QURTUBI-FULL: Banū ʿĀmir pronounce fa-dʿu with kasrah on ʿayn at the consonant meeting, treating the defective verb like a sound verb. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بنو عامر): The Banū ʿĀmir dialect pronounces the command fa-dʿi with kasra on the ʿayn, treating daʿā as a weak verb like ramā-yarmī. [4 notes: NAHHAS; IBNATIYYA-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بنو عامر)]
- 2:61/q09/p2 The imperative has a dialectal form — ZAJJAJ: He prefers construing the following verb as the response to an imperative, as with “tell My servants to establish prayer”; Qur’anic imperatives without a jussive particle are otherwise indicative. SAMIN-DURR: The dialect may instead reflect a verb pattern دعى يدعي like رمى يرمي. SAMIN-DURR: لنا attaches to ادع, and its lām expresses purpose. [3 notes: SAMIN-DURR; ZAJJAJ+]

## 2:61/q10 What are they asking Moses to do?  [فَٱدْعُ لَنَا رَبَّكَ · interpretation]
- 2:61/q10/p1 They ask Moses to petition God for them — THALABI: Fa-dʿu means “ask,” and lanā means “for us.” WAHIDI-BASIT (قتادة): Qatada says they remembered their former Egyptian food and asked Moses to pray for it; ta'am is a generic term for what is eaten. WAHIDI-BASIT (أصحاب المعاني): Some linguists say food was called one because their daily nourishment never changed, like someone described as following one regular course by keeping to fasting and prayer. WAHIDI-BASIT: The petition is expressed in the past tense as an optimistic expression that it has already happened. BAGHAWI-FULL: “Ask for us” means ask God on our behalf. BAYDAWI-FULL: They ask Moses to petition his Lord on their behalf. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The Israelites were weary of the wilderness and repetitive food, distant from their familiar land and habits; they expressed impatience and asked Moses to petition God for them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The second ذلك may repeat the earlier reference to punishment, or point to disbelief and killing; on the latter reading it is neither repetition nor emphasis. BURSEVI: The request asks Moses to supplicate for them; the connective “so” makes their inability to endure the diet the cause of the request. ALUSI-FULL: The phrase asks Moses to pray on their behalf for the listed produce; the conjunction marks their impatience as the reason for asking. ALUSI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet asked ʿUmar to include him among those for whom he prayed. ALUSI-FULL (الأثر): A transmitted saying urges prayer through tongues that have not disobeyed God, understood here as other people's tongues. IBNASHUR-FULL: On either reading, both باءs are causal; Ibn ʿĀshūr rejects making one mean مع, since repetition can replace conjunction without the strained reading in al-Kashshāf and al-Ṭībī. [13 notes: THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(أصحاب المعاني,قتادة); BAGHAWI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الأثر,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q11 What does the supplication word mean?  [فَٱدْعُ · meaning]
- 2:61/q11/p1 Supplication is a humble request to one above — THALABI (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda says the phrase means they bore and acknowledged it, comparing it to the prophetic supplication “I acknowledge Your favor upon me and my sin”; God’s anger means blame, threat, and punishment, and likewise hatred and wrath. WAHIDI-BASIT: Dua means asking one who can grant benefit or harm; it is named for the call used by a petitioner. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن السراج): Ibn al-Sarraj says dua originally means calling; a request is called dua because the petitioner says Lord and calls upon God. TABRISI (ابن السراج): Ibn al-Sarraj derives duʿāʾ, supplication, from nidāʾ, calling. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Duʿāʾ is calling aloud by the name of the person invoked, from daʿā/yadʿū. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: A Prophetic supplication uses باء for both a blessing and a sin, confirming the two usages. SAMIN-DURR: دعاء here means asking, but can also mean naming or calling, as in the quoted poem دعتني أخاها. BURSEVI: Bursevī cites al-Baghawī’s note that Arabic can use a dual form for one thing, as in the phrase about pearls and coral, which come only from salt water. ALUSI-FULL: The proposal that a singular “one” stands for two foods is still less plausible; the cited comparison uses a dual expression for pearls that come from only one sea. ALUSI-FULL: Calling God “Lord” prepares the grounds for the prayer's acceptance. [10 notes: THALABI(أبو عبيدة); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن السراج); TABRISI(ابن السراج); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q12 Why did they say 'your Lord'?  [رَبَّكَ · rhetoric]
turns on: Moses' special standing versus a perceived lack of reverence in the Israelites' wording.
- 2:61/q12/p1 Their wording shows distance or irreverence — BIQAI-FULL: They attribute the title Lord to Moses rather than themselves despite God's repeated manifestation to them as benefactor. ELMALILI: They should preserve proper reverence rather than say “your Lord” where “our Lord” is due, which displays faithlessness. [2 notes: BIQAI-FULL; ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q12/p2 They invoke Moses' relationship with God — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Fa-dʿu lanā rabbaka means ask your Lord for us; the object of the prayer is omitted and understood as asking Him to produce the requested foods. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:61/q12/p3 Moses' special closeness to God — ALUSI-FULL: They asked Moses to pray because prophetic prayers are more likely to be answered; more generally, one person's prayer for another is especially apt to be answered. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q13 How does يُخْرِجْ depend on the preceding request?  [يُخْرِجْ · grammar]
turns on: Imperative response, an implied command, supplication, or omitted command lām.
- 2:61/q13/p1 The verb is jussive in the petition — AKHFASH: The added من is not confined to negation and questions: it appears in the cited verse and in ordinary expressions such as “Zayd is among the best.” AKHFASH: Common idioms omit an understood noun: “Do you want …?” means “Do you have a need for …?”, and “no harm on you” is similarly elliptical. KASHSHAF-FULL: The verb 'bring forth' means to make appear and produce. TABRISI: Another parse makes yukh’rij jussive after an implicit command lām, as in the cited verse from Ibrahim 14:31. TABRISI (الفراء): Al-Farra states that deleting the command lām is allowed only in poetry, where meter forces it. TABRISI (أبو زيد): Abu Zayd cites poetry with yaqūm jussive without the command lām as evidence for its omission. TABRISI: Another poetic example has adʿu coordinated with udʿu after omission of the command lām. TABRISI: A further poetic example makes tafdi jussive with the implied sense li-tafdi. RAZI-FULL: The recognized reading has yukh'rij with yaḍamma and ra kasra, and tanbut with taḍamma and ta kasra. RAZI-FULL (زيد بن علي): Zayd b. ʿAlī reads yukh'rij with ya fatḥa and ra ḍamma, and tanbut with ta fatḥa and ba ḍamma. NASAFI-FULL: They ask Moses to ask God to bring forth food; the verbs mean to ask and to manifest or produce. SAMIN-DURR: When a damma before و creates a long vowel, that length blocks assimilation between identical following sounds, so the letters remain distinct, as in آمنوا وعملوا and الذي يوسوس. SAMIN-DURR: The standard imperative is ادع with ḍamma; Banu Amir use ادع with kasra. SAMIN-DURR: The preposition من may attach to the verb and mark the point of origin. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): An alternative attaches من الله directly to the verb, understanding رجعوا من الله بغضب; the author considers this weak. SAMIN-DURR: من الله most naturally modifies غضب as an adjective, with an implied كائن; من marks metaphorical origin. ALUSI-FULL (بنو عامر): Banu ʿAmir read the imperative with kasra on its final letter, treating دعا like the defective verb رمى. [17 notes: AKHFASH; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(أبو زيد,الفراء); RAZI-FULL(زيد بن علي); NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(قيل); ALUSI-FULL(بنو عامر)]
- 2:61/q13/p2 Jussive response to imperative — NAHHAS: يخرج لنا is jussive as the response to a command with conditional force. WAHIDI-BASIT: Yukhrij means asking God to bring something out; the following imperfect serves as the response to the preceding command, as in two cited verses. IBNATIYYA-FULL: يخرج is jussive through the conditional sense implicit in the command; Abu Umar al-Jarmi instead makes the command itself govern it. BAYDAWI-FULL: Yukhrij means to bring forth or make exist; its jussive form answers the petition because prayer causes the response. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Yukh r ij is jussive as the response to the command udʿu, parallel to the response in the verse about fulfilling God’s covenant. SAMIN-DURR: يخرج is jussive as the response to the imperative. [6 notes: NAHHAS; WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNATIYYA-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:61/q13/p3 Jussive after implied command — ZAJJAJ (بعض النحويين): Some grammarians supply an implied request: ask God and say, “Bring it out for us,” and then He brings it out for them. QURTUBI-FULL: Yukh'rij is jussive as though the command says, 'Ask Him and say: bring forth'; al-Zajjāj weakens the alternative that supplies a deleted lām. [2 notes: ZAJJAJ(بعض النحويين); QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:61/q13/p4 Jussive after omitted command lām — QURTUBI-FULL: Yukh'rij is jussive as though the command says, 'Ask Him and say: bring forth'; al-Zajjāj weakens the alternative that supplies a deleted lām. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعضهم): Some say yukh r ij is jussive under an unexpressed lām of command; Basran grammarians do not allow this analysis. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعضهم)]
- 2:61/q13/p5 The verb itself is supplication — ZAJJAJ (قوم): Others treat “yukhriǧ lanā” as a supplication, equivalent to the direct request “Bring it out for us.” [1 notes: ZAJJAJ(قوم)]

## 2:61/q14 What is the object of 'bring forth,' and how is mā parsed?  [يُخْرِجْ لَنَا مِمَّا تُنۢبِتُ · grammar]
turns on: Whether an edible object is omitted or mā itself functions as the object or verbal noun.
- 2:61/q14/p1 An omitted concrete object — WAHIDI-BASIT: The object of yukhrij is omitted and understood as something from the earth's plants; the same kind of omitted object occurs in the verse about Abraham's descendants. TABRISI: Yukh’rij is jussive as the answer to an omitted command, understood as asking God and telling Him to bring forth the requested food; the implied conditional is indicated by context. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another grammatical analysis supplies an omitted command, ask Him to bring the foods forth, with yukh r ij responding to that command. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash takes the preposition as causal and attached to باء, with an omitted object: they deserved punishment because God was angry with them. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): Some grammarians posit an omitted command lām, understanding ليخرج; al-Zajjaj weakens that analysis. SAMIN-DURR (سيبويه): Sibawayh supplies an omitted object for يخرج: مأكولا or شيئا from what the earth grows. SAMIN-DURR: ما may be a relative noun or an indefinite noun with an adjective, with its resumptive omitted; it cannot be a verbal noun because the object produced is a concrete substance, not cultivation as an action, under either analysis. BURHAN: In “ask your Lord to bring forth for us,” the omitted object is “something.” BURSEVI: In “bring forth for us,” the object is omitted, and the verb is jussive as the response to the command to supplicate. ALUSI-FULL: The verb يخرج is jussive as the response to the imperative; Basran grammarians do not allow explaining its jussive with an omitted لام الطلب. ALUSI-FULL: ما is a relative pronoun with its object omitted; Abu al-Baqa rejects taking it as a verbal noun because the implied referent would be a substance. [11 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT; TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأخفش,قيل); SAMIN-DURR(بعضهم,سيبويه); BURHAN; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q14/p2 Mā itself serves as object — QURTUBI-FULL (النحاس): Al-Naḥḥās says al-Akhfash sought an object for yukh'rij by treating mā as that object. SAMIN-DURR: ما may be a relative noun or an indefinite noun with an adjective, with its resumptive omitted; it cannot be a verbal noun because the object produced is a concrete substance, not cultivation as an action, under either analysis. ALUSI-FULL: ما is a relative pronoun with its object omitted; Abu al-Baqa rejects taking it as a verbal noun because the implied referent would be a substance. [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النحاس); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q14/p3 Mā is a verbal noun — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعضهم): Some take mā as a verbal noun, meaning from the earth’s growing. SAMIN-DURR: ما may be a relative noun or an indefinite noun with an adjective, with its resumptive omitted; it cannot be a verbal noun because the object produced is a concrete substance, not cultivation as an action, under either analysis. ALUSI-FULL: ما is a relative pronoun with its object omitted; Abu al-Baqa rejects taking it as a verbal noun because the implied referent would be a substance. [3 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعضهم); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q14/p4 The object is inferred from the crop list — RAZI-FULL: An objection says God gave them an immediate worldly request after they rejected what He chose, as in 42:20; Razi answers that this departs from the apparent meaning. RAZI-FULL: Razi answers the Muʿtazilite objection by counterarguing with knowledge and motive; he says the nature of disbelief was discussed earlier. SAMIN-DURR (أبو البقاء): Abu al-Baqa explains the kasra as resolving two adjacent sukuns while treating the weak verb like a sound verb; the author objects that normally the first, not second, of two sukuns is vowelled. [3 notes: RAZI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(أبو البقاء)]

## 2:61/q15 What does the first min in mimmā do?  [مِمَّا تُنۢبِتُ ٱلْأَرْضُ · grammar]
turns on: Partitive or extra min in an affirmative clause, with or without an omitted object.
- 2:61/q15/p1 Partitive min — AKHFASH: The first من is partitive: the earth produces some unspecified amount of its plants, with the word “something” left unexpressed. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari reads min as partitive: the Israelites asked for some of what the earth produces, so the particle itself supplies the partitive sense. TABRISI: Min in mimmā is partitive because they sought some of what the earth produces, not everything; the alternative treats it as redundant, but min is added only in negation. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Min is a genitive preposition used for partitive meaning, beginning point, and explanation of a kind; context also licenses figurative uses. QURTUBI-FULL: Al-Qurṭubī prefers an omitted object inferred as edible food; the first min is partitive and the second specifies. BAYDAWI-FULL: The phrase about what the earth grows uses figurative attribution, putting the recipient in the agent's place, and min is partitive. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (سيبويه): Sībawayh takes min in مما تنبت الأرض as partitive, with an omitted object of yukh r ij meaning something edible from what the earth grows. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Both occurrences of من are partitive and attach to يخرج, either directly or through an estimated repeated verb; the latter analysis is standard. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: He prefers taking both particles as partitive; his school rejects the explanatory sense of من here and reinterprets its evidence. SAMIN-DURR: Alternatively, من may qualify the omitted object as something from what the earth grows, with من partitive. BURSEVI: The wording figuratively assigns growth to the receptive earth in place of God, the true agent; “min” is partitive and “mā” is relative. ALUSI-FULL: The first من is partitive: they ask for some of what the earth grows, specifically some edible produce. [12 notes: AKHFASH; TAB-FULL; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(سيبويه); SAMIN-DURR; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; QURTUBI-FULL+; TABRISI+]
- 2:61/q15/p2 Extra min — TAB-FULL (بعضهم): An alternative grammatical view takes min as redundant, yielding the sense of what the earth grows; its proponents cite Arabic usage and Qur'an 2:271. Against — NAHHAS (الأخفش): Akhfash calls من extra so ما can serve as the object of يخرج; Nahhas rejects this under Sibawayh's rule that من is not extra in affirmative clauses, supplying an omitted object: edible produce. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash regards من in مما as extra and ما as the object; Sibawayh rejects extra من outside negation, as in ما رأيت من أحد. TABRISI: Min in mimmā is partitive because they sought some of what the earth produces, not everything; the alternative treats it as redundant, but min is added only in negation. QURTUBI-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash regards min in mimmā as extra, while Sībawayh treats it as non-extra because the clause is affirmative. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash takes min as redundant and mā as a relative pronoun with an omitted object pronoun, a deletion that meets the required conditions. SAMIN-DURR (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash treats من as an extra marker in the object, yielding يخرج ما تنبته الأرض; he does not require a special condition for its being extra. ALUSI-FULL: Here “bring forth” is a figurative consequence of literal extraction: it means producing by creation, not merely revealing what was hidden. Against — ALUSI-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash treats the first من as extra, a parsing the author rejects. [7 notes: TAB-FULL(بعضهم); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الأخفش); QURTUBI-FULL(الأخفش); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأخفش); SAMIN-DURR(الأخفش); ALUSI-FULL; TABRISI+; NAHHAS(الأخفش)-; ALUSI-FULL(الأخفش)-]
- 2:61/q15/p3 The first min has a disputed role — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Sibawayh supplies an omitted object for يخرج, estimated as مأكولا مما تنبت الأرض. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]

## 2:61/q16 How is growth attributed to the earth?  [تُنۢبِتُ ٱلْأَرْضُ · rhetoric]
- 2:61/q16/p1 Growth is figuratively attributed to earth while God causes it — THALABI: The requested items are the plants of the earth. TABRISI: Inbāt means bringing forth plants and derives from the sense of appearing. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The hamza in inbāt is causative: the word means bringing out something whose nature is to grow. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو البقاء): Abū al-Baqāʾ rejects the verbal-noun analysis because an omitted concrete object cannot be described by a verbal noun; saying the earth grows is figurative, since God causes growth and the earth only receives it. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali glosses the earth's producing as nourishment and growth. ALUSI-FULL: The literal reading would require the earth to be an extractor and make the statement nonsensical. ALUSI-FULL: Attributing growth to the earth is figurative, treating the receptacle as the agent; God placed the receptive capacity in the clay layer of the earth. ALUSI-FULL: Locating the receptive power in the seed rather than the soil could lead to the claim that seeds have existed eternally as a kind. [8 notes: THALABI; TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو البقاء); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q17 How is مِنۢ بَقْلِهَا related to the preceding phrase?  [مِنۢ بَقْلِهَا · grammar]
turns on: Partitive, explanatory, or circumstantial min and the limits of apposition.
- 2:61/q17/p1 Partitive min in apposition — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The appositive analysis fails if the first من is partitive but the second is explanatory, since the two particles then differ in meaning. ALUSI-FULL (أبو حيان): Abu Hayyan instead takes the second من as partitive, in apposition to ما, with the phrase attached directly to يخرج. ALUSI-FULL (الساليكوتي): Al-Salikuti says both parsings imply that they sought only some of the listed items; treating the second من as explanatory alongside the first partitive would lose that meaning and suggest all items. [3 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(أبو حيان,الساليكوتي)]
- 2:61/q17/p2 Explanatory min — Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The appositive analysis fails if the first من is partitive but the second is explanatory, since the two particles then differ in meaning. ALUSI-FULL (المولى عصام الدين): Mawla ʿIsam al-Din's explanatory parsing would leave the wording implying that all listed plants were requested, since there is no prior specification. ALUSI-FULL: The second من may be explanatory, with the phrase functioning as a circumstantial expression meaning “being among the greens.” [2 notes: ALUSI-FULL(المولى عصام الدين); ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:61/q17/p3 A circumstantial phrase — BAYDAWI-FULL: The listed crops explain the preceding phrase as a circumstantial clarification; another analysis treats them as an appositive with the preposition repeated. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:61/q17/p4 An appositive with repeated preposition — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: الذي is singular because it points back to the food and to ما in مما تنبت; agreement with the appositive would instead require a feminine plural. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]

## 2:61/q18 What does baql mean?  [بَقْلِهَا · meaning]
- 2:61/q18/p1 Fresh plants and edible greens — IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): When they reached Syria and missed their former foods, they asked Moses to seek vegetables from the earth for them. NAHHAS: من بقلها is appositional with a repeated preposition; وقثائها is coordinated with it. WAHIDI-BASIT: Baql is any plant that does not retain a stalk after livestock graze it. KASHSHAF-FULL: Alternatively, the two foods were one kind because both belonged to luxurious eating, while the farming people wanted their familiar varied grains and vegetables. TABRISI: Baql is spring growth and, more specifically, any plant without a stem; both baqalat and abqalat are eloquent forms for the earth's producing it. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Baql is a collective noun; they meant vegetables. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو منصور اللغوي): Against a popular narrow usage, baql means herbage and spring growth eaten by both people and animals. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو النجم): Abu al-Najm’s poem uses tabaqqalat for camels grazing at the first sprouting of vegetation. BAYDAWI-FULL: Baql means plants grown by the earth; here it refers to the choicest edible greens. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن دريد): Baql is a genus covering fresh growth eaten by people and animals; the land verbs mean that the land has become vegetated, and Ibn Durayd connects al-bāqillāʾ to it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The phrase من بقلها is an appositive to مما تنبت الأرض with the preposition repeated, a fluent construction. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو البقاء): Abu al-Baqa allows من بقلها to explain the kind and treats it as an accusative circumstantial phrase from an omitted pronoun. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (المهدوي وابن عطية): Al-Mahdawi and Ibn Atiyya call من بقلها an appositive even though they take the two prepositions to have different senses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): The greens mean choice vegetables people eat, such as mint, celery, and leek. SAMIN-DURR: من بقلها may be an appositive to ما with the preposition repeated, where من explains the kind. SAMIN-DURR: بقل is all plant growth without a stem, and its plural is بقول. IBNKATHIR-FULL (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masud reports that Israelites killed three hundred prophets in one day, then sold vegetables in the market at day's end. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says baql is the growth of the earth that supplements fatty foods; the author first identifies it as greens. DURR-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud says Israelites killed three hundred prophets in a day and sold vegetables in the market by evening. BURSEVI: The “min” in “from its greens” is explanatory and functions circumstantially with the pronoun. ALUSI-FULL: Baql is a generic term for fresh plants eaten by people or animals; here it means edible herbs and greens. IBNASHUR-FULL: إخراج means bringing forth from the earth; من is partitive, explanatory, or partitive twice, and من بقلها may be circumstantial or an apposition to ما تنبت. [22 notes: IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); NAHHAS; WAHIDI-BASIT; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو النجم,أبو منصور اللغوي); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو البقاء,ابن دريد,الزمخشري,المهدوي وابن عطية); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(ابن مسعود); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q19 How is qiththāʾ pronounced?  [وَقِثَّآئِهَا · readings]
turns on: Hijazi kasra and Tamim ḍamma dialects and their transmitters.
- 2:61/q19/p1 Ḍamma on qāf — ZAJJAJ: Al-qithāʾ has two dialectal forms; some readers pronounce its possessive form with ḍamma, while the more common and better reading has kasra. NAHHAS (طلحة ويحيى بن وثاب): They read وقثائها with ḍamma on qāf. THALABI (يحيى بن وثاب، وطلحة بن مصرف، والأشيب العقيلي): These readers pronounce qiththāʾihā with ḍamma on qāf, a Tamim dialect. JISHUMI (قراءة العامة): The common reading has qiththā with kasra on qāf; Yaḥyā ibn Waththāb uses ḍamma, a Tamīm dialect. KASHSHAF-FULL: A variant reading vocalizes the word for cucumbers with a damma. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طلحة بن مصرف ويحيى بن وثاب): Talha ibn Musarrif and Yahya ibn Waththab read قثائها with a damma on the qaf. TABRISI (عيسى الثقفي): Isa al-Thaqafi is reported to have the anomalous reading with damma on qaf in qiththa. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Qiththāʾ has both kasra and damma on its first consonant; the kasra is better and is the majority reading. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن مسعود، أبو رجاء، قتادة، طلحة بن مصرف، الأعمش): These readers pronounce qiththāʾ with damma on the qāf. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء): The kasra pronunciation is Hijazi; the damma pronunciation belongs to Tamim and some of Banu Asad. RAZI-FULL: The usual reading has qithāʾihā with qaf kasra; al-Aʿmash and Talha read it with qaf ḍamma. QURTUBI-FULL: Baql is any plant without a stem, while a tree has one; qiththāʾ is cucumber, and Yaḥyā b. Waththāb and Ṭalḥah read its qāf with ḍamma, a less common variant. BAYDAWI-FULL: A reading pronounces qiththā with ḍamma, a dialectal form. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Qithāʾ is a generic noun whose singular is qithāʾa, with qāf either ḍamma or kasra; maqthaʾa means land rich in it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (يحيى بن وثاب وطلحة بن مصرف): They read وقثائها with a ḍamma on qāf, which is an established dialect form. SAMIN-DURR: المقثأة and المقثؤة, with fatḥa or ḍamma on tāʾ, name a place where cucumbers grow. SAMIN-DURR (قرئ): The usual pronunciation of قثاء has kasra on qāf, and another reading uses ḍamma. ALUSI-FULL (يحيى بن وثاب وغيره): Yahya ibn Waththab and others read قثاء with damma on qaf, a dialect form. [18 notes: ZAJJAJ; NAHHAS(طلحة ويحيى بن وثاب); THALABI(يحيى بن وثاب، وطلحة بن مصرف، والأشيب العقيلي); JISHUMI(قراءة العامة); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(طلحة بن مصرف ويحيى بن وثاب); TABRISI(عيسى الثقفي); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن مسعود، أبو رجاء، قتادة، طلحة بن مصرف، الأعمش,الفراء); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(يحيى بن وثاب وطلحة بن مصرف); SAMIN-DURR(قرئ); ALUSI-FULL(يحيى بن وثاب وغيره); IBNJAWZI-ZAD+]
- 2:61/q19/p2 Kasra on qāf — ZAJJAJ: Al-qithāʾ has two dialectal forms; some readers pronounce its possessive form with ḍamma, while the more common and better reading has kasra. THALABI: The common reading has qiththāʾihā with kasra on qāf. JISHUMI (قراءة العامة): The common reading has qiththā with kasra on qāf; Yaḥyā ibn Waththāb uses ḍamma, a Tamīm dialect. TABRISI: Qiththa has two pronunciations, with kasra on qaf preferred and identified as Quranic usage. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Qiththāʾ has both kasra and damma on its first consonant; the kasra is better and is the majority reading. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء): The kasra pronunciation is Hijazi; the damma pronunciation belongs to Tamim and some of Banu Asad. RAZI-FULL: The usual reading has qithāʾihā with qaf kasra; al-Aʿmash and Talha read it with qaf ḍamma. QURTUBI-FULL: Baql is any plant without a stem, while a tree has one; qiththāʾ is cucumber, and Yaḥyā b. Waththāb and Ṭalḥah read its qāf with ḍamma, a less common variant. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Qithāʾ is a generic noun whose singular is qithāʾa, with qāf either ḍamma or kasra; maqthaʾa means land rich in it. SAMIN-DURR (قرئ): The usual pronunciation of قثاء has kasra on qāf, and another reading uses ḍamma. [10 notes: ZAJJAJ; THALABI; JISHUMI(قراءة العامة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(قرئ); IBNJAWZI-ZAD+; TABRISI+]

## 2:61/q20 What does qiththāʾ mean and how is it formed?  [وَقِثَّآئِهَا · meaning]
turns on: Lexical sense and the dispute over an original hamza or a wāw root.
- 2:61/q20/p1 Cucumber — NAHHAS: The plural of قثاء is قثائي, like علباء/علابي; قثاء belongs to hamza-root words, as shown by أقثأت القوم. JISHUMI: Baql means any plant without a stalk, and qiththā is a kind of cucumber. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The من in من بقلها marks kind, and بقلها is an apposition repeating the preposition; baql includes all plants the earth grows, and qithāʾ is plural of qithʾa. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA (بعض المفسرين): Min explains the kind meant: the Israelites ask for vegetables and cucumbers among what the earth grows. QURTUBI-FULL: Qithāʾ can form qithāʾī, is a wāw-root, and aqthaʾa can mean to feed people it. QURTUBI-FULL: Min bāqlihā is an appositive for mā with the preposition repeated; qiththāʾ and the remaining foods are coordinated with it. QURTUBI-FULL: The verse asks whether they exchange inferior herbs, cucumbers, grain, lentils, and onions for the better manna and quail. NASAFI-FULL: Qiththa means cucumber. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الخليل): Al-Khalīl identifies qithāʾ as cucumber. SAMIN-DURR: قثاء is the familiar cucumber; its singular is قثاءة, following the قمح/قمحة pattern. SAMIN-DURR: The hamza in قثاء is original, as in أقثأت الأرض when cucumbers become abundant; its pattern is فعال. SAMIN-DURR: قثاء may have the plural قثائي, like علباء and علابي. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): Some derive قثاء from a waw-root and cite أقثأت القوم and فثأت القدر; the author calls this a gross error because the expected forms would instead preserve the waw-derived pattern, with yāʾ substitution in the four-letter verb. BURSEVI: Qiththāʾ is related to qathd and resembles cucumber. BURSEVI: Baql denotes edible greens such as mint, celery, and leek; qiththāʾ is a cucumber-like plant. ALUSI-FULL: The familiar qiththa is identified as cucumber; al-Khalil also says it means cucumber. [16 notes: NAHHAS; JISHUMI; IBNATIYYA-FULL; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الخليل); SAMIN-DURR(بعضهم); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q20/p2 Original hamza in the root — NAHHAS: The plural of قثاء is قثائي, like علباء/علابي; قثاء belongs to hamza-root words, as shown by أقثأت القوم. SAMIN-DURR: The hamza in قثاء is original, as in أقثأت الأرض when cucumbers become abundant; its pattern is فعال. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): Some derive قثاء from a waw-root and cite أقثأت القوم and فثأت القدر; the author calls this a gross error because the expected forms would instead preserve the waw-derived pattern, with yāʾ substitution in the four-letter verb. [3 notes: NAHHAS; SAMIN-DURR(بعضهم)]
- 2:61/q20/p3 A wāw-root derivation — QURTUBI-FULL: Qithāʾ can form qithāʾī, is a wāw-root, and aqthaʾa can mean to feed people it.
