SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): Some derive قثاء from a waw-root and cite أقثأت القوم and فثأت القدر; the author calls this a gross error because the expected forms would instead preserve the waw-derived pattern, with yāʾ substitution in the four-letter verb. Against — SAMIN-DURR: The author rejects that waw-root argument: it should produce أقثيت from أقثوت and فثوت القدر, and the poem should say نفثوها, not نفثؤها. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(بعضهم); SAMIN-DURR-]
- 2:61/q20/p4 A related lexical or morphological form — QURTUBI-FULL (الجعدي): Al-Jaʿdī's poetry uses nafthāʾ for calming a boiling pot by adding water, illustrating the root's sense of calming. QURTUBI-FULL: The f-th-ʾ root also means calming anger, tiring from running, or heat subsiding; the proverb says sour curdled milk calms anger. QURTUBI-FULL: Rathīʾah is milk curdled by milking it onto sour milk; irtathaʾa means the milk thickened. [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الجعدي)]

## 2:61/q21 What report is cited about eating cucumber?  [وَقِثَّآئِهَا · reports]
- 2:61/q21/p1 Cucumber with dates aided weight gain — QURTUBI-FULL (عائشة): ʿĀʾishah says her mother succeeded in fattening her for introduction to the Prophet only after feeding her cucumber with fresh dates; al-Qurṭubī grades the chain sound. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عائشة)]

## 2:61/q22 What does fūm mean here?  [وَفُومِهَا · meaning]
turns on: Arabic usage and poetry, the thūm reading, consonant interchange, and fit with neighboring foods.
- 2:61/q22/p1 Wheat or ears of wheat — IBNHISHAM (ابن هشام): Ibn Hishām glosses fūm as wheat and cites Umayya ibn Abī al-Ṣalt’s poem as evidence. IBNHISHAM (ابن هشام): In the cited poem, al-wudhayl means pieces of silver; fūm means wheat, whose singular is fūma. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Fūm is said to mean wheat and bread. TAB-FULL (بعضهم): Some commentators say fūm means wheat and bread. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says fūm means wheat in the language of Banu Hashim. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also identifies fūm as wheat and cites Aḥīḥa ibn al-Jallāḥ's poem mentioning fūm cultivation. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari says wheat and bread were called fūm in an older Arabic usage, quoting the expression fūmū lanā, meaning bake for us. TAB-FULL (أبو مالك): Abu Malik identifies fūm as wheat. TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi identifies fūm as wheat. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says fūm means wheat and bread. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): A report from Ibn Abbas through al-Dahhak identifies fūm specifically as wheat. ZAJJAJ: “Fūm” means wheat, and more broadly baked grains; the proposed meaning “garlic” is unknown in Arabic and ill-suited to a request for staple food, since wheat is food’s foundation. “Fūmū lanā” means bake bread for us. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses fūm as bread, and on another occasion as wheat. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains fūm as wheat and cites Uhayha ibn al-Halah's verse as lexical evidence for the word. IBNABIHATIM: The author says Mujahid, al-Hasan, Abu Malik, Ikrima, Ata, al-Suddi, and Qatada likewise interpret fūm as wheat, while others differ. THALABI (عطاء وأبو مالك): Fūm means wheat in an old language; the cited poem uses fūm in the context of farming. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Fūm means wheat, according to Ibn Abbas, Qatada, and al-Suddi; Ibn Abbas cites Uhayha ibn al-Jallah's poem as evidence for the word. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra says fawm is an old word for wheat and bread; some Arabs also say fummu lana to mean bake bread for us, as in a verse by Ahihah ibn al-Jallah about sowing fawm. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says fawm means wheat, or grain generally, and rejects garlic as unknown and implausible because the people sought staple food containing grain. WAHIDI-BASIT (اللحياني): Al-Lihyani says fum and thum both refer to wheat. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن دريد، المبرد): Ibn Durayd says the Azd of al-Sarat call the wheat ear fawm; al-Mubarrad chooses this explanation. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: The one food was manna and quail together; baql means plants without lasting stems, qiththa a kind of vegetable, and fūm wheat. JISHUMI (الحسن وقتادة وعطاء ومجاهد): Al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, ʿAṭāʾ, and Mujāhid interpret fūm as wheat; al-Mubarrad also prefers this meaning. BAGHAWI-FULL: One explanation makes wheat lower in value than manna and quail even though it is better; another says it is lower because it is ordinarily easier to obtain. BAGHAWI-FULL (عطاء): ʿAta says fūm means wheat. KASHSHAF-FULL: Fūm means wheat, supported by the idiom 'bring us wheat' meaning bake bread. IBNATIYYA-FULL (عطاء وقتادة): Ata and Qatada define fūm as grains suitable for baking, including wheat, fava beans, and lentils. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says fūm means garlic, supported by Ubayy ibn Kaʿb's reading with thāʾ and a report from Ibn Abbas; Ibn Atiyya prefers wheat because consonant substitution is not analogically productive. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Atiyya prefers the wheat interpretation; Ibn Abbas's poetic citation supports it by using fūm for wheat. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas cites Uhayha ibn al-Jallah's poem as evidence that fūm means wheat. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن دريد): Ibn Durayd says fūm means either crops or wheat, and the Azd of al-Sarat call ears of grain fūm. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس وأكثر المفسرين): Ibn Abbas and most exegetes say fūm means wheat. TABRISI: The cited verse depicts fūm as a crop cultivated in Medina. TABRISI (الفراء والأزهري): Al-Farra and al-Azhari say fūm means wheat or bread; Arabs use it to mean bake bread. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس، السدي عن أشياخه، الحسن، أبو مالك): One interpretation of fūm is wheat. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): One report from Ibn ʿAbbas interprets fum as wheat. RAZI-FULL: Supporters of the garlic interpretation cite the Ibn Masʿud reading thumihā, argue that wheat could not be inferior to manna and quail, and note garlic's fit with lentils and onions. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās and most exegetes identify fūm as wheat; al-Naḥḥās prefers this view because its support is stronger and its chains sound. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās answered a questioner who said fūm means wheat by citing a line of Uḥayḥah b. al-Jallāḥ about wheat cultivation. QURTUBI-FULL (الجوهري): Al-Jawharī defines fūm as wheat and al-Akhfash cites a supporting wheat-cultivation poem. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن دريد): Ibn Durayd says fūmah means an ear of wheat and cites a line describing ears in a scout's hand. BAYDAWI-FULL: Fūm means wheat, and can also mean bread, as in the phrase ‘bring us bread’; another view takes it to mean garlic. NASAFI-FULL (ابن مسعود): Fum means either wheat or garlic; Ibn Masud's reading has thum, garlic. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو مالك وجماعة): Abū Mālik and a group identify fūm as wheat; the cited Uḥayḥa ibn al-Jallāḥ poem speaks of cultivating it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (المبرد): The wheat meaning is identified as Egyptian usage and is al-Mubarrad’s preferred interpretation; al-Farrāʾ calls it an ancient dialect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو عبيدة، وابن دريد): Abū ʿUbayda and Ibn Durayd define fūm as an ear of grain; Abū ʿUbayda specifies that this is the Asad dialect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس والحسن وقتادة والسدي): They interpret فومها as wheat. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): A final interpretation takes فومها to mean a grain ear or spike. SAMIN-DURR (ابن عباس): A second interpretation of الفوم is wheat, supported by a verse of poetry Ibn Abbas recited about farming فوم. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas also explains fūm as wheat in the dialect of Banu Hashim. IBNKATHIR-FULL (آخرون): Another group interprets fūm as wheat, the grain from which bread is made. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says fūm means wheat and cites the poet Ahiha ibn al-Jallah, whose use of the word fūm supports wheat as a cultivated grain. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ali ibn Abi Talha, al-Dahhak, and Ikrima transmit from Ibn Abbas that fūm means wheat. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو مالك): Hasan and Abu Malik are reported to interpret fūm as wheat. IBNKATHIR-FULL (عكرمة): Ikrima, al-Suddi, al-Hasan al-Basri, Qatada, and Abd al-Rahman ibn Zayd ibn Aslam also take fūm to mean wheat. BIQAI-FULL: The author identifies fūm as wheat, while Al-Harali explains it as grain used for baking bread. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): In another exchange, Ibn Abbas says fum means wheat; he tells Nafi that the Ibn Masud reading means the foul thing and cites poems by Abu Mihjan and Umayya b. Abi al-Salt as evidence. DURR-FULL (أبو محجن الثقفي): Abu Mihjan al-Thaqafi's line is cited as evidence for Arabic use of fum for wheat. DURR-FULL (أمية بن أبي الصلت): A second line by Umayya b. Abi al-Salt uses fum for healthy wheat against the image of rain and hail on the ground. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses fum as bread, with another wording identifying it as wheat in the Banu Hashim dialect. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): In another report to Nafi b. al-Azraq, Ibn Abbas says fum means wheat and cites a line by Ahihah b. al-Jallah as Arabic evidence. DURR-FULL (أحيحة بن الجلاح): Ahihah b. al-Jallah's verse is cited to establish Arabic use of fum for wheat. DURR-FULL (الحسن وأبو مالك): A separate wording from Hasan and Abu Malik takes fum to mean wheat. ITQAN: فومها means wheat. ITQAN: Fum means wheat; Abu Mihjan al-Thaqafi’s poem uses the word in a line about farming wheat as evidence for its Arabic usage. BURSEVI: Fūm means wheat here because lentils belong to the same food category. BURSEVI (ابن التمجيد): Ibn al-Tamjīd prefers reading fūm as garlic over wheat because it is mentioned with onion and lentils, which are cooked with garlic and onion. BURSEVI (بعضهم): Some say wheat is inferior here only in comparative value; the verse does not prove they wanted to replace manna and quail altogether, since they may have wanted alternation. ALUSI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says the language authorities agree that الفوم means wheat and other grains used for baking. IBNASHUR-FULL: The cited poem mentions fields of fūm, which the adjacent parenthetical gloss identifies as wheat. IBNASHUR-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj cites a poem by Uḥayḥa b. al-Jallāḥ as evidence that fūm means wheat. BINTSHATI-IJAZ (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses fūm as wheat; Abu Mihjan's line about cultivating fūm is cited as Arabic evidence for that sense. BINTSHATI-IJAZ (الفراء): Al-Farra says fūm in an old dialect can mean both wheat and bread; Arabs also used fawwimū lanā to mean bake for us. BINTSHATI-IJAZ (النحاس): Ibn ʿAbbās and most exegetes read al-fūm as wheat; al-Naḥḥās prefers this interpretation as more apt. STUDYQURAN: The word fūm, rendered garlic, may instead mean wheat. [75 notes: IBNHISHAM(ابن هشام); IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(أبو مالك,ابن عباس,السدي,بعضهم); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); THALABI(عطاء وأبو مالك); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن دريد، المبرد,الزجاج,الفراء,اللحياني); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(الحسن وقتادة وعطاء ومجاهد); BAGHAWI-FULL(عطاء); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن دريد,ابن عباس,ابن عباس وأكثر المفسرين,الضحاك,عطاء وقتادة); TABRISI(الفراء والأزهري); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس، السدي عن أشياخه، الحسن، أبو مالك); RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن دريد,ابن عباس,الجوهري); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL(ابن مسعود); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عبيدة، وابن دريد,أبو مالك وجماعة,ابن عباس والحسن وقتادة والسدي,المبرد,قيل); SAMIN-DURR(ابن عباس); IBNKATHIR-FULL(آخرون,أبو مالك,ابن عباس,عكرمة); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(أبو محجن الثقفي,أحيحة بن الجلاح,أمية بن أبي الصلت,ابن عباس,الحسن وأبو مالك); ITQAN; BURSEVI(بعضهم); ALUSI-FULL(الزجاج); IBNASHUR-FULL(الزجاج); BINTSHATI-IJAZ(ابن عباس,الفراء); STUDYQURAN; BINTSHATI-IJAZ(النحاس)+; BURSEVI(ابن التمجيد)+; IBNATIYYA-FULL+; ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q22/p2 Garlic — MUQATIL: After tiring of manna and quails, the Israelites asked Moses for earth's produce; “fūm” means garlic. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Ibn Qutayba prefers garlic, supported by the reading thūmihā in the codex of ʿAbd Allāh. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: The garlic reading is linguistically supported by Arabic interchange of thāʾ and fāʾ, as in jadath/jadaf and mughāthīr/mughāfīr. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says fūm means garlic. TAB-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabiʿ says fūm is garlic and notes that one reading has thūm in its place. TAB-FULL (عبد الله بن مسعود): Al-Tabari records Ibn Masʿud's reading thūmuhā, with thāʾ, instead of fūmuhā. Against — ZAJJAJ: “Fūm” means wheat, and more broadly baked grains; the proposed meaning “garlic” is unknown in Arabic and ill-suited to a request for staple food, since wheat is food’s foundation. “Fūmū lanā” means bake bread for us. IBNABIHATIM: The author says Sa'id ibn Jubayr, al-Rabi', and al-Dahhak also gave the garlic interpretation. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): A report through al-Hasan attributes to Ibn Abbas the alternative reading of fūm as garlic. THALABI (الكلبي والنضر بن شميل والكسائي والمعرج): These authorities take fūm to mean garlic; al-Muʿarrij cites a line by Ḥassān in which fūm is paired with ḥawqal as food. THALABI: On the garlic interpretation, Arabs interchange fāʾ and thāʾ, as in alternate words for gum and a grave; ʿAbd Allāh’s codex reads “and its garlic.” MAWARDI-FULL (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi ibn Anas and al-Kisaʾi take fūm as thūm, garlic, as explicitly reflected in Ibn Masud's reading with thāʾ. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra prefers reading fawm as thum, garlic: Abdullah's reading with thā fits the neighboring lentils and onions, supported by dialectal interchange of fā' and thā. WAHIDI-BASIT (الكسائي): Al-Kisa'i also chooses the interpretation of fawm as garlic. Against — WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says fawm means wheat, or grain generally, and rejects garlic as unknown and implausible because the people sought staple food containing grain. WAHIDI-BASIT (اللحياني): Al-Lihyani says fum and thum both refer to wheat. JISHUMI (الكلبي والنضر بن شميل): Al-Kalbī and al-Naḍr ibn Shumayl interpret fūm as garlic and explain the reading through interchange of fāʾ and thāʾ. JISHUMI (الكسائي وأبو عبيدة): Al-Kisāʾī and Abū ʿUbayda cite the interchangeable forms jadath and jadaf; ʿAbd Allāh’s codex has thūm, “garlic.” KASHSHAF-FULL: Another view takes fūm to mean garlic; Ibn Masud's reading 'and its garlic' supports it and better pairs it with lentils and onions. Against — IBNATIYYA-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says fūm means garlic, supported by Ubayy ibn Kaʿb's reading with thāʾ and a report from Ibn Abbas; Ibn Atiyya prefers wheat because consonant substitution is not analogically productive. TABRISI (الكسائي): Al-Kisaʾi says fūm means garlic, with fāʾ replacing thāʾ as in the words hadath and jadaf. TABRISI (الفراء): Al-Farra considers the garlic reading more fitting because onion follows fūm in the verse. TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj rejects garlic as an implausible meaning because people would not ask for garlic without bread, the staple. TABRISI: Ṭabrisī calls that objection weak because anomalous readings attributed to Ibn Masud and Ibn Abbas read thūm, garlic, with thāʾ. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء، مجاهد، الربيع بن أنس، مقاتل، الكسائي، النضر بن شميل، ابن قتيبة): A second view identifies fūm as garlic, supported by the variant thūmuhā and its pairing with similar foods. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: The garlic reading is explained by the Arabic interchange of fāʾ and thāʾ, as in several paired word forms. RAZI-FULL: The usual reading is fumihā with fa; a reading attributed to Ibn Masʿud and Ibn ʿAbbas has thumihā, which some consider more fitting beside onions. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Another view, also transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbas and Mujahid, interprets fum as garlic; al-Kisaʾi selects this view. RAZI-FULL: Supporters of the garlic interpretation cite the Ibn Masʿud reading thumihā, argue that wheat could not be inferior to manna and quail, and note garlic's fit with lentils and onions. QURTUBI-FULL (جويبر عن الضحاك): Juwaybir reports from al-Ḍaḥḥāk that fūm means garlic because it pairs with onion; this view invokes interchange of fāʾ and thāʾ. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿūd reads thūmihā with thāʾ; the same reading is also related from Ibn ʿAbbās. QURTUBI-FULL (أمية بن أبي الصلت): Umayyah b. Abī al-Ṣalt's line listing gardens and al-fūm supports interpreting fūm as garlic. QURTUBI-FULL (حسان): Ḥassān pairs fūm with onion as food, supporting the garlic view adopted by al-Kisāʾī and al-Naḍr b. Shumayl. QURTUBI-FULL (النحاس): Al-Naḥḥās rejects the garlic view despite al-Kisāʾī and al-Farrāʾ: interchange cannot be extended by analogy and is uncommon in Arabic. BAYDAWI-FULL: Fūm means wheat, and can also mean bread, as in the phrase ‘bring us bread’; another view takes it to mean garlic. NASAFI-FULL (ابن مسعود): Fum means either wheat or garlic; Ibn Masud's reading has thum, garlic. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Al-Ṣalt’s verse groups fūm with onion among the people’s provisions, supporting the reported garlic interpretation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الصلت): Al-Ṣalt’s verse cites fūm and onion among the people’s provisions, supporting the garlic reading. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: One interpretation takes فومها as garlic; Ibn Masud's ثومها reading supports it and fits the neighboring foods. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The listed foods span hot, cold, moist, and dry qualities: greens vary, cucumber is cold and moist, garlic hot and dry, lentils cold and dry, and onion hot and moist unless cooked. SAMIN-DURR: الفوم means garlic; fāʾ may irregularly replace thāʾ, as in جدف/جدث, عاثور/عافور, and معاثير/معافير. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud's reading has thūm, with thāʾ, where the standard text has fūm. IBNKATHIR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid, in a report through Layth ibn Abi Sulaym, interprets fūm as garlic; al-Rabi ibn Anas and Saʿid ibn Jubayr agree. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas is reported by al-Hasan as interpreting fūm as garlic. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): If the baking gloss is correct, Ibn Jarir says fūm is a letter substitution: fāʾ and thāʾ may exchange because their articulation points are close. DURR-FULL (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi b. Anas says fum is garlic and notes a reading with thum instead. DURR-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud recited the word as thum rather than fum. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Another Ibn Abbas report interprets fum as garlic. BURSEVI (قيل): Another lexical view reads fūm as garlic because garlic and onion are paired as related foods. BURSEVI (ابن التمجيد): Ibn al-Tamjīd prefers reading fūm as garlic over wheat because it is mentioned with onion and lentils, which are cooked with garlic and onion. ALUSI-FULL (الكسائي وجماعة): Al-Kisaʾi and a group identify الفوم as garlic; the text explains the reading through substitution of ثاء for فاء. ALUSI-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud's reading as garlic fits its pairing with onion and lentils, and the author's teacher inclines to that reading. ALUSI-FULL: One rationale for the sequence is its balance of hot, cold, moist, and dry qualities: greens encompass varieties, followed by cucumber, garlic, lentils, and onion. ELMALILI: Israelites say they cannot endure eating one kind of food and ask Moses to pray for produce from the earth, including vegetables, cucumber, garlic, lentils, and onions. ELMALILI: The wording Misr instead of qarya or balad hints that Israel preferred a humiliating condition resembling Egypt for the sake of eating onions and garlic. ELMALILI: The commentary renders qiththā as cucumber and fūm as garlic in the requested list. IBNASHUR-FULL: Fūm most likely means garlic: exchanging ث for ف is common in Arabic, and garlic fits the accompanying produce and the Torah’s list. BINTSHATI-IJAZ: The reading attributed to Abdullah, thūmihā with thāʾ, means garlic and better fits lentils and onions; Arabic speakers can interchange fāʾ and thāʾ. BINTSHATI-IJAZ (الكسائي): One reported reading takes al-fūm to mean garlic; al-Kisāʾī and al-Naḍr ibn Shumayl are named for this view, and al-Farrāʾ also chooses it by analogy with fāʾ replacing thāʾ. Against — BINTSHATI-IJAZ: Bint al-Shāṭiʾ rejects letter substitution as a general proof for garlic, since replacing thāʾ with fāʾ is not a rule that can be analogically extended. STUDYQURAN: The word fūm, rendered garlic, may instead mean wheat. [57 notes: MUQATIL; IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(الربيع,عبد الله بن مسعود,مجاهد); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); THALABI(الكلبي والنضر بن شميل والكسائي والمعرج); MAWARDI-FULL(الربيع بن أنس); WAHIDI-BASIT(اللحياني); JISHUMI(الكسائي وأبو عبيدة,الكلبي والنضر بن شميل); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(الزجاج,الفراء,الكسائي); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء، مجاهد، الربيع بن أنس، مقاتل، الكسائي، النضر بن شميل، ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL(أمية بن أبي الصلت,ابن مسعود,جويبر عن الضحاك,حسان); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL(ابن مسعود); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الصلت); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير,ابن عباس,ابن مسعود,مجاهد); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,ابن مسعود,الربيع بن أنس); BURSEVI(قيل); ALUSI-FULL(الكسائي وجماعة); ELMALILI; BINTSHATI-IJAZ(الكسائي); STUDYQURAN; ALUSI-FULL(ابن مسعود)+; BINTSHATI-IJAZ+; BURSEVI(ابن التمجيد)+; IBNASHUR-FULL+; IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB+; QURTUBI-FULL(النحاس)+; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء,الكسائي)+; ZAJJAJ-; WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج)-; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الضحاك)-; BINTSHATI-IJAZ-]
- 2:61/q22/p3 Bread or bread-making grain — MUJAHID (مجاهد): The word fūm refers to bread. ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة والحسن): They gloss fūm as bread. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Fūm is said to mean wheat and bread. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ says fūm is an old dialect word in the command fūmū, meaning bake bread. TAB-FULL (بعضهم): Some commentators say fūm means wheat and bread. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari says wheat and bread were called fūm in an older Arabic usage, quoting the expression fūmū lanā, meaning bake for us. TAB-FULL (عطاء ومجاهد): ʿAṭāʾ and Mujahid interpret fūm as bread. TAB-FULL (قتادة والحسن): Qatada and al-Hasan say fūm is the grain from which people make bread. TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd identifies fūm as bread. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says fūm means wheat and bread. ZAJJAJ: “Fūm” means wheat, and more broadly baked grains; the proposed meaning “garlic” is unknown in Arabic and ill-suited to a request for staple food, since wheat is food’s foundation. “Fūmū lanā” means bake bread for us. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses fūm as bread, and on another occasion as wheat. THALABI (ابن عباس): Fūm means bread; the Arabs say fūmū lanā to mean “bake for us.” MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid, Ibn Zayd, and ʿAtaʾ say fūm means bread. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra says fawm is an old word for wheat and bread; some Arabs also say fummu lana to mean bake bread for us, as in a verse by Ahihah ibn al-Jallah about sowing fawm. JISHUMI (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās interprets fūm as bread. BAGHAWI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas says fūm means bread. KASHSHAF-FULL: Fūm means wheat, supported by the idiom 'bring us wheat' meaning bake bread. IBNATIYYA-FULL (عطاء وقتادة): Ata and Qatada define fūm as grains suitable for baking, including wheat, fava beans, and lentils. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says fūm means bread. TABRISI (الفراء والأزهري): Al-Farra and al-Azhari say fūm means wheat or bread; Arabs use it to mean bake bread. TABRISI (قوم): Some identify fūm as grains from which bread is baked. TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj rejects garlic as an implausible meaning because people would not ask for garlic without bread, the staple. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ says fūm is an old dialect in the request fūmū lanā, meaning bake bread for us. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Another report from Ibn ʿAbbas interprets fum as bread; this is also transmitted from Mujahid, ʿAta, and Ibn Zayd, and Arabic usage includes fumū lanā meaning bake for us. QURTUBI-FULL (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ calls fūmū lanā an old dialect for 'bake for us'; ʿAṭāʾ and Qatādah define fūm as any grain made into bread. QURTUBI-FULL: Other dialectal explanations make fūm chickpeas in Syrian speech; fāmī is an altered nisbah of fūmī, and fūmū lanā means bake for us. BAYDAWI-FULL: Fūm means wheat, and can also mean bread, as in the phrase ‘bring us bread’; another view takes it to mean garlic. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba chooses the bread interpretation, supported by the cited poem using fūm as a verb for baking. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Other reported definitions make fūm the grains used for baking or bread itself. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مجاهد وابن عطاء وابن زيد): They interpret فومها as bread. IBNKATHIR-FULL (اللغة القديمة): An older Arabic usage takes fūmū lanā to mean bake bread for us. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): If the baking gloss is correct, Ibn Jarir says fūm is a letter substitution: fāʾ and thāʾ may exchange because their articulation points are close. IBNKATHIR-FULL (آخرون): Another group interprets fūm as wheat, the grain from which bread is made. IBNKATHIR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid and Ata interpret fūm as bread. BIQAI-FULL: The author identifies fūm as wheat, while Al-Harali explains it as grain used for baking bread. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses fum as bread, with another wording identifying it as wheat in the Banu Hashim dialect. DURR-FULL (مجاهد وعطاء): Mujahid and Ata identify fum as bread. DURR-FULL (الحسن وأبو مالك): Hasan and Abu Malik also give bread as a meaning of fum. ALUSI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says the language authorities agree that الفوم means wheat and other grains used for baking. ALUSI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas is quoted as identifying الفوم with bread; the author says this can be understood as a word also applied to bread, though bread is unlikely here because it grows neither from earth nor alongside greens. BINTSHATI-IJAZ (الفراء): Al-Farra says fūm in an old dialect can mean both wheat and bread; Arabs also used fawwimū lanā to mean bake for us. [42 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة والحسن); IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB(الفراء); TAB-FULL(ابن زيد,ابن عباس,بعضهم,عطاء ومجاهد,قتادة والحسن); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); THALABI(ابن عباس); MAWARDI-FULL(مجاهد); WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); JISHUMI(ابن عباس); BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(عطاء وقتادة,مجاهد); TABRISI(الزجاج,الفراء والأزهري,قوم); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء); RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL(الفراء); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل,مجاهد وابن عطاء وابن زيد); IBNKATHIR-FULL(آخرون,ابن جرير,اللغة القديمة,مجاهد); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,الحسن وأبو مالك,مجاهد وعطاء); ALUSI-FULL(ابن عباس,الزجاج); BINTSHATI-IJAZ(الفراء); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن قتيبة)+; ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q22/p4 Edible grain generally — TAB-FULL (قتادة والحسن): Qatada and al-Hasan say fūm is the grain from which people make bread. THALABI: An unnamed view takes fūm to mean all grains. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says fawm means wheat, or grain generally, and rejects garlic as unknown and implausible because the people sought staple food containing grain. JISHUMI (القتبي): Al-Qutaybī interprets fūm as all grains. BAGHAWI-FULL (القتيبي): Al-Qutaybi says fūm means all edible grains. IBNATIYYA-FULL (عطاء وقتادة): Ata and Qatada define fūm as grains suitable for baking, including wheat, fava beans, and lentils. QURTUBI-FULL (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ calls fūmū lanā an old dialect for 'bake for us'; ʿAṭāʾ and Qatādah define fūm as any grain made into bread. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن قتيبة، والزجاج): Ibn Qutayba and al-Zajjāj say fūm means edible grains. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Other reported definitions make fūm the grains used for baking or bread itself. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another interpretation takes فومها to mean all grains. IBNKATHIR-FULL (آخرون): Another group interprets fūm as wheat, the grain from which bread is made. IBNKATHIR-FULL (البخاري): Al-Bukhari reports a view that fūm means all grains people eat. ALUSI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says the language authorities agree that الفوم means wheat and other grains used for baking. [13 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة والحسن); THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج); JISHUMI(القتبي); BAGHAWI-FULL(القتيبي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(عطاء وقتادة); QURTUBI-FULL(الفراء); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن قتيبة، والزجاج,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(آخرون,البخاري); ALUSI-FULL(الزجاج)]
- 2:61/q22/p5 A reported sense of fūm — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Another reported interpretation takes fūm to mean grains. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The verse asks whether the Israelites would exchange the inferior herbs, cucumbers, fūm, lentils, and onions for the better manna and quail. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن قتيبة، الزجاج): A third interpretation says fūm means grains. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مؤرج): Muʾarrij’s quotation of Ḥassān’s verse glosses its food words as fūm and onion. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says he follows Zayd's recitation but adopts about fourteen letters from Ibn Masud's reading, including this phrase. DURR-FULL (أمية بن أبي الصلت): Umayya b. Abi al-Salt uses fum in poetry while describing their former homes, gardens, and onions. [6 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; IBNATIYYA-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة، الزجاج); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مؤرج); DURR-FULL(أمية بن أبي الصلت,ابن عباس)]
- 2:61/q22/p6 Chickpeas — QURTUBI-FULL: Other dialectal explanations make fūm chickpeas in Syrian speech; fāmī is an altered nisbah of fūmī, and fūmū lanā means bake for us. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another view identifies fūm as chickpea in a Syrian dialect; its seller is called fāmī, a nisba form from fūmī, like shahli and dahri. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another interpretation takes فومها to mean chickpeas. IBNASHUR-FULL (قيل): Another reported interpretation takes fūm to mean chickpeas in the dialect of the people of Syria. [4 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q22/p7 Other lexical senses — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قطرب): Quṭrub gives fūm three senses: an onion cluster, a large piece of meat, or a large mouthful. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قطرب)]

## 2:61/q23 What rulings are drawn about garlic and onion?  [وَفُومِهَا وَعَدَسِهَا وَبَصَلِهَا · law]
turns on: Sound reports permitting the foods versus the congregational-prayer argument and the mosque-odor rule.
- 2:61/q23/p1 The Prophet disliked the smell — QURTUBI-FULL (جابر): Jābir reports that the Prophet told a companion to eat greens after finding them in a pot, explaining that he privately converses with one the companion does not; Muslim and Abū Dāwūd transmit it. QURTUBI-FULL: Al-Qurṭubī takes Jābir's report to show the restriction was particular to the Prophet, while others may eat these foods. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو أيوب): Abū Ayyūb served the Prophet food with garlic; asked whether it was forbidden, the Prophet said no, but that he disliked it. Muslim records the report. [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو أيوب,جابر)]
- 2:61/q23/p2 Eaters must avoid the mosque until the odor is removed — QURTUBI-FULL: These reports suggest a personal restriction because the Prophet alone was singled out for conversation with the angel. QURTUBI-FULL (جابر): Jābir's report establishes the shared rule: anyone eating garlic, onion, or leek must not approach the mosque because angels are harmed by what harms people. QURTUBI-FULL (عمر بن الخطاب): In a report transmitted by Muslim, ʿUmar says the Prophet removed people smelling of onion or garlic from the mosque to al-Baqīʿ and ordered eaters to cook them thoroughly. [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(جابر,عمر بن الخطاب)]
- 2:61/q23/p3 Eating garlic and onion is lawful — QURTUBI-FULL (جمهور العلماء): Most scholars permit eating onions, garlic, and other foul-smelling greens because sound reports establish their permissibility. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو سعيد الخدري): Abū Saʿīd reports the Prophet saying at Khaybar that he does not prohibit what God made lawful but dislikes the smell of garlic. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري,جمهور العلماء)]
- 2:61/q23/p4 They are prohibited under the congregational prayer argument — QURTUBI-FULL (طائفة من أهل الظاهر): Some literalists who hold congregational prayer obligatory prohibit these foods, arguing that deliberately doing what prevents an obligation is forbidden and the Prophet called them foul. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو سعيد الخدري): Abū Saʿīd reports the Prophet saying at Khaybar that he does not prohibit what God made lawful but dislikes the smell of garlic. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري,طائفة من أهل الظاهر)]

## 2:61/q24 What reports and uses are cited for lentils?  [وَعَدَسِهَا · reports]
- 2:61/q24/p1 Reports commend lentils and describe their use — THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from ʿAlī praises lentils as blessed and purifying, softening the heart and increasing tears; it says seventy prophets blessed them, with Jesus the last. QURTUBI-FULL (علي بن أبي طالب): A report attributed to ʿAlī from the Prophet calls lentils blessed and sanctified, softening the heart and increasing tears, and says seventy prophets including Jesus blessed them. QURTUBI-FULL (عمر بن عبد العزيز): ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz alternated bread with oil, meat, and lentils on successive days. QURTUBI-FULL (الحليمي): Al-Ḥalīmī calls lentils and olive oil food of the righteous; their connection with Abraham's hospitality is itself a virtue, and lentils ease worship without stirring desire like meat. QURTUBI-FULL: Wheat is the soundest identification of fūm, barley was close and fed Medina, and lentils were food in Abraham's town, linking the grains to the two prophets. QURTUBI-FULL: A report says the Prophet and his household never ate their fill of wheat bread for three consecutive days from his arrival in Medina until his death. [6 notes: THALABI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(الحليمي,علي بن أبي طالب,عمر بن عبد العزيز)]

## 2:61/q25 What does ʿadas mean?  [وَعَدَسِهَا · meaning]
- 2:61/q25/p1 The familiar lentil — TABRISI: ʿAdas means the familiar lentil grain. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: ʿAdas is the familiar lentil; ʿAdas is also a proper name and a command used to urge a mule. BURSEVI: Lentils are a familiar seed whose measure by volume matches its weight. BURSEVI: The verse permits eating wholesome and pleasurable foods; the Prophet liked sweets and honey and drank cool, sweet water, while lentils and oil are foods of the righteous. [4 notes: TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BURSEVI]

## 2:61/q26 What does baṣal mean?  [وَبَصَلِهَا · meaning]
- 2:61/q26/p1 Onion — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari prefers the partitive reading: they asked for some of the earth's produce; he also says the listed plants, gourds, lentils, and onions were familiar crops and seeds. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan says they were people accustomed to leeks, onions, and lentils, and their appetites returned to their bad old habits. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Baṣal simply means onion. BURSEVI: Onion is a familiar green used to flavor cooking pots. ALUSI-FULL: Another rationale is that the list starts with food eaten without fire, then gives foods that should be cooked; onion may become cold and moist after cooking according to some. [5 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; TAB-FULL+]

## 2:61/q27 Who speaks the rebuke and descent command?  [قَالَ ٱهْبِطُوا۟ · referent]
turns on: Whether the pronoun resumes Moses or introduces God's answer.
- 2:61/q27/p1 The speaker is disputed — TAB-FULL (جماعة من أهل العربية): A group of Arabic grammarians denies that min can be redundant, arguing that it always signals that the speaker intends some of what follows. TABRISI (أبو علي): Abu Ali argues that the hamzated form of nabiʾ preserves the word's original hamza, supported by Hijazi speakers who pronounce it. TABRISI: Supplication is a request addressed by a speaker to someone above him; its distinction from a command lies in the speakers' rank. SAMIN-DURR: The alif in باء is from wāw, as shown by باء يبوء and the pattern قال يقول; باء means to return, and the prophetic wording أبوء بنعمتك علي illustrates it. BURHAN (النحاة والبيانيون): Grammarians and rhetoricians differ over whether an affirmative question used for acknowledgment asserts a fact or seeks the addressee’s admission despite the speaker already knowing it. ALUSI-FULL: The pause after خبر is sufficient under the same-speaker analysis and complete under the two-speaker analysis. [6 notes: TAB-FULL(جماعة من أهل العربية); TABRISI(أبو علي); SAMIN-DURR; BURHAN(النحاة والبيانيون); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q27/p2 Moses spoke — IBNATIYYA-FULL: The verse omits the intervening events: Moses prayed to his Lord, received an answer, and then told them to descend. BAYDAWI-FULL: The speaker of ‘he said’ may be God or Moses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The pronoun in قال most naturally refers to Moses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If Moses said أتستبدلون, the narrative omits that he prayed and God answered before the command هبطوا. ALUSI-FULL: The question أتستبدلون is a fresh sentence answering the implied question of what Moses or God said. ALUSI-FULL: If both sentences are spoken by the same speaker, their separation has the stated grammatical rationale; if Moses and God speak them separately, the lack of conjunction is self-explanatory. [6 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q27/p3 God spoke — BAYDAWI-FULL: The speaker of ‘he said’ may be God or Moses. ALUSI-FULL: The question أتستبدلون is a fresh sentence answering the implied question of what Moses or God said. ALUSI-FULL: If both sentences are spoken by the same speaker, their separation has the stated grammatical rationale; if Moses and God speak them separately, the lack of conjunction is self-explanatory. [3 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q28 What does the exchange wording mean and how is it constructed?  [أَتَسْتَبْدِلُونَ ٱلَّذِى هُوَ أَدْنَىٰ بِٱلَّذِى هُوَ خَيْرٌ · grammar]
turns on: The transmitted variant and which term the bāʾ marks as relinquished.
- 2:61/q28/p1 Istibdāl replaces one thing with another — TAB-FULL: If the variant is authentic, al-Tabari explains the f/thāʾ difference as letter substitution caused by nearby articulation points, citing Arabic word pairs. QURTUBI-FULL: Istibdāl means putting one thing in place of another, as with badal. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Neither consonant substitution is productive: thāʾ may become fāʾ and fāʾ may become thāʾ, as in athāfī/athāthī. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The interrogative hamza in أتستبدلون expresses denial, and istibdāl means taking something in exchange. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (شاعر): The cited poetic form سأيلتهم is analyzed as the hamzated ساءلتهم with a yāʾ substitution; the poet combines the substitute with the hamza it replaces under metrical pressure. SAMIN-DURR: Qalun's omission of hamza in the two al-Ahzab positions follows his treatment of two adjacent hamzas: a preceding long vowel triggers substitution and assimilation, as in بالسوء إلا. IBNASHUR-FULL: The s and t in استبدل intensify the action rather than request it, as in استغنى, استجاب, and استكبر. IBNASHUR-FULL: استبدل derives from بدل; Arabic uses several augmented derivatives, while a simple unaugmented verb is unattested. IBNASHUR-FULL (أبو الشيص): Abū al-Shayṣ’s verse illustrates بدل taking من for the thing exchanged away. IBNASHUR-FULL: Ibn ʿĀshūr sees no difference among بدل, تبدل, and استبدل in this construction; he finds al-Kashshāf’s account unsupported by lexicographers or its own al-Asās. [10 notes: TAB-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(شاعر); SAMIN-DURR; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو الشيص); IBNASHUR-FULL+]
- 2:61/q28/p2 Direct object taken, bāʾ phrase relinquished — SAMIN-DURR (المهدوي| rejects):  BIQAI-FULL: The author notes that b-d-l has many constructions: the two things exchanged may be mentioned or omitted, the preposition may mark what is relinquished or received, and the verb may take one or two objects with differing senses. BURSEVI: The exchange preposition attaches to what is relinquished, and manna and quail are better for taste, ease, and other reasons than the requested produce. IBNASHUR-FULL: In the common construction with باء, the direct object is what is taken and the باء phrase what is given; here the lesser food is taken for the better food. IBNASHUR-FULL (الكشاف): Al-Kashshāf treats بدل as having a باء construction in which the given and taken items are reversed from تبدل and استبدل; Qutb and al-Taftāzānī develop variants of this analysis. BINTSHATI-IJAZ: Bint al-Shati notes that in substitution constructions the preposition bi enters the thing relinquished, as in exchanging the lower for the better. [6 notes: SAMIN-DURR(المهدوي); BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL(الكشاف); BINTSHATI-IJAZ]
- 2:61/q28/p3 Ubayy's atubaddilūn variant — THALABI: Ubayy’s codex reads atubaddilūn in place of atastabdilūn. ALUSI-FULL (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy read أتبدلون; this is figurative because God effects the change, but their request causes it, so they are described as exchangers. [2 notes: THALABI; ALUSI-FULL(أبي بن كعب)]

## 2:61/q29 What is inferior and what is better, and why?  [ٱلَّذِى هُوَ أَدْنَىٰ بِٱلَّذِى هُوَ خَيْرٌ · interpretation]
turns on: Value, taste, benefit, effortless certainty, divine reward, or freedom from captivity.
- 2:61/q29/p1 Manna and quail are better than requested produce — ZAJJAJ: Dropping hamza is better: hamzated faʿīl forms usually pluralize as faʿlāʾ, whereas weak-root forms take afʿilāʾ; the latter pattern is possible with sound roots but rare. NAHHAS: Though some derive أدنى as inferior or nearer, the best meaning is what is closer in worldly life compared with what is better in the afterlife because of its reward. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Moses calls the requested food lower and worse than the higher, better food they had. DAMGHANI: Adnā means inferior or lesser: the Israelites are asking to exchange food inferior to manna and quail for what is better. JISHUMI (موسى): One report says Moses spoke the rebuke about exchanging the better food for the lower; another says God spoke it. JISHUMI: One interpretation says they were leaving better food for worse; another says they rejected God’s choice for their own, making the rhetorical question a prohibition. JISHUMI: Other readings explain adnā as “closer” in ease of access, or say they exchanged inferior food for tastier food. JISHUMI (الحسن): Though they valued manna and quail, their taste had been formed by onions and lentils, so they longed for familiar food. BAGHAWI-FULL: “Better” may refer to God's choice for them as superior to their own choice. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Their divine origin makes their lawfulness certain, whereas produce acquired through commerce or usurpation can involve doubtful status. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Manna and quail are better because God supplied and commanded them, so continuing to obey and thank Him earns a reward stored for the afterlife; the requested food lacks these qualities. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Manna and quail may be better in taste and pleasure, since vegetables are inferior in this respect. IBNATIYYA-FULL: They require no labor, whereas the requested crops require cultivation and toil. TABRISI: One view makes Moses ask whether they exchange the better food God chose for what is lower and worse; another makes God say it. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Adnā means lower or worse, while khayr means higher; manna and quail were better than what the people requested. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj considers leaving the hamza the better form; nabi may derive from informing or from rising in rank. RAZI-FULL: People may prefer food familiar from their upbringing, even if inferior, to unfamiliar food that is more excellent. RAZI-FULL: An objection takes Moses' question about replacing better with inferior food as an explicit condemnation proving the request sinful. RAZI-FULL: The comparison does not rank the foods' taste universally, since a food delightful to one people may be inferior to another; it concerns the certainty and effortless availability of manna and quail versus the requested foods. QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج): First, al-Zajjāj says manna and quail are better because the requested vegetables have little worth by comparison. BAYDAWI-FULL: The better food means manna and quails, superior in pleasure, benefit, and freedom from the need to work for food. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أكثر أهل الظاهر من المفسرين): Most outward commentators regard the request as sinful because they disliked the manna and quail, Moses called their requested food inferior, and his question rebuked them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: They asked Moses because a prophet’s prayer is more likely to be answered; they had eaten the same food for forty years, were unused to it, and preferred familiar foods even when inferior. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): A third explanation grounds the comparison in taste and pleasure: manna and quails are better tasting than the vegetables requested. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The six explanations locate superiority in value, obedience and reward, pleasure, effort, lawfulness, or the kind of food. IBNKATHIR-FULL: God reminds Israel of the pleasant, nourishing, easy provision of manna and quails, then rebukes their weariness and request to replace it with inferior produce. BIQAI-FULL: Moses' question rebukes them for exchanging what is lower in rank for what is better. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): The Israelites wanted to exchange manna and quail for the greens and foods they listed. ALUSI-FULL: The better food is manna and quails; its superiority lies in greater value, taste, benefit, ease of acquisition, and freedom from doubt about its lawfulness. ALUSI-FULL: The speaker may be God speaking through Moses, which suits the surrounding enumeration of blessings, or Moses himself, which better fits the discourse sequence. STUDYQURAN (commentators): The better food refers to manna and quails, while the lesser food means the items the Israelites requested. [31 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ; DAMGHANI; JISHUMI(الحسن,موسى); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أكثر أهل الظاهر من المفسرين,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(مجاهد); ALUSI-FULL; STUDYQURAN(commentators); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج)+; NAHHAS+; ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q29/p2 God's choice is better than their own — IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatada interprets “lower” as worse; al-Hasan gave the same interpretation. JISHUMI (ابن السراج): Duʿāʾ may derive from calling out; its essential form is a request from someone lower to someone above, unlike a command, which reflects rank. IBNATIYYA-FULL (غيره): Another derives it from dūn, lower: its original form أدون is reshaped to أفلع, with final wāw changing to alif. IBNATIYYA-FULL: They may be superior as nourishment and benefit. QURTUBI-FULL (النبي محمد): The Prophet described Zamzam as nourishment for the hungry and a cure for illness. QURTUBI-FULL: The verb ṭaʿima means to eat or taste; it means 'taste' in 2:249 and 'eat' in 33:53. BURHAN: Al-Zarkashi says this contradicts the rhetoricians’ consensus: the cited questions refer to past actions; no evidence supports future reference, and the grammar of أتقتلون does not determine the intended time. BURHAN: For rhetorical questions used in negative forms, the interrogative may disappear entirely, remain alongside another sense, or allow either reading; context decides. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali warns Muslims against imitating Israel by trading higher, wholesome provision receivedfreely for lower forbidden or doubtful worldly gains. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines supplication as a humble, needy call requesting what necessity demands from the one who can provide it, unlike a command from a higher authority who does not need its object. BURSEVI: Heedlessness sickens the heart and prevents it from tasting faith; as fever makes sugar taste bitter, heedlessness is lethal poison to hearts. ALUSI-FULL: Descent may be spatial, if the wilderness is higher than the town, or a descent in rank; the contextual sense is the latter. ALUSI-FULL: The explanation that the exchange takes place in the stomach is dismissed. IBNASHUR-FULL: The second complement can also take من with the sense of the exchange باء, as in the construction cited with the verse. [14 notes: IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); JISHUMI(ابن السراج); IBNATIYYA-FULL(غيره); QURTUBI-FULL(النبي محمد); BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL+]
- 2:61/q29/p3 Freedom is better than Egyptian captivity — BAYDAWI-FULL: The better food means manna and quails, superior in pleasure, benefit, and freedom from the need to work for food. ALUSI-FULL: The better food is manna and quails; its superiority lies in greater value, taste, benefit, ease of acquisition, and freedom from doubt about its lawfulness. [2 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q30 What does adnā mean or derive from?  [أَدْنَىٰ · meaning]
turns on: Derivation from nearness, hamzated baseness, or dūn.
- 2:61/q30/p1 Lower or less valuable — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari defines substitution as leaving one thing for another and glosses adnā as lower, meaner, and less valuable. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari glosses adnā as lower, meaner, and less valuable, connecting it with d-n-ʾ words for low or base things. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid glosses adnā as more inferior or worse. ZAJJAJ: The unhamzated reading “adnā” is preferred in transmission and means nearer or lower in value; the hamzated “adnaʾ” is also possible and denotes something base or vile. NAHHAS: Deriving أدنى from دنيء is allowable only in poetry, not prose, and so cannot explain the Qurʾānic wording. THALABI: Adnā means lower, baser, and worse; khayr means nobler and better, so the rebuke asks why they exchange good for evil. WAHIDI-BASIT: Another reading takes adna as nearer in value, meaning of lesser worth, or as less tasty and pleasurable than what was better. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra also derives adna from baseness, with Zuhayr al-Farqabi reportedly reading it adna'a with hamza. WAHIDI-BASIT (بعض النحويين): Some grammarians explain adna as adwan, meaning lower or meaner, by swapping n and w; the author rejects this because dun meaning inferior does not yield a verb. BAGHAWI-FULL: “Adnā” means lower, baser, or worse. KASHSHAF-FULL: Adnā means lower in rank and smaller in value; nearness and distance can metaphorically indicate low and high worth. KASHSHAF-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabī reads adnaʾ with hamza, deriving it from meanness. IBNATIYYA-FULL (علي بن سليمان): Ali ibn Sulayman derives أدنى, with eased hamza, from danīʾ, meaning plainly base or inferior. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Istibdāl means seeking to put one thing in another's place; al-Zajjaj derives أدنى from nearness in value. TABRISI (الفراء): Al-Farra prefers deriving adnā from baseness, with hamza omitted. TABRISI (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd distinguishes al-danī without hamza, meaning vile, from al-danīʾ with hamza, meaning lewd. TABRISI: Adnā can mean nearer or lower, as in something close or inferior. TABRISI: Another reading of adnā is the lower-priced choice in place of the more valuable, tasty one. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Adnā can mean lower or inferior. RAZI-FULL: Adnā does not mean inferior in religious benefit, since God granted the request; it refers to worldly benefit. RAZI-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabi reads adnaʾ with hamza, from the word for baseness. QURTUBI-FULL (علي بن سليمان): ʿAlī b. Sulaymān derives adnā from the base dāniʾ, low or vile, with the hamzah softened. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): Another derivation takes adnā from dūn, inferior: its form began as adwan, underwent metathesis, and changed final wāw to alif. BAYDAWI-FULL: Adnā means nearer in rank and lower in worth; spatial nearness is metaphorically used for baseness and distance for honor. BAYDAWI-FULL: A reading has adnaʾ, derived from lowliness. NASAFI-FULL: Adna means lower in rank and lesser in value, while what is better is higher and nobler. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأخفش): ʿAlī ibn Sulaymān al-Akhfash derives adnā from danāʾa, baseness or poor quality, with hamza lightened to alif. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (غيره): Another grammarian derives adnā from dūn, ‘lower in rank,’ as a transformed form of adwan, like awlā from wayl. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: On the Basran analysis, هو أدنى is the relative clause and أدنى its predicate; the comparison phrase من ذلك الطعام الواحد is omitted because the comparative is predicative and its counterpart follows. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If أدنى arose by metathesis from أدون, both forms mean lowliness and make a better contrast with خير; if it means nearer, its opposite is farther. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari chooses the value reading, glossing أدنى as lower in rank and less in worth; nearness can figuratively mean small value. SAMIN-DURR (أبو إسحاق الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says أدنى derives from دنو, nearness; it can be lower in value and baseness, or nearer to them in this world, while the better food brings afterlife benefit through patience. SAMIN-DURR (علي بن سليمان الأخفش): Al-Akhfash derives أدنى from أدنأ, the lowly or base thing, with hamza softened; the poem is cited as a witness. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): A third etymology takes أدنى from أدون, the bad or inferior thing; the middle radical is moved after the final radical, producing أَدْنَو, then undergoes weak-letter change, on pattern أفلع. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid glosses adna as worse or inferior. BURSEVI: The rebuke asks whether they are choosing the lower thing for themselves in exchange for what is better; “adnā” is lower in rank and worth. ALUSI-FULL: أدنى may derive from nearness or be inverted from دون, meaning inferior; on the first derivation, nearness is metaphorically used for low worth, as distance is used for honor. [37 notes: TAB-FULL(مجاهد); NAHHAS; THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء,بعض النحويين); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL(زهير الفرقبي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(علي بن سليمان); TABRISI(ابن زيد); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; RAZI-FULL(زهير الفرقبي); QURTUBI-FULL(علي بن سليمان,قيل); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأخفش,غيره); SAMIN-DURR(أبو إسحاق الزجاج,علي بن سليمان الأخفش,قيل); DURR-FULL(مجاهد); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري)+; TABRISI(الفراء)+; ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q30/p2 From hamzated baseness — ZAJJAJ: The unhamzated reading “adnā” is preferred in transmission and means nearer or lower in value; the hamzated “adnaʾ” is also possible and denotes something base or vile. NAHHAS (علي بن سليمان): He says أدنى can only derive from hamzated دنيء, with hamza substituted. Against — NAHHAS: Deriving أدنى from دنيء is allowable only in poetry, not prose, and so cannot explain the Qurʾānic wording. THALABI (الفراء عن زهير العرقي): Zuhayr al-ʿAriqī is reported to read adnāʾ with hamza; the common reading omits hamza. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra also derives adna from baseness, with Zuhayr al-Farqabi reportedly reading it adna'a with hamza. KASHSHAF-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabī reads adnaʾ with hamza, deriving it from meanness. IBNATIYYA-FULL (علي بن سليمان): Ali ibn Sulayman derives أدنى, with eased hamza, from danīʾ, meaning plainly base or inferior. TABRISI (الفراء): Al-Farra prefers deriving adnā from baseness, with hamza omitted. TABRISI (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd distinguishes al-danī without hamza, meaning vile, from al-danīʾ with hamza, meaning lewd. RAZI-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabi reads adnaʾ with hamza, from the word for baseness. QURTUBI-FULL (علي بن سليمان): ʿAlī b. Sulaymān derives adnā from the base dāniʾ, low or vile, with the hamzah softened. QURTUBI-FULL: A noncanonical reading is adnaʾ, with hamzah. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأخفش): ʿAlī ibn Sulaymān al-Akhfash derives adnā from danāʾa, baseness or poor quality, with hamza lightened to alif. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو زيد): Abū Zayd records the hamzated forms danuʾa/yadnaʾu and danāʾa/danāʾ. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabi, also called Zuhayr al-Kisa'i, reads أدنأ with hamza. SAMIN-DURR (علي بن سليمان الأخفش): Al-Akhfash derives أدنى from أدنأ, the lowly or base thing, with hamza softened; the poem is cited as a witness. SAMIN-DURR: The poem fragment is cited with al-Akhfash's proposal that أدنى originated as أدنأ with hamza and underwent hamza easing. SAMIN-DURR (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Firqabi reads أدنأ with hamza, supporting the derivation from دنأ and dناءة. ALUSI-FULL (زهير والكسائي): Zuhayr and al-Kisaʾi read أدنا with hamza, supporting derivation from الدناءة with hamza changed into alif. [18 notes: NAHHAS(علي بن سليمان); THALABI(الفراء عن زهير العرقي); WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); KASHSHAF-FULL(زهير الفرقبي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(علي بن سليمان); TABRISI(ابن زيد); RAZI-FULL(زهير الفرقبي); QURTUBI-FULL(علي بن سليمان); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو زيد,الأخفش,زهير الفرقبي); SAMIN-DURR(زهير الفرقبي,علي بن سليمان الأخفش); ALUSI-FULL(زهير والكسائي); TABRISI(الفراء)+; ZAJJAJ+; NAHHAS-]
- 2:61/q30/p3 Nearer or readily available — TAB-FULL (بعضهم): Some interpret adnā as nearer, deriving it from d-n-w, nearness. ZAJJAJ: The unhamzated reading “adnā” is preferred in transmission and means nearer or lower in value; the hamzated “adnaʾ” is also possible and denotes something base or vile. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj prefers adna from nearness: the people would exchange a readily available, ordinary food for the noble provision specially granted them over others. WAHIDI-BASIT: Another reading takes adna as nearer in value, meaning of lesser worth, or as less tasty and pleasurable than what was better. KASHSHAF-FULL: Adnā means lower in rank and smaller in value; nearness and distance can metaphorically indicate low and high worth. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Istibdāl means seeking to put one thing in another's place; al-Zajjaj derives أدنى from nearness in value. TABRISI: Adnā can mean nearer or lower, as in something close or inferior. QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj derives adnā from d-n-w, closeness in value: a comparable garment is cheap. BAYDAWI-FULL: Adnā means nearer in rank and lower in worth; spatial nearness is metaphorically used for baseness and distance for honor. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Adnā may be the elative of dunūw, ‘nearness,’ from danā/yadnū. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If أدنى arose by metathesis from أدون, both forms mean lowliness and make a better contrast with خير; if it means nearer, its opposite is farther. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari chooses the value reading, glossing أدنى as lower in rank and less in worth; nearness can figuratively mean small value. SAMIN-DURR (أبو إسحاق الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says أدنى derives from دنو, nearness; it can be lower in value and baseness, or nearer to them in this world, while the better food brings afterlife benefit through patience. ALUSI-FULL: أدنى may derive from nearness or be inverted from دون, meaning inferior; on the first derivation, nearness is metaphorically used for low worth, as distance is used for honor. [14 notes: TAB-FULL(بعضهم); WAHIDI-BASIT; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(أبو إسحاق الزجاج); ALUSI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري)+; WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج)+; ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q30/p4 The comparative denotes an inferior choice — WAHIDI-BASIT (الكسائي): Al-Kisa'i says eloquent Arabs freely decline words normally treated as indeclinable, including in poetry; only comparatives on af'al are never heard declined. IBNATIYYA-FULL (زهير للكسائي): Zuhayr reports al-Kisaʾi's reading أدنأ. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The omitted comparison after خير is منه, referring back to الذي هو أدنى; both relative clauses are nominal to express unbounded states, while the verbal clause مما تنبت suits recurring growth. SAMIN-DURR: أدنى is the predicate of هو, while هو خير is likewise a relative clause with a resumptive element. BURHAN: When context supplies the comparison, an elative used predicatively often omits the less-preferred object of comparison. ALUSI-FULL: The object is the thing they would obtain, while the noun with باء denotes what would be relinquished; أدنى qualifies the latter. [6 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الكسائي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(زهير للكسائي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BURHAN; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q30/p5 From dūn by metathesis — THALABI (some grammarians): Some grammarians derive adnā from adwan by moving the nūn and changing wāw to yāʾ, as in awlā min al-wayl. Against — WAHIDI-BASIT (بعض النحويين): Some grammarians explain adna as adwan, meaning lower or meaner, by swapping n and w; the author rejects this because dun meaning inferior does not yield a verb. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): Another derivation takes adnā from dūn, inferior: its form began as adwan, underwent metathesis, and changed final wāw to alif. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (غيره): Another grammarian derives adnā from dūn, ‘lower in rank,’ as a transformed form of adwan, like awlā from wayl. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If أدنى arose by metathesis from أدون, both forms mean lowliness and make a better contrast with خير; if it means nearer, its opposite is farther. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): A third etymology takes أدنى from أدون, the bad or inferior thing; the middle radical is moved after the final radical, producing أَدْنَو, then undergoes weak-letter change, on pattern أفلع. [5 notes: THALABI(some grammarians); QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(غيره); SAMIN-DURR(قيل); WAHIDI-BASIT(بعض النحويين)-]

## 2:61/q31 How is adnā read?  [أَدْنَىٰ · readings]
turns on: The common unhamzated form versus reported hamzated adnaʾ.
- 2:61/q31/p1 Hamzated adnaʾ — ZAJJAJ: The unhamzated reading “adnā” is preferred in transmission and means nearer or lower in value; the hamzated “adnaʾ” is also possible and denotes something base or vile. NAHHAS (علي بن سليمان): He says أدنى can only derive from hamzated دنيء, with hamza substituted. TABRISI (الفراء): Al-Farra prefers deriving adnā from baseness, with hamza omitted. TABRISI (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd distinguishes al-danī without hamza, meaning vile, from al-danīʾ with hamza, meaning lewd. RAZI-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabi reads adnaʾ with hamza, from the word for baseness. QURTUBI-FULL (علي بن سليمان): ʿAlī b. Sulaymān derives adnā from the base dāniʾ, low or vile, with the hamzah softened. QURTUBI-FULL: A noncanonical reading is adnaʾ, with hamzah. BAYDAWI-FULL: A reading has adnaʾ, derived from lowliness. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأخفش): ʿAlī ibn Sulaymān al-Akhfash derives adnā from danāʾa, baseness or poor quality, with hamza lightened to alif. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو زيد): Abū Zayd records the hamzated forms danuʾa/yadnaʾu and danāʾa/danāʾ. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Farqabi, also called Zuhayr al-Kisa'i, reads أدنأ with hamza. SAMIN-DURR (علي بن سليمان الأخفش): Al-Akhfash derives أدنى from أدنأ, the lowly or base thing, with hamza softened; the poem is cited as a witness. SAMIN-DURR: The poem fragment is cited with al-Akhfash's proposal that أدنى originated as أدنأ with hamza and underwent hamza easing. SAMIN-DURR (زهير الفرقبي): Zuhayr al-Firqabi reads أدنأ with hamza, supporting the derivation from دنأ and dناءة. ALUSI-FULL (زهير والكسائي): Zuhayr and al-Kisaʾi read أدنا with hamza, supporting derivation from الدناءة with hamza changed into alif. [20 notes: NAHHAS(علي بن سليمان); THALABI(الفراء عن زهير العرقي); WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); KASHSHAF-FULL(زهير الفرقبي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(زهير للكسائي,علي بن سليمان); TABRISI(ابن زيد); RAZI-FULL(زهير الفرقبي); QURTUBI-FULL(علي بن سليمان); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو زيد,الأخفش,زهير الفرقبي); SAMIN-DURR(زهير الفرقبي,علي بن سليمان الأخفش); ALUSI-FULL(زهير والكسائي); TABRISI(الفراء)+; ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q31/p2 Unhamzated adnā — ZAJJAJ: The unhamzated reading “adnā” is preferred in transmission and means nearer or lower in value; the hamzated “adnaʾ” is also possible and denotes something base or vile. THALABI (الفراء عن زهير العرقي): Zuhayr al-ʿAriqī is reported to read adnāʾ with hamza; the common reading omits hamza. TABRISI (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd distinguishes al-danī without hamza, meaning vile, from al-danīʾ with hamza, meaning lewd. [3 notes: THALABI(الفراء عن زهير العرقي); TABRISI(ابن زيد); ZAJJAJ+]
- 2:61/q31/p3 A variant of adnā is reported — WAHIDI-BASIT: Another reading takes adna as nearer in value, meaning of lesser worth, or as less tasty and pleasurable than what was better. TABRISI: Another reading of adnā is the lower-priced choice in place of the more valuable, tasty one. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari chooses the value reading, glossing أدنى as lower in rank and less in worth; nearness can figuratively mean small value. [3 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT; TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري)+]

## 2:61/q32 What does the command to descend mean?  [ٱهْبِطُوا۟ مِصْرًۭا · interpretation]
turns on: Literal settlement, loss of rank, or a reproachful command whose fulfillment is disputed.
- 2:61/q32/p1 Movement into a settlement — BAGHAWI-FULL: “Go down to a city” means descend to one of the towns if they insist on these foods. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: This interpretation explains the order to descend to a city by the availability there of the requested foods. QURTUBI-FULL: The command to go down to a town was a challenge incapacitating them, as in the command to become stone or iron. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (غيره): Another account says they tired of manna and quail, were told to descend to a city, and first encountered Jericho. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The Israelites were then told to settle where their request could be met and descend to the source of the foods they wanted. BURSEVI: “Descend” means leave the wilderness if they want these foods. ELMALILI: Moses asks whether they would exchange a better blessing for an inferior thing and tells them to descend to a town, where they can obtain what they requested; this both fulfills the demand and exposes their poor choice and its outcome. STUDYQURAN: Habaṭa, “go down,” need not describe a change in elevation; English similarly uses “descend” for arriving at a place or moving into or out of a valley. [8 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(غيره); BURSEVI; ELMALILI; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:61/q32/p2 Reproach or impossible command — QURTUBI-FULL: The command to go down to a town was a challenge incapacitating them, as in the command to become stone or iron. IBNASHUR-FULL: The wording اهبطوا rather than لنَهْبِط signals that Moses does not include himself; the threat recalls their desire for their former humiliating life. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q32/p3 A conditional permission to descend — QURTUBI-FULL: Their being commanded to descend while in the wandering was a punishment. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the command means to descend to one of the cities. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:61/q32/p4 Descent in rank or dignity — IBNASHUR-FULL: The wording اهبطوا rather than لنَهْبِط signals that Moses does not include himself; the threat recalls their desire for their former humiliating life. ASAD-NOTES: Habata literally means descending a slope and figuratively falling in dignity; since Moses' exclamation is not literal, Asad combines both senses as returning to Egypt in shame. [2 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL; ASAD-NOTES]

## 2:61/q33 How is ihbiṭū read?  [ٱهْبِطُوا۟ · readings]
turns on: Kasra versus ḍamma on bāʾ.
- 2:61/q33/p1 Ḍamma on bāʾ — KASHSHAF-FULL: The reading with a damma means descend to the place from the wilderness; h-b-ṭ can mean entering a valley or leaving it. RAZI-FULL: The usual reading of ihbiṭū is with bāʾ kasra; another reading has bāʾ ḍamma. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: A reading with ḍamma on the bāʾ in اهبطوا is another dialect form; kasra is more eloquent. ALUSI-FULL: A reading has أهبطوا with damma on both hamza and ba. [4 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL; RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q33/p2 Kasra on bāʾ — RAZI-FULL: The usual reading of ihbiṭū is with bāʾ kasra; another reading has bāʾ ḍamma. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: A reading with ḍamma on the bāʾ in اهبطوا is another dialect form; kasra is more eloquent. [2 notes: RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:61/q33/p3 Two vowel forms are reported — IBNASHUR-FULL: Ibn ʿĀshūr rejects both a causal explanation and a command-answer reading: Moses is angry, not encouraging or persuading them to descend. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q34 What place-name syntax is compared with miṣr?  [مِصْرًۭا · grammar]
- 2:61/q34/p1 Place expressions involve omitted prepositions or fixed appositions — SIBAWAYH (بعض المفسرين): Some exegetes are reported to take مصر in the verse as Egypt specifically. SIBAWAYH: Sībawayh distinguishes verbal-noun or direction expressions such as forward and back from place names, which instead involve an omitted preposition. SIBAWAYH: ʿĀmir ibn al-Ṭufayl's line uses place names; the intended sense includes the preposition bi, unlike the verbal-noun examples. SIBAWAYH: In expressions such as back and belly or plain and mountain, the second noun may be nominative as apposition or emphasis, or accusative in an expanded construction. SIBAWAYH: The implied sense is on the back and belly or in the plain and mountain; this is not ordinary adverbial syntax and is restricted to established pairs. SIBAWAYH (الخليل): Khalīl reports the idiom مطرنا الزرع والضرع; Sībawayh allows its second nouns to be parsed as apposition, emphasis, or accusative. SIBAWAYH: Nominative forms such as night and day can be understood as apposition or as broad usage in which the time is treated as part of the expression. SIBAWAYH: Jarīr's verse treats night as a named part of the expression, supporting Sībawayh's broad nominative construction. SIBAWAYH: In the conditional examples, accusative follows one analysis, while nominative is possible when the clause means that truth or falsehood occurs or is present. SIBAWAYH: A pronoun may be clarified by an explicit body-part noun, as in the phrase about turning a back and belly; the poetry supplies parallel replacements. SIBAWAYH: Al-Jaʿdī's line permits أهلها to replace the implied people associated with Ḥimyar. [11 notes: SIBAWAYH(الخليل,بعض المفسرين)]

## 2:61/q35 Which place does miṣr denote here?  [مِصْرًۭا · referent]
turns on: Tanwīn and proper-name grammar, the Holy Land command, and reports of later settlement.
- 2:61/q35/p1 An unspecified settled town or city — AKHFASH (بعضهم): Some distinguish the two occurrences: مصر in Joseph is the named Egypt, while مصر here means any city among cities. TAB-FULL: Most reciters pronounce miṣran with tanwīn; on this reading it means any town, since the requested crops are found in settled places rather than wilderness. TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada takes miṣran to mean one of the cities or towns. TAB-FULL (من قال إنها مصرا من الأمصار): Proponents of the generic-city reading argue that the Israelites were settled in Syria after leaving Egypt; the holy land was assigned to them, and their descendants entered it under Joshua after the forty years of wandering, with no text saying they returned to Egypt. ZAJJAJ: Most readers include final alif in “miṣran”; it can mean any town, appropriate to their wilderness setting, or Egypt as a masculine proper name and is then declinable. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says “a town” means any one of the cities; al-Suddi, Qatada, and al-Rabi' agree. NAHHAS: مصرا is best read as indefinite, matching the written alif; taking مصر as a proper name is possible but less apt because it is called a town, not a land or country. THALABI (قتادة): Miṣr means any one of the cities: had the verse meant the particular Egypt, it would be uninflected, as in the command to enter Egypt in Joseph’s story. MAWARDI-FULL (قتادة): Qatada, al-Suddi, Mujahid, and Ibn Zayd say miṣran means any city, since the requested produce is available only in towns. WAHIDI-BASIT: With tanwin, Misran can mean any city, since the requested produce was available in towns and cities. JISHUMI (قراءة العامة): The common tanwīn on miṣran means any city; al-Ḥasan omits tanwīn to mean Egypt specifically, and Ibn Masʿūd’s codex lacks alif and tanwīn. JISHUMI (قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وأبو علي): Miṣran may mean any city; Abū ʿAlī argues it cannot mean Egypt because they were told to enter Jerusalem. BAGHAWI-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak identifies the city as the Egypt of Moses and Pharaoh; al-Baghawi prefers the generic meaning because the proper name Egypt would not take tanwīn. KASHSHAF-FULL: If it means the country, it has only one of the two causes of indeclinability; if it means any city, it is indefinite, while Abdullah's codex and al-Aʿmash read the city name without tanwin. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد وغيره ممن صرفها): Mujahid and others who inflect مصر take it as an unspecified town, citing the command to enter a town and reports that the Israelites settled Syria after the wilderness. TABRISI: All canonical readers give Miṣr tanwīn, taking it as an unspecified city since the Israelites were in the wilderness; it may instead mean Egypt proper, a masculine name. TABRISI (قتادة والسدي ومجاهد): Qatada, al-Suddi, and Mujahid take miṣr as any city: the requested crops are found in towns, not in the wilderness. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن مسعود، ابن عباس، قتادة، ابن زيد): One view takes miṣran as any unspecified Egyptian city, since the requested produce was available in towns. RAZI-FULL (مفسرون): Among those accepting tanwin, some take Egypt to mean Pharaoh's country, while others understand it as any town where the requested foods could be found. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم الأصفهاني): Abu Muslim allows Pharaoh's Egypt: without tanwin, Miṣr must be a proper name and that is the only city with this name; with tanwin, it could remain a proper name or mean any city, allowing a choice among cities. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid and others take tanwīn miṣran as an indefinite town among the towns. QURTUBI-FULL: The indefinite-town view cites the apparent order to enter a town and reports that they lived in Syria after the wandering; the Egypt view cites their inheritance of Pharaoh's lands. NASAFI-FULL: Misr may mean any city, to which they descend from the wilderness, located between Jerusalem and Qinnasrin; the wilderness is twelve by eight farsakhs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وابن زيد): With tanwin, مصر can mean any unspecified city, supported by the command to enter a town, later settlement in Syria, and the need for cities to obtain the foods requested. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): An indefinite مصر may refer specifically to Pharaoh's Egypt, using an indefinite noun for a known person or place, as in asking for a man while meaning Zayd. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن بن بحر): Hasan ibn Bahr says مصر means Jerusalem: although indefinite in form, it refers to a specific place. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعض الناس): Some reject Pharaoh's Egypt because the Israelites left it, refused to fight the giants in the Holy Land, wandered for forty years and died, while their descendants entered Syria and later returned to Jerusalem; no exegete or historian says the wanderers went to Egypt. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: He summarizes four tanwin interpretations: an unspecified city anywhere, an unspecified Syrian city, Jerusalem as a specific place, or Pharaoh's Egypt. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas interprets Miṣran as any one of the cities, as transmitted by Ibn Abi Hatim through Ikrima. IBNKATHIR-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi, Qatada, and al-Rabi ibn Anas are reported to give the same interpretation as Ibn Abbas: a city among cities. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Ibn Kathir rejects Ibn Jarir's hesitation and prefers a generic city, as reported from Ibn Abbas and others. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada likewise takes misran to mean a city among cities. BURSEVI: Miṣr can mean a great town; its name is linked to cutting off open space through building, and it can be used for a town or village. It is indefinite here, so it declines. BURSEVI: Miṣr means any town, not Pharaoh’s Egypt: the Israelites had been commanded to enter the Holy Land, so Egypt would not fit. ELMALILI (ekser-i müfessirîn): Most commentators take Misr as a common noun meaning a town, specifically one of the towns in the Holy Land, since Israel did not return to Egypt after leaving it. [35 notes: AKHFASH(بعضهم); TAB-FULL(قتادة,من قال إنها مصرا من الأمصار); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); THALABI(قتادة); MAWARDI-FULL(قتادة); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI(قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وأبو علي,قراءة العامة); BAGHAWI-FULL(الضحاك); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(مجاهد وغيره ممن صرفها); TABRISI(قتادة والسدي ومجاهد); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن مسعود، ابن عباس، قتادة، ابن زيد); RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم الأصفهاني,مفسرون); QURTUBI-FULL(مجاهد); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن بن بحر,بعض الناس,قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وابن زيد,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس,السدي); DURR-FULL(قتادة); BURSEVI; ELMALILI(ekser-i müfessirîn); BURSEVI+; IBNKATHIR-FULL+; NAHHAS+]
- 2:61/q35/p2 Pharaoh's Egypt — TAB-FULL (بعض من قرأ بالإجراء والتنوين): Al-Tabari allows that a reader who adds tanwīn might still intend the proper name Egypt, following the written form of the codex. TAB-FULL (الذي لم ينون مصر): The reading without tanwīn and the alif clearly means the specific, well-known Egypt. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says it means a city, and reports that the Israelites did not return to Egypt. TAB-FULL (آخرون): Other commentators say the command means the Egypt where Pharaoh lived. TAB-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya identifies miṣran with Pharaoh's Egypt. TAB-FULL (أبي بن كعب وعبد الله بن مسعود): The pro-Egypt argument also cites Ubayy ibn Kaʿb's and Ibn Masʿud's reading without an alif, which they take to mean Egypt itself. IBNABIHATIM (أبو العالية): Al-Aliyah interprets the destination as Pharaoh's Egypt. IBNABIHATIM (الأعمش): Al-A'mash identifies it as Egypt, then governed by Salih ibn Ali. THALABI (الأعمش): Miṣr means the Egypt ruled by Ṣāliḥ b. ʿAlī; the uninflected reading of al-Ḥasan and Ṭalḥa and the codices of ʿAbd Allāh and Ubayy support its being a proper name, though inflected for shortness. THALABI (الضحاك): Miṣr means the Egypt of Moses and Pharaoh. MAWARDI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan, Abu al-ʿAliya, and al-Rabi say the verse means the specific Egypt from which the Israelites left, Pharaoh's Egypt. BAGHAWI-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak identifies the city as the Egypt of Moses and Pharaoh; al-Baghawi prefers the generic meaning because the proper name Egypt would not take tanwīn. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة ممن صرفها): Some who inflect مصر still identify it as Pharaoh's Egypt, citing the Quran's account that Israel inherited Pharaoh's lands and traces; they allow its inflection. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وغيرهما): Al-Hasan, Aban ibn Taghlib, and others read مصر without inflection, as does Ubayy's codex, and mean Pharaoh's Egypt. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الأعمش): Al-Aʿmash identifies it as the Egypt over which Salih ibn Ali presides. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أشهب): Ashhab reports Malik's view that it is Malik's own Egypt, the town where Pharaoh resided. TABRISI (الحسن والربيع): Al-Hasan and al-Rabiʿ say the requested Egypt is Pharaoh's Egypt from which the Israelites departed. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو صالح عن ابن عباس، أبو العالية، الضحاك): A second view identifies Miṣr as the specific land of Egypt, Pharaoh’s Egypt according to Ibn Abbas. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ prefers the specific Egypt interpretation and cites the non-tanwīn reading of Miṣr as evidence. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الأعمش): When asked about the reading, al-Aʿmash said it means the Egypt ruled by Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī. RAZI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says the final alif in miṣran was added by a scribe, making the word definite and referring specifically to Pharaoh's Egypt; this is also transmitted from Abu al-ʿAliya and al-Rabiʿ. RAZI-FULL (مفسرون): Among those accepting tanwin, some take Egypt to mean Pharaoh's country, while others understand it as any town where the requested foods could be found. RAZI-FULL (كثير من المفسرين): Many commentators deny that Miṣr means Pharaoh's Egypt, citing the command to enter the Holy Land, the grant of that land, the ban on turning back, and the forty-year exclusion before entry. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم الأصفهاني): Abu Muslim allows Pharaoh's Egypt: without tanwin, Miṣr must be a proper name and that is the only city with this name; with tanwin, it could remain a proper name or mean any city, allowing a choice among cities. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وطلحة): Al-Ḥasan, Abān b. Taghlib, and Ṭalḥah read Miṣr without tanwīn; the Ubayy codex and Ibn Masʿūd likewise identify Pharaoh's Egypt. QURTUBI-FULL (مالك): Mālik told Ashhab that this is Egypt, his own town and Pharaoh's residence, as quoted by Ibn ʿAṭiyyah. QURTUBI-FULL (طائفة ممن صرفها): Other grammarians who read the word with tanwīn still identify it as Egypt itself. QURTUBI-FULL: The indefinite-town view cites the apparent order to enter a town and reports that they lived in Syria after the wandering; the Egypt view cites their inheritance of Pharaoh's lands.
