NASAFI-FULL: Alternatively, Misr means Pharaoh's Egypt; it is declined despite feminine and proper-name features because it means a town or has a quiescent middle consonant, like Noah and Lot. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): An indefinite مصر may refer specifically to Pharaoh's Egypt, using an indefinite noun for a known person or place, as in asking for a man while meaning Zayd. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مالك): Malik identified the place as Egypt, the speaker's town where Pharaoh lived, in a report transmitted by Ashhab. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The un-tanwined reading مصر means the proper name of Pharaoh's land. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: He summarizes four tanwin interpretations: an unspecified city anywhere, an unspecified Syrian city, Jerusalem as a specific place, or Pharaoh's Egypt. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): They may instead have been sent to Egypt itself, which is declinable because its middle consonant is silent and the name is light like هند and دعد; a poem illustrates the forms, or the declension may reflect treating it as a place name. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah and al-Rabi ibn Anas explain the non-declined reading as Egypt of Pharaoh; Ibn Abi Hatim also transmits this from al-Aliyah and al-Aʿmash. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir allows Egypt of Pharaoh even with declension, treating the spelling as following the codex, like 76:15–16, but leaves the intended place unresolved. DURR-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya interprets the place as Pharaoh's Egypt. BURSEVI (قيل): An alternative identifies Miṣr as Pharaoh’s Egypt; its declension is explained by its medial still consonant, or by construing it as “country” rather than “city.” BURSEVI: Miṣr means any town, not Pharaoh’s Egypt: the Israelites had been commanded to enter the Holy Land, so Egypt would not fit. ALUSI-FULL (أشهب عن مالك): Ashhab relates that Malik identified the place as Egypt, his town, where Pharaoh lived, making مصر a proper name. ELMALILI (ekser-i müfessirîn): Most commentators take Misr as a common noun meaning a town, specifically one of the towns in the Holy Land, since Israel did not return to Egypt after leaving it. IBNASHUR-FULL (مالك): Ashhab reports Mālik’s view that مصر here means their own town, the dwelling place of Pharaoh. STUDYQURAN (Ṭ): The orthography requires miṣran rather than miṣra and therefore excludes Egypt itself; some early commentators nevertheless understood it as Pharaoh's miṣr. [43 notes: TAB-FULL(آخرون,أبو العالية,أبي بن كعب وعبد الله بن مسعود,الذي لم ينون مصر,بعض من قرأ بالإجراء والتنوين,مجاهد); IBNABIHATIM(أبو العالية,الأعمش); THALABI(الأعمش,الضحاك); MAWARDI-FULL(الحسن); BAGHAWI-FULL(الضحاك); IBNATIYYA-FULL(أشهب,الأعمش,الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وغيرهما,طائفة ممن صرفها); TABRISI(الحسن والربيع); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو صالح عن ابن عباس، أبو العالية، الضحاك,الأعمش); RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم الأصفهاني,الحسن,كثير من المفسرين,مفسرون); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وطلحة,طائفة ممن صرفها,مالك); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل,مالك); SAMIN-DURR(قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو العالية,ابن جرير); DURR-FULL(أبو العالية); BURSEVI(قيل); ALUSI-FULL(أشهب عن مالك); ELMALILI(ekser-i müfessirîn); IBNASHUR-FULL(مالك); STUDYQURAN(Ṭ); BURSEVI+; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء)+]
- 2:61/q35/p3 The destination remains open — AKHFASH (بعض الناس): Some claimed that مصر in both this verse and the Joseph passage means the specific country of Egypt. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari says the requested produce is found in settled towns and villages, so they must leave the wilderness for a place where it grows. ZAJJAJ: A reading without alif can refer to Egypt: the place name is treated as masculine but named after a feminine city, so it is indeclinable, as in the cited verse. WAHIDI-BASIT: The command to descend to Misr may be spoken by Moses, or by God after an omitted prayer and response. WAHIDI-BASIT: Misr may instead mean Egypt specifically; it could then take declension because the name is light and short. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Their requested produce is available only in towns and cities. JISHUMI (أبو مسلم): Abū Muslim says the command does not entail entry into Egypt. KASHSHAF-FULL: Miṣran may be the proper name of Egypt; it is declined despite properness and femininity because its middle consonant is quiescent, like Noah and Lot. TABRISI (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud is reported to read Miṣr without the alif; it may mean Egypt proper as in Yusuf 12:99, whose name is masculine despite denoting a city, or reflect the written muṣḥaf. BAYDAWI-FULL: Ihbiṭū miṣran means descend from the wilderness to it; the verb describes entering a valley or leaving a higher place. BAYDAWI-FULL (قيل): A reported interpretation takes Miṣr as Egypt; its treatment as a proper name is supported by nonnunation in Ibn Masʿūd's codex. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari compares مصر, when treated as a proper name, to Nuh and Lut, which are declined despite foreignness because they are short with a still middle consonant. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: هبطوا مصرا can mean settle in a city; هبط منه means leave it, as though a person arriving in a town descends upon it. SAMIN-DURR (قرأ الجمهور): The majority read مصر with nunation, matching the written muṣḥaf; one explanation is that they were sent to any Egyptian city, so the word is declinable. SAMIN-DURR (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari says the name is an Arabicized Persian form of مصرائيم; if it names a specific place, its foreignness means it should be indeclinable, like ماه and جور and حمص. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines a miṣr as a substantial town where people coordinate worldly affairs and which gathers the kinds of goods Israel requested; smaller places do not have all of them. DURR-FULL (الأعمش): Al-Aʿmash read misra without nunation and identified it as Egypt governed by Salih b. Ali. ELMALILI: Their request reflects a desire for civilized life, but it rests on a petty craving rather than a lofty purpose and betrays longing for their former Egyptian degradation and slavery, showing failure to value freedom. ELMALILI: One interpretation takes Misr as Egypt, making the command simply a reproach; the author notes that the same reproachful implication remains under the town interpretation. IBNASHUR-FULL (الفخر): Al-Fakhr's reading treats ‘go down to Egypt, and you will have what you asked’ as God's answer and therefore a favor, even though it is conditional on entering a town. IBNASHUR-FULL: اهبطوا is permission colored by rebuke: if this is what they want, they may descend to any Egyptian town, though no nearby town was then reachable. IBNASHUR-FULL (قيل): An alternative reads مصر as Egypt, the country they left; the impossible command to return is pure reproach and a threat recalling their old hardship. IBNASHUR-FULL: If مصر means the country, it may be diptote as a feminine place-name or triptote as a place-name or a feminine three-letter name with a still middle consonant. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Despite their Abrahamic religious and moral heritage, Israelites were degraded by centuries in Egypt and its idolatrous culture, settling for comfort and becoming fearful. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: They longed for ordinary Egyptian life and vegetables, though manna, quail, and twelve springs were extraordinary divine gifts of special value; Moses rebuked their preference for the inferior. STUDYQURAN: Miṣr can mean a town or city; as a proper name or foreign word, it means Egypt. [26 notes: AKHFASH(بعض الناس); TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(ابن مسعود); BAYDAWI-FULL(قيل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري,قرأ الجمهور); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(الأعمش); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(الفخر,قيل); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:61/q35/p4 Jerusalem or a town in the Holy Land — TAB-FULL (من قال إنها مصرا من الأمصار): Proponents of the generic-city reading argue that the Israelites were settled in Syria after leaving Egypt; the holy land was assigned to them, and their descendants entered it under Joshua after the forty years of wandering, with no text saying they returned to Egypt. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari concludes that God gave them a settled, cultivated place where they could obtain the crops they requested; it could have been Egypt or Syria. JISHUMI (قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وأبو علي): Miṣran may mean any city; Abū ʿAlī argues it cannot mean Egypt because they were told to enter Jerusalem. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد وغيره ممن صرفها): Mujahid and others who inflect مصر take it as an unspecified town, citing the command to enter a town and reports that the Israelites settled Syria after the wilderness. TABRISI (أبو مسلم وابن زيد): Abu Muslim, with a report also attributed to Ibn Zayd, identifies Egypt as Jerusalem. RAZI-FULL (كثير من المفسرين): Many commentators deny that Miṣr means Pharaoh's Egypt, citing the command to enter the Holy Land, the grant of that land, the ban on turning back, and the forty-year exclusion before entry. QURTUBI-FULL: The indefinite-town view cites the apparent order to enter a town and reports that they lived in Syria after the wandering; the Egypt view cites their inheritance of Pharaoh's lands. NASAFI-FULL: Misr may mean any city, to which they descend from the wilderness, located between Jerusalem and Qinnasrin; the wilderness is twelve by eight farsakhs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وابن زيد): With tanwin, مصر can mean any unspecified city, supported by the command to enter a town, later settlement in Syria, and the need for cities to obtain the foods requested. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن بن بحر): Hasan ibn Bahr says مصر means Jerusalem: although indefinite in form, it refers to a specific place. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: He summarizes four tanwin interpretations: an unspecified city anywhere, an unspecified Syrian city, Jerusalem as a specific place, or Pharaoh's Egypt. BURSEVI: Miṣr means any town, not Pharaoh’s Egypt: the Israelites had been commanded to enter the Holy Land, so Egypt would not fit. ALUSI-FULL: The claim that مصر is a proper name is supported by Ibn Masʿud's codex spelling it without alif after ra, but opposed by the Qur'anic tanwin and by the command to enter the holy land, understood as Syria, which implies they were confined to the wilderness. ELMALILI (ekser-i müfessirîn): Most commentators take Misr as a common noun meaning a town, specifically one of the towns in the Holy Land, since Israel did not return to Egypt after leaving it. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Moses reminded them of their national dignity and ordered them toward the holy land, but they refused to enter while its strong people remained and told Moses and God to fight without them. [15 notes: TAB-FULL(من قال إنها مصرا من الأمصار); JISHUMI(قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وأبو علي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(مجاهد وغيره ممن صرفها); TABRISI(أبو مسلم وابن زيد); RAZI-FULL(كثير من المفسرين); QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن بن بحر,قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وابن زيد); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI(ekser-i müfessirîn); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; BURSEVI+; TAB-FULL+]
- 2:61/q35/p5 The destination is not settled — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari concludes that God gave them a settled, cultivated place where they could obtain the crops they requested; it could have been Egypt or Syria. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir allows Egypt of Pharaoh even with declension, treating the spelling as following the codex, like 76:15–16, but leaves the intended place unresolved. [2 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير); TAB-FULL+]

## 2:61/q36 How is miṣr read and inflected?  [مِصْرًۭا · readings]
turns on: Written alif and majority recitation versus non-tanwīn codices and proper-name grammar.
- 2:61/q36/p1 Alif and tanwīn — TAB-FULL: Most reciters pronounce miṣran with tanwīn; on this reading it means any town, since the requested crops are found in settled places rather than wilderness. TAB-FULL (بعض من قرأ بالإجراء والتنوين): Al-Tabari allows that a reader who adds tanwīn might still intend the proper name Egypt, following the written form of the codex. TAB-FULL (الذي لم ينون مصر): The reading without tanwīn and the alif clearly means the specific, well-known Egypt. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari accepts the reading with alif and tanwīn because it agrees with Muslim codices and the widespread recitation; he rejects the opposing reading as non-authoritative. NAHHAS: مصرا is best read as indefinite, matching the written alif; taking مصر as a proper name is possible but less apt because it is called a town, not a land or country. MAWARDI-FULL: Most reciters read miṣran with tanwīn; some read it without tanwīn, and Ibn Masud's reading omits the alif. WAHIDI-BASIT: With tanwin, Misran can mean any city, since the requested produce was available in towns and cities. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra prefers leaving Misr undeclined; unlike common feminine names of three letters with a still middle letter, a place-name is rarely used as a personal name, and its written alif marks pause. JISHUMI (قراءة العامة): The common tanwīn on miṣran means any city; al-Ḥasan omits tanwīn to mean Egypt specifically, and Ibn Masʿūd’s codex lacks alif and tanwīn. BAGHAWI-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak identifies the city as the Egypt of Moses and Pharaoh; al-Baghawi prefers the generic meaning because the proper name Egypt would not take tanwīn. KASHSHAF-FULL: If it means the country, it has only one of the two causes of indeclinability; if it means any city, it is indefinite, while Abdullah's codex and al-Aʿmash read the city name without tanwin. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Most readers inflect مصر with tanwin, in agreement with the codex, though some Uthmani codices are reported otherwise. TABRISI: All canonical readers give Miṣr tanwīn, taking it as an unspecified city since the Israelites were in the wilderness; it may instead mean Egypt proper, a masculine name. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ prefers the specific Egypt interpretation and cites the non-tanwīn reading of Miṣr as evidence. RAZI-FULL: The well-known reading gives Miṣran tanwin despite the two factors of definiteness and feminine gender because its middle consonant is quiescent, as with Nuh and Lut; if common noun, it has only one factor. RAZI-FULL (عبد الله): The codex of ʿAbd Allah and al-Aʿmash's reading have Miṣr without tanwin. RAZI-FULL (مفسرون): Among those accepting tanwin, some take Egypt to mean Pharaoh's country, while others understand it as any town where the requested foods could be found. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم الأصفهاني): Abu Muslim allows Pharaoh's Egypt: without tanwin, Miṣr must be a proper name and that is the only city with this name; with tanwin, it could remain a proper name or mean any city, allowing a choice among cities. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وطلحة): Al-Ḥasan, Abān b. Taghlib, and Ṭalḥah read Miṣr without tanwīn; the Ubayy codex and Ibn Masʿūd likewise identify Pharaoh's Egypt. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid and others take tanwīn miṣran as an indefinite town among the towns. QURTUBI-FULL (طائفة ممن صرفها): Other grammarians who read the word with tanwīn still identify it as Egypt itself. QURTUBI-FULL (الأخفش والكسائي): Al-Akhfash and al-Kisāʾī permit tanwīn because the name is light like Hind and Daʿd, citing a poem that uses both case forms. QURTUBI-FULL (سيبويه والخليل والفراء): Sībawayh, al-Khalīl, and al-Farrāʾ reject that declension, reasoning that Zayd would not take tanwīn as a woman's name. QURTUBI-FULL (غير الأخفش): Another grammarian explains the tanwīn by saying the word means a place, making it declinable. BAYDAWI-FULL (قيل): A reported interpretation takes Miṣr as Egypt; its treatment as a proper name is supported by nonnunation in Ibn Masʿūd's codex. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن وطلحة والأعمش وأبان بن تغلب): They read مصر without tanwin, as also written in the codices of Ubayy, Ibn Masud, and some Uthmanic copies; most readers give it tanwin. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وابن زيد): With tanwin, مصر can mean any unspecified city, supported by the command to enter a town, later settlement in Syria, and the need for cities to obtain the foods requested. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (عيسى بن عمر): Isa ibn Umar allowed مصر to be diptote by analogy with Hind, but this is unattested from Arabs except in the declined form, so the author rejects the analogy. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The un-tanwined reading مصر means the proper name of Pharaoh's land. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: He summarizes four tanwin interpretations: an unspecified city anywhere, an unspecified Syrian city, Jerusalem as a specific place, or Pharaoh's Egypt. SAMIN-DURR (قرأ الجمهور): The majority read مصر with nunation, matching the written muṣḥaf; one explanation is that they were sent to any Egyptian city, so the word is declinable. SAMIN-DURR (الحسن وغيره): Al-Hasan and others read مصر without nunation; the same form occurs in some Uthman and Ubayy codices, as if a particular place were intended. SAMIN-DURR: The majority unanimously prevent declension for مصر in يوسف 12:99. IBNKATHIR-FULL: The written and majority reading is Miṣran, declined with tanwin and written with an alif in the Uthmanic codices. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir refuses a reading without declension because the codices unanimously use the declined form. DURR-FULL (الأعمش): Al-Aʿmash read misra without nunation and identified it as Egypt governed by Salih b. Ali. ALUSI-FULL: The claim that مصر is a proper name is supported by Ibn Masʿud's codex spelling it without alif after ra, but opposed by the Qur'anic tanwin and by the command to enter the holy land, understood as Syria, which implies they were confined to the wilderness. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya notes مصر without nunation in Ibn Masʿūd’s reading and without alif in Ubayy’s codex and some ʿUthmānic codices. [38 notes: TAB-FULL(الذي لم ينون مصر,بعض من قرأ بالإجراء والتنوين); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); JISHUMI(قراءة العامة); BAGHAWI-FULL(الضحاك); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم الأصفهاني,عبد الله,مفسرون); QURTUBI-FULL(الأخفش والكسائي,الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وطلحة,سيبويه والخليل والفراء,طائفة ممن صرفها,غير الأخفش,مجاهد); BAYDAWI-FULL(قيل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن وطلحة والأعمش وأبان بن تغلب,عيسى بن عمر,قتادة والسدي ومجاهد وابن زيد); SAMIN-DURR(الحسن وغيره,قرأ الجمهور); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير); DURR-FULL(الأعمش); ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عطية); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء)+; NAHHAS+; TAB-FULL+]
- 2:61/q36/p2 No tanwīn or final alif — TAB-FULL (الذي لم ينون مصر): The reading without tanwīn and the alif clearly means the specific, well-known Egypt. ZAJJAJ: A reading without alif can refer to Egypt: the place name is treated as masculine but named after a feminine city, so it is indeclinable, as in the cited verse. THALABI (الأعمش): Miṣr means the Egypt ruled by Ṣāliḥ b. ʿAlī; the uninflected reading of al-Ḥasan and Ṭalḥa and the codices of ʿAbd Allāh and Ubayy support its being a proper name, though inflected for shortness. THALABI (قتادة): Miṣr means any one of the cities: had the verse meant the particular Egypt, it would be uninflected, as in the command to enter Egypt in Joseph’s story. MAWARDI-FULL: Most reciters read miṣran with tanwīn; some read it without tanwīn, and Ibn Masud's reading omits the alif. WAHIDI-BASIT: The noninflected reading is described as rare; the author favors inflection because the name is light and short. JISHUMI (قراءة العامة): The common tanwīn on miṣran means any city; al-Ḥasan omits tanwīn to mean Egypt specifically, and Ibn Masʿūd’s codex lacks alif and tanwīn. KASHSHAF-FULL: If it means the country, it has only one of the two causes of indeclinability; if it means any city, it is indefinite, while Abdullah's codex and al-Aʿmash read the city name without tanwin. RAZI-FULL (عبد الله): The codex of ʿAbd Allah and al-Aʿmash's reading have Miṣr without tanwin. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم الأصفهاني): Abu Muslim allows Pharaoh's Egypt: without tanwin, Miṣr must be a proper name and that is the only city with this name; with tanwin, it could remain a proper name or mean any city, allowing a choice among cities. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وطلحة): Al-Ḥasan, Abān b. Taghlib, and Ṭalḥah read Miṣr without tanwīn; the Ubayy codex and Ibn Masʿūd likewise identify Pharaoh's Egypt. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن وطلحة والأعمش وأبان بن تغلب): They read مصر without tanwin, as also written in the codices of Ubayy, Ibn Masud, and some Uthmanic copies; most readers give it tanwin. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The proposed diptote form rests on analogy to a disputed case and conflicts with Arabic usage, so it must be discarded. ALUSI-FULL: The claim that مصر is a proper name is supported by Ibn Masʿud's codex spelling it without alif after ra, but opposed by the Qur'anic tanwin and by the command to enter the holy land, understood as Syria, which implies they were confined to the wilderness. ELMALILI: Misr may be a proper name or a common noun; as a proper name it is indeclinable, though declension is permitted because it has three letters and a quiescent middle letter. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya notes مصر without nunation in Ibn Masʿūd’s reading and without alif in Ubayy’s codex and some ʿUthmānic codices. [15 notes: TAB-FULL(الذي لم ينون مصر); ZAJJAJ; THALABI(الأعمش,قتادة); MAWARDI-FULL; JISHUMI(قراءة العامة); KASHSHAF-FULL; RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم الأصفهاني,عبد الله); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وطلحة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن وطلحة والأعمش وأبان بن تغلب); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عطية); WAHIDI-BASIT+; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:61/q36/p3 The reading or inflection is disputed — TAB-FULL (من قال إنها مصرا من الأمصار): Proponents of the generic-city reading argue that the Israelites were settled in Syria after leaving Egypt; the holy land was assigned to them, and their descendants entered it under Joshua after the forty years of wandering, with no text saying they returned to Egypt. TAB-FULL (أبي بن كعب وعبد الله بن مسعود): The pro-Egypt argument also cites Ubayy ibn Kaʿb's and Ibn Masʿud's reading without an alif, which they take to mean Egypt itself. NAHHAS (الكسائي): Al-Kisāʾī allows declension of مصر even as a definite name because it is light like هند; Nahhas rejects this under the rules of Khalil, Sibawayh, and Farraʾ. NAHHAS: The argument is wrong: a woman named زيد would remain diptote under Khalil, Sibawayh, and Farraʾ; the claim that Arabs freely decline all diptotes is also rejected. WAHIDI-BASIT: Misr may instead mean Egypt specifically; it could then take declension because the name is light and short. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد وغيره ممن صرفها): Mujahid and others who inflect مصر take it as an unspecified town, citing the command to enter a town and reports that the Israelites settled Syria after the wilderness. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة ممن صرفها): Some who inflect مصر still identify it as Pharaoh's Egypt, citing the Quran's account that Israel inherited Pharaoh's lands and traces; they allow its inflection. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وغيرهما): Al-Hasan, Aban ibn Taghlib, and others read مصر without inflection, as does Ubayy's codex, and mean Pharaoh's Egypt. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الأعمش): When asked about the reading, al-Aʿmash said it means the Egypt ruled by Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī. RAZI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says the final alif in miṣran was added by a scribe, making the word definite and referring specifically to Pharaoh's Egypt; this is also transmitted from Abu al-ʿAliya and al-Rabiʿ. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Abu Hayyan says foreignness only blocks declension in names longer than three letters, so a three-letter name with a still middle consonant is declined. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The proposed diptote form rests on analogy to a disputed case and conflicts with Arabic usage, so it must be discarded. SAMIN-DURR (قيل): They may instead have been sent to Egypt itself, which is declinable because its middle consonant is silent and the name is light like هند and دعد; a poem illustrates the forms, or the declension may reflect treating it as a place name. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبي بن كعب): The readings of Ubayy ibn Kab and Ibn Masud have Miṣr without declension. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah and al-Rabi ibn Anas explain the non-declined reading as Egypt of Pharaoh; Ibn Abi Hatim also transmits this from al-Aliyah and al-Aʿmash. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir allows Egypt of Pharaoh even with declension, treating the spelling as following the codex, like 76:15–16, but leaves the intended place unresolved. BURHAN (ابن جني): Ibn Jinni notes that grammatical inflection can differ from the meaning; the future-subjunctive أن in “that he says” does not make the condemnatory question’s event future in sense. BURSEVI (قيل): An alternative identifies Miṣr as Pharaoh’s Egypt; its declension is explained by its medial still consonant, or by construing it as “country” rather than “city.” IBNASHUR-FULL (الفخر): Al-Fakhr's reading treats ‘go down to Egypt, and you will have what you asked’ as God's answer and therefore a favor, even though it is conditional on entering a town. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن عطاء الله): Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh warns that a seeker may misread delayed punishment as approval; God may quietly withdraw spiritual provision or leave the seeker to personal desires, placing the person at a distance without awareness. [20 notes: TAB-FULL(أبي بن كعب وعبد الله بن مسعود,من قال إنها مصرا من الأمصار); NAHHAS(الكسائي); WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الحسن وأبان بن تغلب وغيرهما,طائفة ممن صرفها,مجاهد وغيره ممن صرفها); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الأعمش); RAZI-FULL(الحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو العالية,أبي بن كعب,ابن جرير); BURHAN(ابن جني); BURSEVI(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عطاء الله,الفخر)]
- 2:61/q36/p4 A proper name can be declined — AKHFASH: Feminine names such as هند and جمل may be declined or left without declension, depending on Arab usage. ZAJJAJ: Most readers include final alif in “miṣran”; it can mean any town, appropriate to their wilderness setting, or Egypt as a masculine proper name and is then declinable. ZAJJAJ: A reading without alif can refer to Egypt: the place name is treated as masculine but named after a feminine city, so it is indeclinable, as in the cited verse. Against — NAHHAS: The argument is wrong: a woman named زيد would remain diptote under Khalil, Sibawayh, and Farraʾ; the claim that Arabs freely decline all diptotes is also rejected. THALABI (الأعمش): Miṣr means the Egypt ruled by Ṣāliḥ b. ʿAlī; the uninflected reading of al-Ḥasan and Ṭalḥa and the codices of ʿAbd Allāh and Ubayy support its being a proper name, though inflected for shortness. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra prefers leaving Misr undeclined; unlike common feminine names of three letters with a still middle letter, a place-name is rarely used as a personal name, and its written alif marks pause. KASHSHAF-FULL: Miṣran may be the proper name of Egypt; it is declined despite properness and femininity because its middle consonant is quiescent, like Noah and Lot. TABRISI: All canonical readers give Miṣr tanwīn, taking it as an unspecified city since the Israelites were in the wilderness; it may instead mean Egypt proper, a masculine name. TABRISI (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud is reported to read Miṣr without the alif; it may mean Egypt proper as in Yusuf 12:99, whose name is masculine despite denoting a city, or reflect the written muṣḥaf. RAZI-FULL: The well-known reading gives Miṣran tanwin despite the two factors of definiteness and feminine gender because its middle consonant is quiescent, as with Nuh and Lut; if common noun, it has only one factor. NASAFI-FULL: Alternatively, Misr means Pharaoh's Egypt; it is declined despite feminine and proper-name features because it means a town or has a quiescent middle consonant, like Noah and Lot. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (المعربون والمفسرون): Some grammarians and exegetes allow the tanned مصر to be a proper name, explaining its declension by its lightness or by treating it as a place noun. ELMALILI: Misr may be a proper name or a common noun; as a proper name it is indeclinable, though declension is permitted because it has three letters and a quiescent middle letter. IBNASHUR-FULL: If مصر means the country, it may be diptote as a feminine place-name or triptote as a place-name or a feminine three-letter name with a still middle consonant. [13 notes: AKHFASH; ZAJJAJ; THALABI(الأعمش); WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(ابن مسعود); RAZI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(المعربون والمفسرون); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; NAHHAS-]

## 2:61/q37 What does miṣr mean as a place word?  [مِصْرًۭا · meaning]
turns on: Boundary and separation, foreign-name, and milking analogies.
- 2:61/q37/p1 A bounded or separated place — MAWARDI-FULL: Others derive miṣr from separating one thing from another. MAWARDI-FULL: A line by ʿAdi ibn Zayd uses miṣran for what separates day and night, as lexical evidence. WAHIDI-BASIT: Misr means a boundary; Adi ibn Zayd's verse makes the sun a boundary between day and night, and the word also means a house's limits or cultivated land separated from wilderness. TABRISI (بعضهم): One etymology derives Miṣr from being cut off from its surroundings by cultivation. TABRISI (آخرون): Another etymology derives Miṣr from its separation from other places. TABRISI (عدي بن زيد): Adi ibn Zayd's verse depicts the sun making Egypt a clear division between day and night, supporting the separation etymology. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (عدي): ʿAdī’s verse uses miṣran to mean a boundary or limit between day and night. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (مفضل الضبي): Al-Mufaḍḍal al-Ḍabbī explains Egypt’s name as the boundary between the eastern and western limits. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: In Hajar usage, miṣr means a place’s boundaries, as in a deed describing a house with its borders. QURTUBI-FULL: Miṣr originally means boundary; miṣr al-dār is the house's limit, and Ibn Fāris says people of Hajar used the term for property boundaries. QURTUBI-FULL (عدي): ʿAdī's line calls the sun a clear boundary dividing day from night, illustrating the root meaning. BAYDAWI-FULL: Miṣr can mean a great city; the word originally denotes a boundary between two things. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Miṣr can instead mean a boundary between two lands; Hajar writes bimuṣawrihā for its borders, and the poem depicts the sun as a boundary between day and night. SAMIN-DURR: In the basic language, المصر is the boundary between two things; people of Hajar used مصورها to mean a property's boundaries, and a poem calls the sun a boundary between day and night. ALUSI-FULL: أهبطوا مصرا is a separate reported sentence; it is unjoined because the first is declarative and the second is not, and because descending explains how they could exchange foods. ALUSI-FULL: مصر means a great town and originally denotes a boundary between two things; a quoted poetic line uses it for the boundary dividing day and night. [16 notes: MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; TABRISI(آخرون,بعضهم,عدي بن زيد); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(عدي,مفضل الضبي); QURTUBI-FULL(عدي); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q37/p2 A disputed place-name derivation — MAWARDI-FULL: Some derive miṣr from cutting off, as the city is bounded off by its built-up area. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash explains declension despite proper-name and feminine status by the word's lightness from a still middle consonant. BIQAI-FULL: Egypt's name suits its gathering of worldly needs and unusual water; even if it came from a man's name, that coincidence reflects God's knowledge and wisdom over all its details. ALUSI-FULL: Place names can be masculine when considered as places and feminine when considered as lands; if مصر is a feminine town name it takes tanwin because its middle consonant is still. [4 notes: MAWARDI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأخفش); BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q37/p3 An Arabized foreign name — KASHSHAF-FULL (قيل): One view identifies Egypt with Miṣrāyim, an Arabized form. BAYDAWI-FULL: Another account derives the name from Miṣrātim and says it was Arabized. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Some derive مصر from the Arabicized Misraim, a son of Noah who first laid out the city; Arabicization permits declension because foreignness no longer governs the form. [3 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(قيل); BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q37/p4 A name linked to drawing people — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن فارس): Some explain the name Miṣr by people’s intent toward it, like maṣartu al-shāh for milking a sheep because people flock there and rarely leave. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Miṣr means a city and may derive from milking a sheep completely, drawing everything from its udder. Against — ALUSI-FULL: Deriving the town name from milking every drop from a sheep is far-fetched. [2 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن فارس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL-]
- 2:61/q37/p5 A large settled town — BAYDAWI-FULL: Miṣr can mean a great city; the word originally denotes a boundary between two things. ALUSI-FULL: مصر means a great town and originally denotes a boundary between two things; a quoted poetic line uses it for the boundary dividing day and night. [2 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q38 How is the final rāʾ of miṣran recited?  [مِصْرًۭا · readings]
turns on: Whether tanwīn favors tafkhīm or the preceding vowel favors tarqīq at connection and pause.
- 2:61/q38/p1 Softened rāʾ — NASHR (طاهر بن غلبون): Some authorities, including Ṭāhir b. Ghalbūn, soften every tanwīn rāʾ without exception; al-Dānī read this from him. The readers exclude مصرا, إصرا, قطرا, وزرا, وقرا because of an emphatic consonant. NASHR (أبو عمرو الداني): Al-Dānī, Abū al-Fatḥ and Ibn Khāqān, Ibn Bulayma, Ibn al-Faḥḥām and al-Shāṭibī soften tanwīn rāʾ in both environments, including after silent yāʾ or adjacent kasra. NASHR (ابن سفيان): Ibn Sufyān and al-Mahdawī instead use tafkhīm in connected recitation because of tanwīn, then tarqīq at pause; this is a second face in al-Kāfī. NASHR (صاحب التبصرة): Al-Tabṣira uniquely reports a second view: a tanwīn rāʾ on the faʿīl pattern is softened at pause but emphatic in connection, attributed to Abū al-Ṭayyib. [4 notes: NASHR(أبو عمرو الداني,ابن سفيان,صاحب التبصرة,طاهر بن غلبون)]
- 2:61/q38/p2 Emphatic rāʾ — NASHR (طاهر بن غلبون): Some authorities, including Ṭāhir b. Ghalbūn, soften every tanwīn rāʾ without exception; al-Dānī read this from him. The readers exclude مصرا, إصرا, قطرا, وزرا, وقرا because of an emphatic consonant. NASHR (أبو شامة): Abū Shāma sees no difference between a fatḥa and ḍamma tanwīn rāʾ and says the ḍamma should more strongly favor tafkhīm; the author says al-Jaʿbarī adopted this and wrongly faulted al-Shāṭibī. NASHR (ابن سفيان): Ibn Sufyān and al-Mahdawī instead use tafkhīm in connected recitation because of tanwīn, then tarqīq at pause; this is a second face in al-Kāfī. NASHR (صاحب التبصرة): Al-Tabṣira uniquely reports a second view: a tanwīn rāʾ on the faʿīl pattern is softened at pause but emphatic in connection, attributed to Abū al-Ṭayyib. [4 notes: NASHR(أبو شامة,ابن سفيان,صاحب التبصرة,طاهر بن غلبون)]

## 2:61/q39 How does 'you have what you asked' connect to the command?  [فَإِنَّ لَكُم مَّا سَأَلْتُمْ · grammar]
turns on: Causal explanation, response to an imperative, or simple sequence marked by fāʾ.
- 2:61/q39/p1 The promise follows the command — MUQATIL: Moses rebuked them for exchanging the inferior produce of the earth for the better manna and quails, and told them to go down to a city where they could obtain what they requested. TAB-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya says their food was quail and their drink manna, and they asked for the produce listed in the verse. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari supplies the omitted event: Moses prayed, God answered him, and gave the Israelites what they had asked for. BAGHAWI-FULL: What they requested means the plants of the earth. TABRISI: One view says their request was no sin because they asked for another permissible food; Ṭabrisī prefers the view that it was sinful because they rejected God's choice and were reproached. RAZI-FULL: Nothing in the Quran says they were forbidden to request a different food, and the response granting what they asked for indicates the request was permissible; otherwise answering it would entail prophetic sin. QURTUBI-FULL: Third, what God gave them was more delicious than what they requested. QURTUBI-FULL: Mā is the object governed by inna in 'you have what you asked for.' ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The verb has a hamza-root pattern and a wāw-root pattern; يتساولان, analogous to يتجاوبان, supports the latter. ALUSI-FULL: The request is included in the account of blessings: God grants what they asked for by ordering them down to a town, although their ingratitude deserved wrath. IBNASHUR-FULL: The command ‘go down to a town’ is a comparable case where the intended relation is clear without inventing an implicit condition. [11 notes: MUQATIL; TAB-FULL(أبو العالية); BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q39/p2 Promise explains command — BURSEVI: The promise that they will have what they asked for explains the command to descend. ALUSI-FULL: “You will have what you asked” explains the order to descend; al-Bahr instead calls it an answer to the imperative, which can be answered by a clause, with an implied link and pronoun: “therein” and “what you asked for.” [2 notes: BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q39/p3 Fāʾ marks sequence after descent — IBNASHUR-FULL: The فاء in فإن لكم ما سألتم marks sequence: they obtain what they requested upon descending, rather than giving a reason for the command. IBNASHUR-FULL: Ibn ʿĀshūr invokes ʿAbd al-Qāhir’s point that إن can itself link and motivate a clause; adding فاء therefore signals simple coordination and sequence. [2 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q39/p4 Response to the imperative — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: فإن لكم ما سألتم answers the imperative like a jussive clause; grammarians dispute whether اهبطوا implies a conditional or has an omitted condition. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]

## 2:61/q40 Did they receive the food they asked for?  [فَإِنَّ لَكُم مَّا سَأَلْتُمْ · reports]
- 2:61/q40/p1 Receipt depends on descent — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says the Israelites had been given provisions in the wilderness but tired of them and asked for crops. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): An alternative report says they were given the food they asked for. BIQAI-FULL: In the city they will get what they requested, while manna and quail will cease. [3 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي); QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); BIQAI-FULL]

## 2:61/q41 How is sāʾaltum read and formed?  [سَأَلْتُمْ · readings]
turns on: The initial sīn vowel and a long-alif dialectal verb form.
- 2:61/q41/p1 The common form is sāʾaltum — TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says manna was honey sent from heaven and quail was their food; they knew no bread and asked for plants. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The statement فإن لكم ما سألتم implies that God left them to their own devices. TABRISI: On this reading, the speech ends with the promise that the produce they requested would be available in a settled city; the verse then begins a new account of Sabbath violators and prophet-killers. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The construction omits both a locative link, فيها, and the pronoun in سألتموه, with the conditions for ellipsis satisfied. SAMIN-DURR: ما is an accusative noun of إن, whose predicate is لكم; it means that which, with its resumptive pronoun omitted: الذي سألتموه. IBNASHUR-FULL (أبو حيان): Abū Ḥayyān reads فإن لكم ما سألتم as the answer to the command, allowing a nominal sentence to answer an imperative. [6 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو حيان)]
- 2:61/q41/p2 Kasra on initial sīn — IBNATIYYA-FULL (النخعي وابن وثاب): Al-Nakhaʿi and Ibn Waththab read سألتم with kasra on the sīn, a dialectal form. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن وثاب والنخعي): Ibn Waththāb and al-Nakhaʿī read saʾaltum with kasrah on sīn; saʾalta and salta are both used, and yatasāwalan shows the wāw root. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (إبراهيم النخعي ويحيى بن وثاب): They read سألتم with a kasra on the initial sīn, a form produced by overlap between two verb patterns for سأل. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن جني): Ibn Jinni offers another account: hamza in سألتم became yāʾ as in a cited line, then the sīn was kasrated before yāʾ and the speaker restored hamza. ALUSI-FULL (النخعي ويحيى): Al-Nakhaʿi and Yahya read سألتم with kasra on the first letter. [5 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(النخعي وابن وثاب); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن وثاب والنخعي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(إبراهيم النخعي ويحيى بن وثاب,ابن جني); ALUSI-FULL(النخعي ويحيى)]
- 2:61/q41/p3 Long-alif form — SAMIN-DURR (قرئ): A reading سلتم, like بعتم, is derived from the long-alif verb سال. SAMIN-DURR: Hassan's poem سالت هذيل رسول الله witnesses the long-alif form; the origin of its alif is disputed as yāʾ, waw, or hamza, with details deferred to 70:1. [2 notes: SAMIN-DURR(قرئ)]

## 2:61/q42 What does 'humiliation was struck upon them' express?  [وَضُرِبَتْ عَلَيْهِمُ ٱلذِّلَّةُ وَٱلْمَسْكَنَةُ · rhetoric]
turns on: The image of a tent or plaster versus the idiom of imposed obligation.
- 2:61/q42/p1 Imposes a binding condition — TAB-FULL: Ḍuribat means imposed and made binding, as when an authority imposes tribute, tax, or conscription. WAHIDI-BASIT: The expression ‘struck upon’ extends to imposing an obligation, as in a levy imposed on a servant or merchants. BAGHAWI-FULL: “Was imposed upon them” means it was made binding on them. QURTUBI-FULL (الفرزدق): Al-Farazdaq's line to Jarīr uses ḍuribat for an affliction imposed and sealed by scripture. SAMIN-DURR: ضربت means imposed or decreed upon them, by analogy with erecting tents; al-Farazdaq's poem to Jarir uses ضربت عليك. [5 notes: TAB-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; BAGHAWI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الفرزدق); SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:61/q42/p2 Surrounds like a pitched tent — SAMIN-DURR: ضربت means imposed or decreed upon them, by analogy with erecting tents; al-Farazdaq's poem to Jarir uses ضربت عليك. IBNASHUR-FULL: ضرب originally means forceful contact between surfaces; related idioms extend it to strong attachment, such as fixing a tent or house to the ground. IBNASHUR-FULL: Al-Ṭībī takes the dependent-metaphor path, while al-Taftāzānī also treats the striking as a dependent metaphor and allows tent or clay as the comparison. IBNASHUR-FULL: The verse’s tent is only a figurative comparison, whereas the tent in Ziyād’s poem may be real; the verse is an explicit metaphor and the poem can be literal with an allusion. IBNASHUR-FULL (الطيبي): Al-Ṭībī emphasizes that the metaphor in the verse differs from Ziyād’s potentially literal tent. [5 notes: SAMIN-DURR; IBNASHUR-FULL(الطيبي)]
- 2:61/q42/p3 The metaphor fixes the condition — WAHIDI-BASIT: The phrase ‘a lasting blow’ describes something enduring, and al-Nābigha uses it of evil that people do not think temporary. IBNASHUR-FULL: Verses by ʿAbda b. al-Ṭabīb and Ziyād al-Aʿjam attest ضرب بيتا and قبة ضربت for firmly setting up a dwelling. [2 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q42/p4 Adheres like plaster on a wall — IBNASHUR-FULL: Al-Ṭībī takes the dependent-metaphor path, while al-Taftāzānī also treats the striking as a dependent metaphor and allows tent or clay as the comparison. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:61/q43 When and on whom was the humiliation imposed?  [وَضُرِبَتْ عَلَيْهِمُ ٱلذِّلَّةُ وَٱلْمَسْكَنَةُ · referent]
turns on: Whether the condition began in Moses' era, concerns later Jews, or applies to a subset.
- 2:61/q43/p1 Applies to later Jews — WAHIDI-BASIT (جماعة من المفسرين): One reported interpretation takes the humiliation as referring to the people living in Moses’ time; another takes it as referring to people in the Prophet’s time. WAHIDI-BASIT (الكلبي): Many exegetes, including al-Kalbī, say humiliation and poverty began then as signs of hardship because God knew they would kill prophets, and their descendants inherited them. WAHIDI-BASIT: This points to a later application: before Islam they paid no jizya and were not marked by distinctive clothing. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: The humiliation applies to Jews in the Prophet's era: it means the jizya and Jewish dress, imposed so they cannot escape; poverty is shown by its garb and visible misery. BAYDAWI-FULL: Jews are generally humiliated and poor, either actually or by affectation for fear of increased jizya. ALUSI-FULL: Their humiliation is retribution for ingratitude toward the blessing, linking this punishment to the preceding account; the third-person pronoun includes all Jews through later generations and is not an instance of iltifat. [6 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الكلبي,جماعة من المفسرين); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q43/p2 The condition spans generations — NASAFI-FULL: Humiliation and poverty surround and cling to them; Jews are subdued and poor either literally or by displaying poverty out of fear of an increased poll tax. IBNASHUR-FULL: The humiliation clause is a result of the earlier blessings of liberation and a land to inhabit, which they failed to value when they yearned for Egypt and shrank from entering the town. IBNASHUR-FULL: Their humiliation means they lost courage and displayed need despite abundant blessings; weariness made those blessings seem worthless, and greed persisted in their descendants. [3 notes: NASAFI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q43/p3 Began in Moses' era — WAHIDI-BASIT (جماعة من المفسرين): One reported interpretation takes the humiliation as referring to the people living in Moses’ time; another takes it as referring to people in the Prophet’s time. WAHIDI-BASIT (الكلبي): Many exegetes, including al-Kalbī, say humiliation and poverty began then as signs of hardship because God knew they would kill prophets, and their descendants inherited them. [2 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الكلبي,جماعة من المفسرين)]

## 2:61/q44 How is ʿalayhim pronounced and explained?  [عَلَيْهِمُ · readings]
turns on: The transmitted hāʾ and mīm vowels and competing original-vowel or harmony analyses.
- 2:61/q44/p1 The pronoun follows transmitted dialects — FARISI-HUJJA: Vowel-following is not always productive analogy: some forms are transmitted, while it is regular in رد، عض، فر and apparently in مردفين. FARISI-HUJJA: Following kasra is already accepted on the mīm of an active-participle marker even though it obscures the pattern, making it still likelier on a pronoun marker. FARISI-HUJJA: Arabic allows vowel-following across an intervening consonant, as in أجوءك for أجيئك and منتن. FARISI-HUJJA: In منحدر, the ḍamma follows the inflectional ḍamma, as in ابنم، امرؤ، أخوك، فوك، and ذو مال. FARISI-HUJJA: أنبؤك can follow the inflectional ḍamma or the initial imperfect-prefix ḍamma, even across an intervening consonant. FARISI-HUJJA (عبد الله بن أبي إسحاق): Abū ʿUthmān reports through Maḥbūb and ʿĪsā that Ibn Abī Isḥāq read بين المرء وقلبه and said رأيت مرءا وهذا مرء. FARISI-HUJJA: The exceptional pronunciation هو يئبى does not refute the pattern because it is anomalous; يخطف has kasra to favor vowel-following. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو الحسن): Abū al-Ḥasan explains يخطف by first kasrating خ for two sukūns, then matching the prefix yāʾ to that kasra. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو الحسن): Abū al-Ḥasan reports from ʿĪsā ibn ʿUmar that some Arabs give three-letter nouns beginning with ḍamma a heavy pronunciation. FARISI-HUJJA: Forms of فم show case-conditioned endings (فوك، فاك، فيك); ذو مال behaves similarly but ذو does not take a pronominal suffix. FARISI-HUJJA: With a first-person suffix, فم ends in yāʾ in all cases because the following pronoun imposes kasra, unlike عصاي whose weak ending does not shift with case. FARISI-HUJJA: Following works alike when the triggering vowel is inflectional or built; the distinction does not change the pattern, as ابنم shows. FARISI-HUJJA: The proposal that ابنم’s nūn follows an added mīm is unsound: امرؤ shows the same following without deletion, and مرء is also attested. FARISI-HUJJA: The contrasting forms غد and غدو show that فم’s alternating deletion and completion is not a general consequence of matching وزن. FARISI-HUJJA: Vowel-following has two types: a non-inflectional vowel follows another non-inflectional vowel, as in مغيرة، منتن، يعفر، and ظلمات. FARISI-HUJJA: In the second type, a non-inflectional vowel follows an inflectional one, as in امرؤ، ابنم، فوك، أجوءك، and أنبؤك. FARISI-HUJJA: The examples of فم undermine the claim that these words are inflected in two places; movement can follow either inflection or construction. FARISI-HUJJA: The reversed pattern cited in the grammar book is too rare to establish a rule; comparison of inflection with building is likewise only an exceptional analogy. FARISI-HUJJA: The same principle explains ذال in مذ اليوم: its original ḍamma is retained when the consonant is moved. FARISI-HUJJA: Other readings likewise take more than one dialect form, including variants in أنعام، تكملوا، and يألتكم. FARISI-HUJJA (النبي): A transmitted prophetic report says the Qurʾān was revealed in seven modes, all healing and sufficient. FARISI-HUJJA (نافع): Nāfiʿ is reported to alternate between عليهمو and عليهم, another instance of using both dialect forms. FARISI-HUJJA: The objection that transitional movement should not trigger ḍamma on hāʾ only challenges the reading under a different analysis; it does not touch his explanation. FARISI-HUJJA: Weak radicals remain deleted in forms such as رمت المرأة and لم يخف الرجل despite a vowel inserted for two sukūns; if that vowel counted, forms like لم يقولا would retain the radical. FARISI-HUJJA: The poetic restorations أجره الرمح and ولا تهاله are too rare to outweigh the ordinary evidence against counting the transitional vowel. FARISI-HUJJA: The retained yāʾ in لم يكن الذين كفروا is a stronger counterexample because it is frequent and occurs in revelation. FARISI-HUJJA: Poetic deletion can restore an underlying form for metrical necessity, as ضننوا, although ordinary usage keeps the consonant. FARISI-HUJJA: In اضرب الاثنين and اكتب الاسم, the imperative lām remains kasrated because movement of the article lām for two sukūns counts like its being still. FARISI-HUJJA: Although the article lām attaches closely and adds definiteness, it behaves like a separate element because it is not part of the word’s root, as in hesitation forms ألي. FARISI-HUJJA: Jussive حذف of nūn in لم يضربا and لم يضربوا shows that transitional movement is not counted like a stable vowel. FARISI-HUJJA: Identical consonants do not assimilate when the second is moved only for two sukūns, another sign that this movement is disregarded. FARISI-HUJJA: Ordinary ḍamma on wāw can permit its replacement by hamza, as in أقتت، أدؤر، and النئوور; the next argument tests transitional ḍamma. FARISI-HUJJA: Wāw is not replaced by hamza when its ḍamma is only transitional; reports that some do so are treated as mistakes. FARISI-HUJJA: A non-obligatory vowel behaves like a transitional one: in رمتا and غزتا, the weak radical stays deleted as in رمت المرأة. FARISI-HUJJA: For the poetic form لها متنتان خظاتا, al-Fārisī prefers the account of metrical restoration over deleting the dual nūn, since such deletion occurs with relatives. NASAFI-FULL (الكسائي): Al-Kisa'i is reported to read the following word as حفوا. NASHR: The passage notes a disagreement over sukūn versus ḍamma on ʿayn in a catalogue of words, but this excerpt supplies no reader assignments. NASHR: The author points back to the earlier discussion of reading the hāʾ and mīm in عليهم الذلة with either ḍamma or kasra. NASHR (نافع): Nāfiʿ’s hamza treatment of النبياء, النبيئين, النبيء and النبوة is referred to the separate hamza chapter. QIRAAT-ER: The ḍamma on hāʾ is a canonical vowel-harmony variant of the kasra form and does not alter meaning. QIRAAT-ER: The kasra on mīm is a canonical case-vowel variant of the ḍamma form, varying in pause or connection without changing meaning. [41 notes: FARISI-HUJJA(أبو الحسن,النبي,عبد الله بن أبي إسحاق,نافع); NASAFI-FULL(الكسائي); NASHR(نافع); QIRAAT-ER; FARISI-HUJJA+]
- 2:61/q44/p2 Kasra on hāʾ and mīm — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The kasra reading of both hāʾ and mīm in عليهم reflects the hāʾ's proximity to yāʾ and the need to break the meeting of two sukūns. FARISI-HUJJA: Abu ʿAmr kasras the pronoun mīm because its underlying connected form ended in yāʾ; after deletion, the mīm resumes that original vowel when moved. FARISI-HUJJA: He treats Abu ʿAmr’s kasra like the original ḍamma of Ibn Kathīr, Nāfiʿ, ʿĀṣim, and Ibn ʿĀmir, preferring an original vowel to an added one. FARISI-HUJJA (سيبويه): Sībawayh’s vocative truncation of Rād retains its original vowel at a consonant meeting, an analogy al-Fārisī uses for Abu ʿAmr. NASAFI-FULL (حمزة وعلى): Hamzah and al-Kisa'i have a distinct vocalization of the pronoun in عليهم; Abu Amr kasras both hā and mīm, while the other readers kasra the hā and damma the mīm. NASAFI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abu Amr reads the pronoun with kasra on both hā and mīm; the remaining readers kasra hā and damma mīm. [6 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA(سيبويه); NASAFI-FULL(أبو عمرو,حمزة وعلى)]
- 2:61/q44/p3 Ḍamma on hāʾ and mīm — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: Al-Kisāʾī matches Ḥamza here but differs in أنعمت عليهم: there mīm stays still, avoiding a shift from yāʾ to ḍamma; here hāʾ returns to its original vowel before mīm is moved. FARISI-HUJJA (حمزة): Ḥamza moves the mīm in عليهم، لديهم، and إليهم with ḍamma, matching the pronoun hāʾ, which he also pronounces with ḍamma. FARISI-HUJJA: Because Ḥamza gives hāʾ its underlying ḍamma, the mīm takes that original vowel rather than an added kasra; no reader with hāʾ-ḍamma kasras this mīm. FARISI-HUJJA (الكسائي): Al-Kisāʾī joins Ḥamza in ḍamma on عليهم and لديهم, though they normally kasra this hāʾ outside the relevant environments. FARISI-HUJJA: Al-Kisāʾī and Ḥamza use two dialect forms, a choice that broadens and eases recitation and accords with the transmitted seven modes. [5 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA(الكسائي,حمزة)]
- 2:61/q44/p4 Kasra on hāʾ, ḍamma on mīm — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: A kasra on hāʾ with ḍamma on mīm combines yāʾ proximity with the mīm's original ḍamma, needed to avoid two sukūns. [1 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA]

## 2:61/q45 What is the imposed dhilla?  [ٱلذِّلَّةُ · interpretation]
turns on: General humiliation, jizya, distinctive dress, or another levy.
- 2:61/q45/p1 Abasement and humiliation — MAJAZ: Al-dhilla means abasement or lowliness. ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة والحسن): The humiliation imposed on them means that they pay the poll tax by hand while subdued. TAB-FULL: Al-dhilla is abasement and humiliation, the state of subjection imposed on them. TAB-FULL: The abasement is linked to the rule that protection under continued unbelief is withheld unless the People of the Book pay jizya in submission. TAB-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): They explain the imposed humiliation as paying the jizya while subdued. ZAJJAJ: “Dhilla” is abasement and “maskana” is submission or humility; the latter derives from stillness, since poverty reduces a poor person’s movement. IBNABIHATIM (الحسن، قتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada explain abasement as paying the jizya while subdued. IBNABIHATIM (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak glosses abasement as humiliation. RUMMANI: The metaphor of striking humiliation upon them makes its imposition perceptible and suggests degradation, intensifying the rebuke and aversion from their condition. THALABI: Ḍuribat means “was imposed” or “made binding”; al-dhilla is abasement and humiliation, explained as the jizya, with a supporting verse. THALABI: Miskana means possessing poverty; people may look poor while wealthy. It can also mean humiliation and poverty of heart; miskīn derives from stillness and few movements. MAWARDI-FULL: Al-dhilla can mean humiliation and abasement, or the imposition of jizya, the latter according to al-Hasan and Qatada. WAHIDI-BASIT: ‘They were struck with humiliation’ means it was imposed on them so firmly that it did not leave them; the image is of one thing affixed to another, like a nail driven in. WAHIDI-BASIT (الحسن وقتادة): They interpret the humiliation as paying the jizya while subdued. WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو عبيد عن الكسائي): ذلة means abasement: a docile horse is the opposite of a difficult one, while a man is described as ذليل or ذلة. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأنباري): He favors the interpretation that humiliation followed their descent into Egypt: the omitted connective sense is that they descended, acted corruptly, and were then struck with humiliation. WAHIDI-BASIT (عطاء بن السائب): He identifies the humiliation with the Jewish distinguishing badge and clothing, and poverty with a poor appearance; even a wealthy Jew appeared impoverished for fear of increased jizya. WAHIDI-BASIT: The apparent objection that wealthy Jews cannot be poor is answered by taking humiliation as jizya and poverty as inward poverty of heart and soul, whose effects need not show outwardly. WAHIDI-BASIT: ‘That’ points back to the humiliation, poverty, and wrath just mentioned. JISHUMI: The promised provision is the plant food they had asked for; being struck with humiliation means being bound to disgrace, or being charged jizya, as in the tribute verse. BAGHAWI-FULL: One view interprets humiliation as the jizya tax. BAGHAWI-FULL (عطاء بن السائب): ʿAta ibn al-Saʾib explains humiliation as the Jewish kistīj, zunnār, and distinctive clothing. BAGHAWI-FULL: Even wealthy Jews appear poor, and one view identifies humiliation as poverty of the heart; the Jews are described as especially abject and greedy for wealth among religious communities. KASHSHAF-FULL: Humiliation surrounds them like a tent, or sticks to them like plaster; Jews are lowly and destitute either in reality or by feigning poverty to avoid a higher poll tax. IBNATIYYA-FULL: ذلك points to the imposition of humiliation and what follows; the bāʾ in بأنهم marks cause. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The humiliation and poverty were imposed and decreed for them; ضرب is used in idioms for imposing or binding an obligation, and the verbal noun is annexed semantically to its object. TABRISI: Ḍuribat ʿalayhim al-dhilla means humiliation was imposed on them, by analogy with a ruler imposing a tax. TABRISI (قوم): Another explanation says they settled in the abode of humiliation, using the idiom of pitching tents. TABRISI (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada interpret the humiliation as the subjection expressed in paying jizya under Quran 9:29. TABRISI (عطاء): Ata says the humiliation refers to the Jewish kistīj, a distinctive belt and dress. TABRISI (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says God exchanged the Jews' honor for humiliation, prosperity for misery, and approval for anger, as requital for disbelief and unjust killing of prophets. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Being struck with humiliation means being made to bear it. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الحسن): Al-Hasan interprets the imposed humiliation as the jizya tax. RAZI-FULL: Razi concludes that the request was permissible, so the humiliation and divine anger were causedby the subsequent disbelief and killing of prophets, not by asking for other food. RAZI-FULL: Humiliation was made to surround and adhere to them, like someone inside a tent or clay stuck to a wall; the more likely meaning is deserved humiliation. RAZI-FULL: The view that humiliation means specifically the jizya is unlikely because jizya was not imposed on them from the beginning. RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): The Muʿtazilites argue that if God creates disbelief just as He creates humiliation and poverty, there is no reason to make one the recompense for the other rather than the reverse. QURTUBI-FULL: Ḍuribat ʿalayhim means humiliation and poverty were imposed and decreed, an image compared to pitching tents; the verb can mean a judge imposed a penalty. QURTUBI-FULL: Al-Qurṭubī glosses dhillah as abasement and maskanah as poverty, claiming that even a rich Jew displays poverty's dress, submission, and humiliation. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbaydah glosses dhillah as lowliness; maskanah is the verbal noun of miskīn. QURTUBI-FULL (الضحاك بن مزاحم عن ابن عباس): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's explanation of the people struck by humiliation and poverty as holders of tax-farming contracts. BAYDAWI-FULL: Humiliation and poverty enveloped or clung to them like a canopy or plaster on a wall, as requital for ingratitude. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: ذلك may point to the punishment of wrath, or to the blow of humiliation and poverty. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The first causal clause explains humiliation and wrath; the second points to disbelief and killing, mapping the two punishments to those two causes as a balanced luff-and-nashr and avoiding redundancy. SAMIN-DURR: الذلة means humiliation; ذل with ḍamma is subjection imposed by coercion, while ذل with kasra follows resistance without coercion, as al-Raghib says. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Humiliation and poverty were imposed on them by divine decree and law; they remain subdued and are humiliated by whoever gains power over them. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada explain the imposed humiliation as paying jizya by hand while subdued. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak glosses dhilla as abasement. BIQAI-FULL: Humiliation is pictured as a covering that surrounds Israel on every side, and the word denotes inward smallness under domination. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas identifies those struck with humiliation and poverty as the people who pay jizya. BURSEVI: Their humiliation and poverty are retribution for ingratitude; the image is like plastering clay onto a wall, and even wealthy Jews seem poor. BURSEVI: Dhilla means humiliation and maskana means poverty; the poor are called miskīn because poverty stills them and impedes movement. BURSEVI: Humiliation and poverty surround and cling to them like a dome, becoming an inescapable covering. ALUSI-FULL: The image of humiliation being laid over them evokes either a tent or dome set over people or clay plastered onto a wall; the metaphor conveys encompassing, adherence, and humiliation. ELMALILI: Their humiliation and misery are pictured as a structure built or a stamp set upon them, leaving them under contempt, heavy taxes, poverty, and destitution. IBNASHUR-FULL: The phrase may be a concealed metaphor: humiliation and poverty are pictured as a tent or house fixed around them, with ضرب as an imaginative metaphor. IBNASHUR-FULL: Another analysis makes ضربت a dependent metaphor for humiliation adhering like clay to a wall, without a concealed metaphor. IBNASHUR-FULL (التفتزاني): Al-Taftāzānī reads either metaphorical analysis as an allusion to the Jews’ humiliation; Ibn ʿĀshūr says these rhetorical insights can coexist. IBNASHUR-FULL: ذلة means disgrace or abasement, is pronounced with kasra only, and opposes honor; al-Samawʾal or al-Ḥārithī contrasts the two in a verse. IBNASHUR-FULL: The باء is causal: disbelief and the related acts caused worldly humiliation and poverty and God’s anger, warning against repeating them. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The denunciation is stronger in Āl ʿImrān: it universalizes humiliation with “wherever they are found” and repeats the verb and preposition before poverty; al-Baqara simply joins humiliation and poverty. [61 notes: MAJAZ; ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة والحسن); TAB-FULL(الحسن وقتادة); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(الحسن، قتادة,الضحاك); RUMMANI; THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو عبيد عن الكسائي,الحسن وقتادة,عطاء بن السائب); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(عطاء بن السائب); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI(ابن زيد,الحسن وقتادة,عطاء,قوم); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الحسن); RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عبيدة,الضحاك بن مزاحم عن ابن عباس); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL(الحسن وقتادة,الضحاك); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(التفتزاني); SAMARRAI-ASRAR; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; RAZI-FULL+; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأنباري)+]
- 2:61/q45/p2 Subjection through jizya — ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة والحسن): The humiliation imposed on them means that they pay the poll tax by hand while subdued. TAB-FULL: The abasement is linked to the rule that protection under continued unbelief is withheld unless the People of the Book pay jizya in submission. TAB-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): They explain the imposed humiliation as paying the jizya while subdued. IBNABIHATIM (الحسن، قتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada explain abasement as paying the jizya while subdued. IBNABIHATIM (الحسن): Al-Hasan says this ummah overtook them, and even the Magians collected jizya from them. THALABI: Ḍuribat means “was imposed” or “made binding”; al-dhilla is abasement and humiliation, explained as the jizya, with a supporting verse. MAWARDI-FULL: Al-dhilla can mean humiliation and abasement, or the imposition of jizya, the latter according to al-Hasan and Qatada. WAHIDI-BASIT (الحسن وقتادة): They interpret the humiliation as paying the jizya while subdued. WAHIDI-BASIT (عطاء بن السائب): He identifies the humiliation with the Jewish distinguishing badge and clothing, and poverty with a poor appearance; even a wealthy Jew appeared impoverished for fear of increased jizya. WAHIDI-BASIT: The apparent objection that wealthy Jews cannot be poor is answered by taking humiliation as jizya and poverty as inward poverty of heart and soul, whose effects need not show outwardly. JISHUMI: The promised provision is the plant food they had asked for; being struck with humiliation means being bound to disgrace, or being charged jizya, as in the tribute verse. BAGHAWI-FULL: One view interprets humiliation as the jizya tax. KASHSHAF-FULL: Humiliation surrounds them like a tent, or sticks to them like plaster; Jews are lowly and destitute either in reality or by feigning poverty to avoid a higher poll tax. TABRISI (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada interpret the humiliation as the subjection expressed in paying jizya under Quran 9:29. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الحسن): Al-Hasan interprets the imposed humiliation as the jizya tax. Against — RAZI-FULL: The view that humiliation means specifically the jizya is unlikely because jizya was not imposed on them from the beginning. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Ḥasan and Qatādah explain dhillah as the obligation of paying jizyah. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak transmits from Ibn Abbas that the people described are holders of jizya obligations. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada explain the imposed humiliation as paying jizya by hand while subdued. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says God left them without protection and beneath Muslims' feet; this community encountered them while Magians collected jizya from them. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas identifies those struck with humiliation and poverty as the people who pay jizya. DURR-FULL (قتادة والحسن): Qatada and Hasan say they pay the jizya by hand while subdued. [21 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة والحسن); TAB-FULL(الحسن وقتادة); IBNABIHATIM(الحسن,الحسن، قتادة); THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(الحسن وقتادة,عطاء بن السائب); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(الحسن وقتادة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الحسن); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن وقتادة); IBNKATHIR-FULL(الحسن,الحسن وقتادة,الضحاك); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,قتادة والحسن); RAZI-FULL-]
- 2:61/q45/p3 A tax or levy — ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة والحسن): The humiliation imposed on them means that they pay the poll tax by hand while subdued. THALABI (unidentified speaker): An unidentified gloss identifies the term with al-kastibinj, a Jewish weight. BAGHAWI-FULL: One view interprets humiliation as the jizya tax. KASHSHAF-FULL: Humiliation surrounds them like a tent, or sticks to them like plaster; Jews are lowly and destitute either in reality or by feigning poverty to avoid a higher poll tax. TABRISI: Ḍuribat ʿalayhim al-dhilla means humiliation was imposed on them, by analogy with a ruler imposing a tax. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الحسن): Al-Hasan interprets the imposed humiliation as the jizya tax. QURTUBI-FULL (الضحاك بن مزاحم عن ابن عباس): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's explanation of the people struck by humiliation and poverty as holders of tax-farming contracts. ELMALILI: Their humiliation and misery are pictured as a structure built or a stamp set upon them, leaving them under contempt, heavy taxes, poverty, and destitution. [8 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة والحسن); THALABI(unidentified speaker); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الحسن); QURTUBI-FULL(الضحاك بن مزاحم عن ابن عباس); ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q45/p4 An imposed low condition — IBNABIHATIM (الحسن): Al-Hasan says God humiliated them, left them without protection, and placed them under the Muslims' feet. IBNATIYYA-FULL: ذلة is a state or condition derived from ذل. TABRISI: Dhilla derives from the verb meaning to be humbled or low. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ treats dhilla and dhull as synonyms. SAMIN-DURR: ضربت is passive, with الذلة as its نائب الفاعل. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali reads the conjunction as signaling many omitted intervening events: Israel was placed where it had lowered itself, and enemies then subdued and humiliated those who remained. [6 notes: IBNABIHATIM(الحسن); IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 2:61/q45/p5 Distinctive Jewish clothing — WAHIDI-BASIT (عطاء بن السائب): He identifies the humiliation with the Jewish distinguishing badge and clothing, and poverty with a poor appearance; even a wealthy Jew appeared impoverished for fear of increased jizya. BAGHAWI-FULL (عطاء بن السائب): ʿAta ibn al-Saʾib explains humiliation as the Jewish kistīj, zunnār, and distinctive clothing. TABRISI (عطاء): Ata says the humiliation refers to the Jewish kistīj, a distinctive belt and dress. QURTUBI-FULL: Al-Qurṭubī glosses dhillah as abasement and maskanah as poverty, claiming that even a rich Jew displays poverty's dress, submission, and humiliation. [4 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(عطاء بن السائب); BAGHAWI-FULL(عطاء بن السائب); TABRISI(عطاء); QURTUBI-FULL]

## 2:61/q46 What is the imposed maskana?  [وَٱلْمَسْكَنَةُ · interpretation]
turns on: Material poverty, outward appearance, inward need, submission, or levy.
- 2:61/q46/p1 Material poverty or need — MAJAZ: Al-maskana is the verbal noun of miskīn; an example calls the poorer of two men the more miskīn. TAB-FULL: Here al-maskana means the humility and abasement that accompany want and poverty. TAB-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya glosses al-maskana as need or destitution. TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi glosses the imposed maskana as poverty. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas identifies those under abasement and poverty as people subject to levies; the author clarifies that these are jizya payers. IBNABIHATIM (أبو العالية): Al-Aliyah explains poverty as indigence; al-Suddi and al-Rabi' gave the same explanation. IBNABIHATIM (عطية): Atiyya interprets poverty as a tax or land levy. IBNABIHATIM (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak explains poverty as the jizya. MAWARDI-FULL (أبي العالية): Maskana may mean need, according to Abu al-ʿAliya, or poverty, according to al-Suddi. WAHIDI-BASIT (الليث): He derives مسكنة from سکون and defines it as the verbal noun of the poor person; its verb is تمسكن, ‘became poor.’ BAGHAWI-FULL: “Maskanah” means poverty; a poor person is so named because poverty stills and immobilizes him. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Al-Zajjaj derives maskana from stillness and says it is the appearance of poverty and subjection, which even a wealthy Jew retains. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya interprets maskana as poverty and need. TABRISI: Maskana derives from miskīn; here it means the humility and lowliness of poverty and need, with forms tamaskana and taskana also used. TABRISI: Maskana means the appearance of poverty; even their wealthy people feign destitution lest the tax be doubled. TABRISI (قوم): Some argue this verse shows wealth's merit because it condemns poverty. TABRISI: Ṭabrisī says that is not the right interpretation: the blame is for poverty of heart, since Jews can be materially wealthy yet spiritually needy; the Prophet says true wealth is of the soul. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو العالية، السدي، أبو عبيدة): One interpretation of maskana is poverty and destitution. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي): Al-Suddī also transmits an interpretation of maskana as poverty of the soul. RAZI-FULL: Al-maskana means poverty, need, and intensified hardship; this kind of suffering can function as punishment. QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj takes maskanah as submissiveness, deriving it from stillness because poverty reduces movement. NASAFI-FULL: They may be poor in fact or display poverty and humility from fear that the jizya will be doubled. SAMIN-DURR: مسكنة is on the pattern مفعلة from stillness, since poverty leaves a person with little movement; مسكين follows مفعيل. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliyah, al-Rabi ibn Anas, and al-Suddi interpret miskinah as poverty. DURR-FULL (أبو العالية): Abu al-Aliya glosses miskena as poverty. ALUSI-FULL: The image alludes to their poll tax paid in submission, distinctive clothing that identifies them as Jews, ingrained miserliness and inner poverty, and their habit of displaying hardship to avoid increased tax. IBNASHUR-FULL: مسكنة means poverty, from stillness because poverty reduces a person’s movement; it also means weakness, and مسكين denotes a poor person. [27 notes: MAJAZ; TAB-FULL(أبو العالية,السدي); IBNABIHATIM(أبو العالية,ابن عباس,الضحاك,عطية); MAWARDI-FULL(أبي العالية); WAHIDI-BASIT(الليث); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو العالية,الزجاج); TABRISI(قوم); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو العالية، السدي، أبو عبيدة,السدي); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج); NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو العالية); DURR-FULL(أبو العالية); ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q46/p2 Submissiveness or lowliness — TAB-FULL: Here al-maskana means the humility and abasement that accompany want and poverty. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Al-Zajjaj derives maskana from stillness and says it is the appearance of poverty and subjection, which even a wealthy Jew retains. TABRISI: Maskana derives from miskīn; here it means the humility and lowliness of poverty and need, with forms tamaskana and taskana also used. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Another interpretation of maskana is submissiveness. QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj takes maskanah as submissiveness, deriving it from stillness because poverty reduces movement. NASAFI-FULL: They may be poor in fact or display poverty and humility from fear that the jizya will be doubled. SAMIN-DURR: مسكنة is on the pattern مفعلة from stillness, since poverty leaves a person with little movement; مسكين follows مفعيل. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines maskanah as outwardly visible lowliness or submission, shown in stillness and a slackening of movement. ALUSI-FULL: The image alludes to their poll tax paid in submission, distinctive clothing that identifies them as Jews, ingrained miserliness and inner poverty, and their habit of displaying hardship to avoid increased tax. IBNASHUR-FULL: مسكنة means poverty, from stillness because poverty reduces a person’s movement; it also means weakness, and مسكين denotes a poor person. [10 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الزجاج); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج); QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج); NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q46/p3 A tax or land levy — IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas identifies those under abasement and poverty as people subject to levies; the author clarifies that these are jizya payers. IBNABIHATIM (عطية): Atiyya interprets poverty as a tax or land levy. IBNABIHATIM (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak explains poverty as the jizya. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada interpret maskana as the poll tax, jizya. TABRISI: Maskana means the appearance of poverty; even their wealthy people feign destitution lest the tax be doubled. NASAFI-FULL: They may be poor in fact or display poverty and humility from fear that the jizya will be doubled. IBNKATHIR-FULL (عطية العوفي): Atiyya al-Awfi interprets miskinah as a tax or land levy. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak also glosses miskinah as jizya. ALUSI-FULL: The image alludes to their poll tax paid in submission, distinctive clothing that identifies them as Jews, ingrained miserliness and inner poverty, and their habit of displaying hardship to avoid increased tax. [9 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,الضحاك,عطية); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الحسن وقتادة); TABRISI; NASAFI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(الضحاك,عطية العوفي); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q46/p4 A poverty-related condition — TAB-FULL: Al-maskana is the verbal noun of miskīn; Arabic also uses forms such as tamaskana and tamaskunan. NAHHAS: ما is accusative as the noun of فإن لكم ما سألتم; الذلة is the passive subject of ضربت, and المسكنة is coordinated with it. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأنباري): He explains مسكنة as conditions that still and restrain a person; مسكين was then understood as if its initial mīm belonged to the root, by analogy with other formations. SAMIN-DURR (الراغب): Al-Raghib says the mīm in مسكنة is extra in the stronger view; citing the dispute also suggests the nūn is extra and the root is مسك. SAMIN-DURR: The mīm recurs in تمسكن and متمسكن, but this does not make it a root letter: derivation shows it is extra, as with تمندل and تمذرع. [5 notes: TAB-FULL; NAHHAS; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأنباري); SAMIN-DURR(الراغب)]
- 2:61/q46/p5 Outward appearance of poverty — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Al-Zajjaj derives maskana from stillness and says it is the appearance of poverty and subjection, which even a wealthy Jew retains. TABRISI: Maskana means the appearance of poverty; even their wealthy people feign destitution lest the tax be doubled. NASAFI-FULL: They may be poor in fact or display poverty and humility from fear that the jizya will be doubled. ALUSI-FULL: The image alludes to their poll tax paid in submission, distinctive clothing that identifies them as Jews, ingrained miserliness and inner poverty, and their habit of displaying hardship to avoid increased tax. [4 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الزجاج); TABRISI; NASAFI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q46/p6 Poverty of heart — TABRISI: Ṭabrisī says that is not the right interpretation: the blame is for poverty of heart, since Jews can be materially wealthy yet spiritually needy; the Prophet says true wealth is of the soul. TABRISI (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet is reported to say that true wealth is wealth of the soul. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي): Al-Suddī also transmits an interpretation of maskana as poverty of the soul. [3 notes: TABRISI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(السدي)]

## 2:61/q47 What does bāʾū bi-ghaḍab mean?  [وَبَآءُو بِغَضَبٍۢ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ · meaning]
turns on: Return, bearing, deserving, settling, equivalence, and acknowledgment idioms.
- 2:61/q47/p1 Return bearing wrath — AKHFASH: باءوا بغضب means they returned bearing it or that it came upon them; the verb is also used in باء بذنبه. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Bāʾū means they returned; the verb is used as bǔtu bi-kadhā and abūʾu bihi, not bāʾa bi-l-shayʾ. TAB-FULL: Bāʾū means they turned back bearing something; the verb is joined to either good or evil, as in the verse about bearing another’s sin. TAB-FULL: They returned carrying God’s anger, which had settled on them and made them liable to His displeasure. THALABI (الكسائي وغيره): They gloss bawāʾū bi-ghaḍab as “returned.” MAWARDI-FULL (الكسائي): Al-Kisaʾi says bāʾū means they returned with God's wrath; return must be accompanied by something good or evil. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): He glosses باءوا بغضب as ‘they returned.’ WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأنباري): He cites the report ‘Ṭalḥa returned with Paradise’ to illustrate باء as returning with something. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: To incur or return bearing something is the meaning of باؤوا; they incurred God's anger because they disbelieved signs sent to Muhammad. BAGHAWI-FULL: “Bāʾū” means they returned, and the expression is used only of an evil thing. TABRISI: They returned bearing God's anger, which was due upon them and had overtaken them. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Bāʾū means they returned. RAZI-FULL: The word bāʾū can mean they returned, and it is only used with evil; al-Zajjaj gives another view that it means God's anger was made equal to them. QURTUBI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned and remained bound to the consequence; the root means return, including the prayer acknowledging and binding oneself to God's blessing. QURTUBI-FULL: Bawāʾ can mean returning retribution for blood or equal status in a matter, as poetry also describes return with captives and spoils.
