BAYDAWI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned carrying anger or became deserving of God's anger; the idiom means someone is liable to be killed for another, and its root connotes equality. NASAFI-FULL: To incur God's anger means to become deserving of it, by analogy with one who is liable to be killed in return for an equal victim. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If باء means return, its preposition marks accompanying state; if it means deserve, the preposition completes the verb; if it means settle or become equal, it is locative, with different attachment analyses. SAMIN-DURR: البواء can mean return in retaliation, and people may be بواء, equal in a matter; the poem لا يبوء الدم بالدم means blood is not repaid with blood in retribution. SAMIN-DURR: بغضب is a circumstantial phrase describing those who returned: they returned under wrath, not with wrath as a direct object. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains bāʾū as returning while carrying something; here they returned burdened with God's anger, paralleling 5:29. BIQAI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned, and they deserved the anger; the phrase can mean being liable to be killed in equivalence for another's killing. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali glosses their return with anger as a powerful ruler's resolve to exact vengeance for defiance. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada glosses ba'u as turning back or returning. BURSEVI: To “return bearing” God’s anger means they deserved and bound themselves to it; the Prophet’s wording “I acknowledge” illustrates the verb. ALUSI-FULL: باءوا may mean they settled into worldly trials, afterlife punishment, or hardships in both worlds; it may also mean that wrath came upon them, so no object of “return” is needed. IBNASHUR-FULL: باءوا means return, metaphorically describing a reversal from a state pleasing to God into His anger. [27 notes: AKHFASH; IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL; THALABI(الكسائي وغيره); MAWARDI-FULL(الكسائي); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأنباري,الفراء); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(قتادة); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:61/q47/p2 Bear or incur wrath — MUQATIL: Humiliation imposed on the Jews means the jizya, and neediness means poverty; they incurred God's anger. MAJAZ: Bāʾū bi-ghaḍab means that they bore or took on the anger. AKHFASH: باءوا بغضب means they returned bearing it or that it came upon them; the verb is also used in باء بذنبه. TAB-FULL: Bāʾū means they turned back bearing something; the verb is joined to either good or evil, as in the verse about bearing another’s sin. TAB-FULL: They returned carrying God’s anger, which had settled on them and made them liable to His displeasure. ZAJJAJ: The expression “bāʾū bi-ghaḍab” means that they bore or incurred the wrath. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr explains incurring God's anger as deserving His displeasure. WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو عبيدة): He explains باؤوا بغضب as bearing it; al-Zajjāj gives the same lexical gloss for the related wording in 2:90 and for 5:29. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): He derives the root from equality: people are بواء when equal, a spear is aligned toward a horseman, and bloods are equal; bearing a sin is carrying it evenly. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: To incur or return bearing something is the meaning of باؤوا; they incurred God's anger because they disbelieved signs sent to Muhammad. BAGHAWI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu ʿUbayda says “bāʾū” means they bore and acknowledged the wrath, as in the prayer “I acknowledge Your favor and my sin.” KASHSHAF-FULL: To incur God's wrath means to become deserving of it, like someone who merits being killed in retaliation for an equal victim. TABRISI (قوم): Some say bāʾū means they confessed what incurred God's wrath; an anonymous verse uses the verb for admitting fault and sin. TABRISI: They returned bearing God's anger, which was due upon them and had overtaken them. RAZI-FULL: A third interpretation of bāʾū is that they deserved the anger, as in 5:29, where tabūʾ means to incur or deserve the two sins. BAYDAWI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned carrying anger or became deserving of God's anger; the idiom means someone is liable to be killed for another, and its root connotes equality. NASAFI-FULL: To incur God's anger means to become deserving of it, by analogy with one who is liable to be killed in return for an equal victim. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: باءوا may carry a present-tense sense, as in other expressions where an impending event is stated in the past tense. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says they incurred God's anger. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains bāʾū as returning while carrying something; here they returned burdened with God's anger, paralleling 5:29. DURR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says they incurred and deserved God's anger. BURSEVI: To “return bearing” God’s anger means they deserved and bound themselves to it; the Prophet’s wording “I acknowledge” illustrates the verb. ELMALILI: They incurred God's anger and deserved it; their state was extinguished, their society scattered, and they became those described in al-Fātiḥa as subject to wrath. [23 notes: MUQATIL; MAJAZ; AKHFASH; TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو عبيدة,الزجاج); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL(أبو عبيدة); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(قوم); RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير,الضحاك); DURR-FULL(الضحاك); BURSEVI; ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q47/p3 Deserve or become liable to wrath — AKHFASH: باءوا بغضب means they returned bearing it or that it came upon them; the verb is also used in باء بذنبه. TAB-FULL: They returned carrying God’s anger, which had settled on them and made them liable to His displeasure. TAB-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabiʿ says that anger from God befell them. TAB-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says they deserved God’s anger. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr explains incurring God's anger as deserving His displeasure. IBNABIHATIM (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi' says God's anger came upon them; al-Dahhak gave a similar account. THALABI (أبو روق): Abū Rawq glosses the phrase as “deserved”; he considers the bāʾ an extra particle. WAHIDI-BASIT (لبيد): Labīd’s line likewise uses بؤت بحقها for accepting or acknowledging its due right. KASHSHAF-FULL: To incur God's wrath means to become deserving of it, like someone who merits being killed in retaliation for an equal victim. TABRISI: They returned bearing God's anger, which was due upon them and had overtaken them. RAZI-FULL: A third interpretation of bāʾū is that they deserved the anger, as in 5:29, where tabūʾ means to incur or deserve the two sins. BAYDAWI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned carrying anger or became deserving of God's anger; the idiom means someone is liable to be killed for another, and its root connotes equality. NASAFI-FULL: To incur God's anger means to become deserving of it, by analogy with one who is liable to be killed in return for an equal victim. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If باء means return, its preposition marks accompanying state; if it means deserve, the preposition completes the verb; if it means settle or become equal, it is locative, with different attachment analyses. SAMIN-DURR (الراغب): Al-Raghib explains باؤوا بغضب as settling in a place with wrath; باء signals that their fitting place carries God's anger, and he compares the ironic announcement in 3:21. SAMIN-DURR (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari reads باء فلان بفلان as deserving to be killed in retaliation for him, hence becoming deserving God's wrath; this makes the bāʾ phrase unlike a circumstantial phrase. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi ibn Anas says anger from God was brought down upon them; Saʿid ibn Jubayr says they deserved divine displeasure. BIQAI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned, and they deserved the anger; the phrase can mean being liable to be killed in equivalence for another's killing. DURR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says they incurred and deserved God's anger. BURSEVI: To “return bearing” God’s anger means they deserved and bound themselves to it; the Prophet’s wording “I acknowledge” illustrates the verb. ALUSI-FULL: باءوا may mean they settled into worldly trials, afterlife punishment, or hardships in both worlds; it may also mean that wrath came upon them, so no object of “return” is needed. ALUSI-FULL: Other interpretations say they became fitting for wrath or deserved punishment; the author calls the latter account far-fetched. ELMALILI: They incurred God's anger and deserved it; their state was extinguished, their society scattered, and they became those described in al-Fātiḥa as subject to wrath. [23 notes: AKHFASH; TAB-FULL(الربيع,الضحاك); IBNABIHATIM(الربيع بن أنس,سعيد بن جبير); THALABI(أبو روق); WAHIDI-BASIT(لبيد); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الراغب,الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR-FULL(الربيع بن أنس); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(الضحاك); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q47/p4 Become matched or equivalent — MAWARDI-FULL (أبو العباس المبرد): Al-Mubarrad derives bāʾū from station or position: they came to occupy a station of God's wrath; a retaliation report says the killer becomes equivalent to the slain brother's killer. MAWARDI-FULL (أبو إسحاق الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj derives bāʾū from equality: they were made equal in wrath. MAWARDI-FULL: Layla al-Akhyaliyya's verse uses bawāʾ for equivalence between slain parties, supporting the interpretation of bāʾū. MAWARDI-FULL (عبادة بن الصامت): A report from ʿUbada ibn al-Samit says the Prophet distributed spoils among them on an equal basis, illustrating bawāʾ as equality. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): He derives the root from equality: people are بواء when equal, a spear is aligned toward a horseman, and bloods are equal; bearing a sin is carrying it evenly. WAHIDI-BASIT (عبادة بن الصامت): A report from him says the Prophet divided spoils equally among the people; عن بواء means on an equal basis. KASHSHAF-FULL: To incur God's wrath means to become deserving of it, like someone who merits being killed in retaliation for an equal victim. TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says the root sense of bāʾū is equalization. TABRISI (عبادة بن الصامت): Ubada ibn al-Samit reports that the Prophet distributed the spoils equally among the recipients, illustrating bāʾūʾan as equal shares. TABRISI: A line of poetry uses bawāʾ for an equal or balanced exchange, while noting that blood is not measured out like goods. RAZI-FULL: The word bāʾū can mean they returned, and it is only used with evil; al-Zajjaj gives another view that it means God's anger was made equal to them. QURTUBI-FULL: Bawāʾ can mean returning retribution for blood or equal status in a matter, as poetry also describes return with captives and spoils. BAYDAWI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned carrying anger or became deserving of God's anger; the idiom means someone is liable to be killed for another, and its root connotes equality. NASAFI-FULL: To incur God's anger means to become deserving of it, by analogy with one who is liable to be killed in return for an equal victim. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If باء means return, its preposition marks accompanying state; if it means deserve, the preposition completes the verb; if it means settle or become equal, it is locative, with different attachment analyses. SAMIN-DURR: البواء can mean return in retaliation, and people may be بواء, equal in a matter; the poem لا يبوء الدم بالدم means blood is not repaid with blood in retribution. SAMIN-DURR (الراغب): Al-Raghib says البواء originally means equal parts sharing one place, contrasted with النبوة, the parts' incompatibility. SAMIN-DURR (خلف الأحمر): Khalaf al-Ahmar says حياك الله وبياك originally was بوأك and changed for sound-pattern matching. SAMIN-DURR (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari reads باء فلان بفلان as deserving to be killed in retaliation for him, hence becoming deserving God's wrath; this makes the bāʾ phrase unlike a circumstantial phrase. BIQAI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned, and they deserved the anger; the phrase can mean being liable to be killed in equivalence for another's killing. [20 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(أبو إسحاق الزجاج,أبو العباس المبرد,عبادة بن الصامت); WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج,عبادة بن الصامت); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(الزجاج,عبادة بن الصامت); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الراغب,الزمخشري,خلف الأحمر); BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:61/q47/p5 God's anger came upon them — WAHIDI-BASIT (الكسائي): He glosses باءوا as ‘they departed with it’ and says the verb always takes something good or bad, not bare departure. IBNATIYYA-FULL (المهدوي): Al-Mahdawi says bāʾ means lām, yielding the sense 'because they'. IBNATIYYA-FULL: ذلك refers back to the earlier indication and reinforces it; the bāʾ in بما expresses cause. TABRISI (قوم): Some explain the wrath as worldly affliction and retribution replacing ease and blessing. RAZI-FULL: The usual reading is atastabdilūna; Ubayy b. Kaʿb's codex has atubaddilūna with bāʾ quiescent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: In the construction, الذي is the object and denotes what is gained, while the item introduced by باء is what is given up. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعض الناس): Some claim باء is used only in evil contexts. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Describing the wrath as being from God magnifies and exalts its seriousness. SAMIN-DURR: A poem's آبوا is wrongly offered as evidence for باء: it belongs to آَبَ يَؤوب, with radicals hamza-waw-bāʾ, unlike باء يبوء; reversing the roots is unattested. BIQAI-FULL: The word dhālika may refer either to the anger and punishment they suffered or to their intense concern for livelihood and desires in this base fashion. ALUSI-FULL: Describing the wrath as coming from God heightens and magnifies its severity. IBNASHUR-FULL: The pronoun in وضربت عليهم ... وباءوا refers to all Israelites, including later generations; the addressed Israelites are included by implication through inherited ancestral traits. [12 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الكسائي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(المهدوي); TABRISI(قوم); RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعض الناس); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q47/p6 Acknowledge or admit — WAHIDI-BASIT (لبيد): Labīd’s line likewise uses بؤت بحقها for accepting or acknowledging its due right. BAGHAWI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu ʿUbayda says “bāʾū” means they bore and acknowledged the wrath, as in the prayer “I acknowledge Your favor and my sin.” TABRISI (قوم): Some say bāʾū means they confessed what incurred God's wrath; an anonymous verse uses the verb for admitting fault and sin. QURTUBI-FULL: Bāʾū means they returned and remained bound to the consequence; the root means return, including the prayer acknowledging and binding oneself to God's blessing. BURSEVI: To “return bearing” God’s anger means they deserved and bound themselves to it; the Prophet’s wording “I acknowledge” illustrates the verb. [5 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(لبيد); BAGHAWI-FULL(أبو عبيدة); TABRISI(قوم); QURTUBI-FULL; BURSEVI]
- 2:61/q47/p7 Settle in a station of wrath — TAB-FULL: They returned carrying God’s anger, which had settled on them and made them liable to His displeasure. MAWARDI-FULL (أبو العباس المبرد): Al-Mubarrad derives bāʾū from station or position: they came to occupy a station of God's wrath; a retaliation report says the killer becomes equivalent to the slain brother's killer. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: If باء means return, its preposition marks accompanying state; if it means deserve, the preposition completes the verb; if it means settle or become equal, it is locative, with different attachment analyses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: In ذلك بأنهم, ذلك is the subject, the following prepositional phrase its predicate, and باء marks cause: this happened because they disbelieved and killed. ALUSI-FULL: باءوا may mean they settled into worldly trials, afterlife punishment, or hardships in both worlds; it may also mean that wrath came upon them, so no object of “return” is needed. [5 notes: TAB-FULL; MAWARDI-FULL(أبو العباس المبرد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q48 What is meant by God's wrath here?  [بِغَضَبٍۢ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ · theology]
turns on: Divine will, censure, worldly affliction, or afterlife punishment.
- 2:61/q48/p1 Condemnation and punishment — WAHIDI-BASIT: Thus the wrath is pictured as settled equally upon them; God’s wrath means His condemnation and punishment, not a human-like emotional state. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Wrath meaning will is an essential attribute, while wrath displayed through punishment is an attribute of action. TABRISI (آخرون): Others take the wrath as punishment for their sins in the afterlife. RAZI-FULL: God's anger means His will to exact punishment. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Wrath here can mean the worldly calamities and punishments that befell them or punishment awaiting them in the afterlife. BURSEVI: God’s anger means His censure of them in this world and punishment in the next. [6 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI(آخرون); RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BURSEVI]
- 2:61/q48/p2 God's will to punish — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Wrath meaning will is an essential attribute, while wrath displayed through punishment is an attribute of action. RAZI-FULL: God's anger means His will to exact punishment. [2 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; RAZI-FULL]
- 2:61/q48/p3 Afterlife punishment — TABRISI (آخرون): Others take the wrath as punishment for their sins in the afterlife. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Wrath here can mean the worldly calamities and punishments that befell them or punishment awaiting them in the afterlife. [2 notes: TABRISI(آخرون); ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:61/q48/p4 Divine displeasure — QURTUBI-FULL: Al-Qurṭubī says the meaning of divine anger has already been treated in the Fātiḥah. ELMALILI: After explaining the divine anger, the author transitions by asking whether any possibility of deliverance remains for them. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q48/p5 Worldly affliction — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Wrath here can mean the worldly calamities and punishments that befell them or punishment awaiting them in the afterlife. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]

## 2:61/q49 What does the first dhālika point to?  [ذَٰلِكَ · referent]
turns on: All punishments, wrath alone, or their concern for livelihood.
- 2:61/q49/p1 Humiliation, poverty, and wrath together — TAB-FULL: The demonstrative dhālika gathers the multiple punishments just described: humiliation, poverty, and God’s anger. ZAJJAJ: “That” refers to the wrath that came upon them because they disbelieved in God’s signs. BAGHAWI-FULL: “That” refers to the wrath just mentioned. TABRISI: Ṭabrisī says dhālika points back to the wrath in bāʾū bi-ghaḍab; the causal phrase is governed by the predicate, and the deleted clause is understood from what follows. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: The demonstrative that refers either to God’s wrath alone or to all that was imposed on them, including humiliation and poverty. BAYDAWI-FULL: The demonstrative points back to the humiliation, poverty, and incurring of anger. NASAFI-FULL: The first that refers to the imposed humiliation, poverty and deserved anger. SAMIN-DURR: The demonstrative ذلك points back to the humiliation, poverty, and incurring of wrath; the clause بأنهم is its predicate and explains the cause of that fate. BURSEVI: “That” refers to the humiliation, poverty, their bearing the anger, and the great wrath. ALUSI-FULL: The demonstrative refers to the preceding humiliation, poverty, and wrath; its distance may reflect the intervening material, their surprising punishment despite being People of the Book, or the enormity of these deeds. IBNASHUR-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda challenged Ruʾba’s singular كأنه when the poet had named plural lines, and Ruʾba replied that he meant ذلك, the whole matter. IBNASHUR-FULL (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshāf explains singular demonstrative and relative forms by saying their grammatical number and gender are not literal. Against — IBNASHUR-FULL: Ibn ʿĀshūr finds that explanation of little value because it still relies on interpretation or metaphor, with no special advantage for these forms. IBNASHUR-FULL: He suggests al-Kashshāf may mean that ذا and الذي denote a genus, while their dual and plural forms are separate words rather than inflections of a singular. [13 notes: TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو عبيدة,صاحب الكشاف); IBNASHUR-FULL-]
- 2:61/q49/p2 The fate just described — TABRISI: He applies the same parsing to dhālika bimā ʿaṣaw, with mā and its clause interpreted as a verbal noun. RAZI-FULL (الفريق الأول): The first group answers Abu Muslim that the standard reading is with tanwin and that the apparent general choice can be restricted to this specific city by their earlier evidence. RAZI-FULL (الفريق الأول): The first group replies to Abu Muslim that property may be limited, as with pledged and leased property, so prior evidence overrides the default that ownership permits unrestricted use. SAMIN-DURR: يكفرون is the accusative predicate of كان; كان and its clause predicate أن, while the أن-clause is governed by باء and that phrase predicates ذلك. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): A reported alternative takes the causal باء to mean “with,” and links the demonstrative to the earlier punishment; it omits a conjunction to show that each wrongdoing independently deserves punishment. ALUSI-FULL: The author rejects that alternative as repetitious and as losing the earlier subtle causal relation; it also leaves the demonstrative without a clear purpose. IBNASHUR-FULL: The singular demonstrative ذالك summarizes the preceding statement as one mentioned matter; the punishment terms themselves are unseen and are not pointed to as objects. [7 notes: TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(الفريق الأول); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q49/p3 God's wrath alone — ZAJJAJ: “That” refers to the wrath that came upon them because they disbelieved in God’s signs. BAGHAWI-FULL: “That” refers to the wrath just mentioned. TABRISI: Ṭabrisī says dhālika points back to the wrath in bāʾū bi-ghaḍab; the causal phrase is governed by the predicate, and the deleted clause is understood from what follows. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: The demonstrative that refers either to God’s wrath alone or to all that was imposed on them, including humiliation and poverty. [4 notes: ZAJJAJ; BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD]

## 2:61/q50 What does the second dhālika point to?  [ذَٰلِكَ · referent]
turns on: Repetition of punishment versus disbelief and killing as later outcomes.
- 2:61/q50/p1 The earlier matter is resumed — KASHSHAF-FULL: The repeated demonstrative reiterates the previous reference. TABRISI: Those who read without hamza cite the Quranic plural anbiyaʾ, whose pattern commonly comes from weak-final nouns, as evidence that the singular's hamza was replaced. RAZI-FULL: A second answer is emphatic repetition, as in 23:117, whose wording that an idol has no proof makes the added denial rhetorically emphatic. [3 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL]
- 2:61/q50/p2 It repeats the earlier punishment — QURTUBI-FULL: The second dhālika points back to and reinforces the first; bi in bimā marks cause, and al-Akhfash glosses it as 'because of their disobedience.' BAYDAWI-FULL (قيل): The repeated demonstrative stresses that their punishment resulted both from disbelief and killing and from their sins and transgression. SAMIN-DURR: The repeated demonstrative ذلك either emphasizes the earlier reference or, with al-Zamakhshari, points specifically to disbelief and killing the prophets as caused by persistent disobedience and transgression. [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:61/q50/p3 It points to disbelief and killing — BAYDAWI-FULL (قيل): The repeated demonstrative stresses that their punishment resulted both from disbelief and killing and from their sins and transgression. BAYDAWI-FULL (قيل): Alternatively, the demonstrative refers to disbelief and killing, with bāʾ meaning ‘along with’; a singular demonstrative can cover multiple matters under a contextual singular expression or ellipsis. BAYDAWI-FULL: Rubaʾ's description of a cow uses a singular pronoun for multiple marks, illustrating that demonstratives and pronouns are not strictly marked for real number or gender; alladhī can likewise mean plural. SAMIN-DURR: The repeated demonstrative ذلك either emphasizes the earlier reference or, with al-Zamakhshari, points specifically to disbelief and killing the prophets as caused by persistent disobedience and transgression. [4 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:61/q51 Which signs did they reject?  [يَكْفُرُونَ بِـَٔايَٰتِ ٱللَّهِ · referent]
turns on: Miracles, scriptures, Muhammad's description, or God's proofs generally.
- 2:61/q51/p1 Revealed scriptures and verses — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari says neither the Qur'an nor a decisive prophetic report settles which interpretation is correct, and exegetes disagree. THALABI: Their unbelief in God’s signs concerns the description of Muḥammad as merciful in the Torah, Gospel, and Qurʾān. WAHIDI-BASIT (غيره): Another exegetical view identifies the signs as Muhammad’s description and the stoning verse. BAGHAWI-FULL: The signs they disbelieved in included Muhammad's description, the Torah's stoning verse, the Gospel, and the Qurʾan. IBNATIYYA-FULL: آيات may mean the nine signs and other extraordinary signs proving the truth of the messenger, or the signs of the Torah analogous to Quranic signs. TABRISI: One explanation takes God's signs to mean the Gospel and Quran, making the first wrath for rejecting Jesus and the Gospel and a second for rejecting Muhammad and the Quran; another takes them as Muhammad's attributes. BAYDAWI-FULL: Alternatively, the signs are revealed scriptures, including the Gospel, Qurʾān, stoning verse, and the Torah's description of Muhammad. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The Israelites asked Moses to pray because a prophet’s prayer was more likely to be answered; saying your Lord reflects his special closeness through conversation and revelation of the Torah. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: They said your Lord because God had specially favored Moses through intimate speech and giving him the Torah, so they hoped He would answer Moses’s prayer for them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): God's signs may mean Moses's nine miracles and others, the Torah, selected Torah passages, the Qur'an, or all signs revealed to the messengers. BIQAI-FULL: The author links denial of God's signs to the Qur'anic description of such deniers as companions of ill fortune. BURSEVI: Their disbelief also involved rejecting the Qur’an and Muhammad, denying his description in the Torah, and rejecting Jesus and the Gospel. ALUSI-FULL: They say “your Lord” because Moses had a special intimacy with God through His favor, direct speech, and the Torah, as if asking him to pray to the benefactor who favored him. ALUSI-FULL: The signs may mean miracles generally, Moses's nine signs, those nine and other miracles, revealed scriptural signs generally, the Torah or some of its verses, verses describing Muhammad, the stoning passages, or the Qur'an. [14 notes: TAB-FULL; THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(غيره); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q51/p2 Muhammad's description — THALABI: Their unbelief in God’s signs concerns the description of Muḥammad as merciful in the Torah, Gospel, and Qurʾān. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن عباس): He says the signs of God they disbelieved in refer to the wisdom sent down to Muhammad. WAHIDI-BASIT (غيره): Another exegetical view identifies the signs as Muhammad’s description and the stoning verse. BAGHAWI-FULL: The signs they disbelieved in included Muhammad's description, the Torah's stoning verse, the Gospel, and the Qurʾan. TABRISI: One explanation takes God's signs to mean the Gospel and Quran, making the first wrath for rejecting Jesus and the Gospel and a second for rejecting Muhammad and the Quran; another takes them as Muhammad's attributes. BAYDAWI-FULL: Alternatively, the signs are revealed scriptures, including the Gospel, Qurʾān, stoning verse, and the Torah's description of Muhammad. BIQAI-FULL: The author links denial of God's signs to the Qur'anic description of such deniers as companions of ill fortune. BURSEVI: Their disbelief also involved rejecting the Qur’an and Muhammad, denying his description in the Torah, and rejecting Jesus and the Gospel. ALUSI-FULL: The signs may mean miracles generally, Moses's nine signs, those nine and other miracles, revealed scriptural signs generally, the Torah or some of its verses, verses describing Muhammad, the stoning passages, or the Qur'an. [9 notes: THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس,غيره); BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; BAYDAWI-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q51/p3 Miracles shown to Israel — IBNATIYYA-FULL: آيات may mean the nine signs and other extraordinary signs proving the truth of the messenger, or the signs of the Torah analogous to Quranic signs. RAZI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars regard the prediction of their humiliation and poverty as a miracle because the Prophet foretold it and it occurred, revealing unseen knowledge. BAYDAWI-FULL: God's signs may mean miracles they experienced: the sea split, clouds shaded them, manna and quails descended, and springs burst from stone. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): God's signs may mean Moses's nine miracles and others, the Torah, selected Torah passages, the Qur'an, or all signs revealed to the messengers. BURSEVI: God’s signs include the conspicuous miracles shown through Moses, whether familiar or unfamiliar. ALUSI-FULL: The signs may mean miracles generally, Moses's nine signs, those nine and other miracles, revealed scriptural signs generally, the Torah or some of its verses, verses describing Muhammad, the stoning passages, or the Qur'an. [6 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; RAZI-FULL(بعض العلماء); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q51/p4 God's signs generally — TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj explains that the wrath came upon them because of their disbelief in God's signs. TABRISI: Dhālika points back to the wrath, humiliation, and poverty already mentioned as caused by their disbelief in God's signs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The signs are attributed to God because they originate from Him. SAMIN-DURR: The phrase بآيات الله attaches to يكفرون, and its باء marks transitivity. BIQAI-FULL: Kānū depicts a settled natural disposition; yakfurūna describes continuing, renewed concealment of assent and belief in God's signs, misleading those unaware of them. [5 notes: TABRISI(الزجاج); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:61/q51/p5 Proofs of God's oneness — BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali ranks denial of God's signs as farthest from faith and nearer than denial of God, since the former rejects manifest evidence while the latter rejects the unseen. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]

## 2:61/q52 How is prophet-killing attributed to these Israelites?  [وَيَقْتُلُونَ ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · interpretation]
turns on: Direct perpetrators versus later people approving or following them.
- 2:61/q52/p1 Offenders among Israel are charged — MUQATIL: The signs of God they disbelieved in mean the Qur'an; they killed prophets unjustly because they disobeyed and transgressed in their religion. MAWARDI-FULL: A report describes a man bringing his brother's killer to the Prophet, who says “he is equivalent to him,” meaning the killer takes the victim-killer's place. QURTUBI-FULL: The verse permits eating wholesome, pleasurable foods; the Prophet liked sweets and honey and drank cool, fresh water. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The wording does not imply any prophets were killed justly: prophets cannot deserve execution; the qualification shames and condemns the killers, who claimed no justified basis even by their own standard. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Their humiliation, poverty, and wrath resulted from rejecting miraculous signs that should have led to faith and killing prophets whose guidance brings worldly honor and salvation; earlier disobedience and transgression led to those acts. IBNKATHIR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the most severely punished at the Resurrection include someone killed by a prophet or who killed a prophet, a deviant leader, and an image-maker. ALUSI-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffal says they claimed prophets were liars and their miracles deceptions, and killed them for this reason and to suppress what they regarded as truth. IBNASHUR-FULL: The charge of killing prophets covers Jewish generations who killed, ordered, or silently failed to defend them. [8 notes: MUQATIL; MAWARDI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); ALUSI-FULL(القفال); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q52/p2 Later Israelites share guilt through solidarity — IBNKATHIR-FULL: Their humiliation and anger followed their arrogance against truth, disbelief in God's signs, and dishonoring prophets and their followers until they killed prophets. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]

## 2:61/q53 Which prophets are said to have been killed?  [وَيَقْتُلُونَ ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · reports]
turns on: Named reports versus general reference to Israelite prophets.
- 2:61/q53/p1 God's prophets generally — TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says God replaced their honor with humiliation, blessing with hardship, and favor with anger for disbelief, killing prophets, wrongful aggression, and disobedience. TAB-FULL: The phrase bima means ‘because of’: their punishment was for disbelief in God’s signs and killing prophets unjustly. TAB-FULL: The prophets are God’s messengers, sent to convey what He commissioned them to tell their audiences. KASHSHAF-FULL: Their disobedience means committing many sins and transgressing God's limits in every matter, alongside disbelief and prophet-killing. TABRISI (أبو علي): Abu Ali says nabāwa means elevation, which is not necessarily prophethood, whereas a messenger from God is precisely described by nabaʾ, news. TABRISI: Dhālika bimā ʿaṣaw refers to their Sabbath violation and killing prophets, or to breaking the covenant and assaulting prophets; God acted against them for disobedience and transgression. RAZI-FULL: Their killing of prophets also makes them deserve the preceding punishment; disbelief in God's signs means ignorance and denial and does not itself include killing prophets. RAZI-FULL: The punishment's causes are ordered excellently: first offenses against God through ignorance and denial, then killing prophets, then personal sins, and finally sins against others through aggression. BAYDAWI-FULL: Their disobedience, persistence, and transgression led them into disbelief in signs and killing prophets. NASAFI-FULL: The final that may point to unbelief and killing prophets together with the other acts of disobedience. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (علي): Ali's doubled-consonant reading يقتّلون intensifies the sense of repeated killing. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (صاحب المنتخب): The punishment is explained by causes ordered by gravity: first disbelief in God's signs, then killing prophets, then lesser sins and transgression. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another explanation takes transgression as persistence in disobedience and killing prophets. IBNKATHIR-FULL (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masud reports Malik ibn Marara asking whether his good looks and wish no one surpass him by two sandal straps count as arrogance; the Prophet says arrogance is rejecting truth and belittling people. BIQAI-FULL: The author's description treats killing prophets as a settled disposition and habit. DURR-FULL (ابن مسعود): The Prophet says the most severely punished at Resurrection include a killer of a prophet or someone killed by a prophet, a leader of misguidance, and an image maker. BURSEVI: This account does not conflict with 40:51 or 37:171–172; alternatively, victory can mean victory through proof and disclosure of truth, which every prophet had. BURSEVI: The Mathnawī couplet links foolishness with killing prophets and alludes to people calling the messengers ill omens. BURSEVI: Persistent disobedience and transgression led to the previously described disbelief and prophet-killing. ALUSI-FULL: The root باء first denotes equality of parts and then any equivalence or matching, as in saying someone is another's equal and in cited idioms and a prophetic warning. ALUSI-FULL: Attributing the signs to God intensifies the condemnation; disbelief is mentioned first because it is the gravest offense, then prophet-killing as its consequence or source. ALUSI-FULL (بعض المتأخرين): A later scholar says the qualifier is useful if غير means “for a cause other than the truth”: they killed prophets for falsehood and to defend it. ALUSI-FULL: The final ذلك points back to disbelief and prophet-killing as causes of the earlier punishments; a singular demonstrative can gather several matters by treating them as one mentioned event. STUDYQURAN: The killing of prophets recurs in Qurʾānic passages and has parallels in Luke, Matthew, Romans, 1 Thessalonians, and 1 Kings. [24 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(أبو علي); RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(صاحب المنتخب,علي,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن مسعود); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(بعض المتأخرين); STUDYQURAN]
- 2:61/q53/p2 Isaiah, Zechariah, John and others — THALABI: The prophets killed unjustly include Isaiah, Zechariah, John, and the other prophets killed by the Jews. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): The prophets meant include Zakariyyā, Yaḥyā, and Isaiah, along with the other prophets the Jews killed. KASHSHAF-FULL: The demonstrative refers to humiliation, indigence and incurring wrath, caused by disbelief and killing prophets; the Jews killed Isaiah, Zechariah, John and others. QURTUBI-FULL: Dhālika explains the cause: they disbelieved in God's book and prophets' miracles, including those of Jesus, John, Zechariah, and Muhammad, and killed prophets. BAYDAWI-FULL: They killed Isaiah, Zechariah, John, and others without grounds even by their own standards; desire and worldly love drove them. NASAFI-FULL: Their unbelief and killing of prophets caused their punishment; the prophets killed by the Jews included Isaiah, Zechariah and John. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The prophets they killed included John, Isaiah, and Zechariah. ALUSI-FULL: That false accusation explains many cases, but Isaiah, John, and Zechariah were killed for other reasons: Isaiah rebuked factional violence after a king's death, John was killed over a woman, and Zechariah was sawn in two inside a tree while fleeing the king. ELMALILI: Their punishment came because they disbelieved God's manifest signs and unjustly killed prophets, including Isaiah, Zechariah, and John. IBNASHUR-FULL: Ibn ʿĀshūr says Isaiah son of Amoz, alive in the mid-eighth century BCE, was sawn on a tree by King Manasseh around 700 BCE. IBNASHUR-FULL: Herod killed John son of Zechariah because Herod’s niece was angry with John. ASAD-NOTES: The passage concerns a later phase of Jewish history; Asad cites John the Baptist and Gospel accusations by Jesus, plus accounts of Zachariah's murder, as evidence that Jews killed prophets. [12 notes: THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); KASHSHAF-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; ASAD-NOTES]

## 2:61/q54 How many prophets were reportedly killed?  [وَيَقْتُلُونَ ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · reports]
turns on: The different transmitted counts of prophets and worshippers.
- 2:61/q54/p1 Three hundred prophets in one day — IBNABIHATIM (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Mas'ud reports that the Israelites killed three hundred prophets in a day and continued killing them through the evening. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masʿūd reports that Israelites killed three hundred prophets in one day, then opened their vegetable market that evening. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن مسعود): A report from Ibn Masud says the Israelites killed seventy prophets; another version says three hundred in the morning, with killings openly active by evening. ALUSI-FULL: The indefinite plural النبيين appears fewer than الأنبياء, while the two forms are equivalent when definite, so the report that they killed three hundred prophets in one morning does not contradict the wording. [4 notes: IBNABIHATIM(عبد الله بن مسعود); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(عبد الله بن مسعود); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن مسعود); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q54/p2 Seventy prophets in a morning or hour — BAGHAWI-FULL: A report says the Jews killed seventy prophets in the morning and set up a marketplace to kill them again in the evening. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن مسعود): A report from Ibn Masud says the Israelites killed seventy prophets; another version says three hundred in the morning, with killings openly active by evening. BURSEVI: A report says the Israelites killed seventy prophets in one day. [3 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن مسعود); BURSEVI]

## 2:61/q55 How is the killing verb read?  [وَيَقْتُلُونَ · readings]
turns on: Light third-person form, intensified gemination, or second-person address.
- 2:61/q55/p1 Intensive doubled consonant — THALABI: The common reading has yaqtulūn with light consonants; al-Sulamī reads it with shadda, expressing intensive killing. JISHUMI (السلمي): The common reading has light yaqtulūn; al-Sulamī reads it with an intensified consonant. KASHSHAF-FULL (علي رضي الله عنه): Ali reads the verb with gemination, emphasizing repeated killing. SAMIN-DURR: ويقتلون is coordinated in the accusative with كان's predicate; one reading shifts to direct address, while gemination in يقتّلون marks repeated killing. ALUSI-FULL (علي والحسن): ʿAli and, in one report, al-Hasan read يقتلون with a doubled qaf. [5 notes: THALABI; JISHUMI(السلمي); KASHSHAF-FULL(علي رضي الله عنه); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(علي والحسن)]
- 2:61/q55/p2 Light third-person form — THALABI: The common reading has yaqtulūn with light consonants; al-Sulamī reads it with shadda, expressing intensive killing. JISHUMI (السلمي): The common reading has light yaqtulūn; al-Sulamī reads it with an intensified consonant. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الحسن بن أبي الحسن): Al-Hasan ibn Abi al-Hasan reads وتقتلون in the second person, returning to direct address; a third-person reading is also reported from him. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan has a variant reading of yaqtulūn; another transmission from him matches the common reading. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Hasan reads وتقتلون with second-person address, an instance of turning to address; another report gives the common third-person reading. [5 notes: THALABI; JISHUMI(السلمي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الحسن بن أبي الحسن); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن)]
- 2:61/q55/p3 Second-person address — IBNATIYYA-FULL (الحسن بن أبي الحسن): Al-Hasan ibn Abi al-Hasan reads وتقتلون in the second person, returning to direct address; a third-person reading is also reported from him. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Hasan reads وتقتلون with second-person address, an instance of turning to address; another report gives the common third-person reading. SAMIN-DURR: ويقتلون is coordinated in the accusative with كان's predicate; one reading shifts to direct address, while gemination in يقتّلون marks repeated killing. ALUSI-FULL (الحسن): In another reading, al-Hasan uses second-person تقتلون, creating a shift in address. [4 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الحسن بن أبي الحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(الحسن)]

## 2:61/q56 How can prophets be killed despite divine protection?  [وَيَقْتُلُونَ ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
turns on: Protection in fighting or mission, victory through proof, and rank through martyrdom.
- 2:61/q56/p1 Fighting prophets were not killed — MAWARDI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says God never ordered a prophet to wage war and then let him be killed; He allowed unbelievers to kill prophets who had not been commanded to fight. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس وغيره): Ibn Abbas and others say only prophets not commanded to fight were killed; every prophet commanded to fight was victorious. TABRISI (الحسن): Al-Hasan says God never ordered a prophet to fight and then be killed in battle; those killed were killed outside combat. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عباس والحسن): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Ḥasan say prophets not commanded to fight could be killed, while every prophet commanded to fight was victorious. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس وغيره): Ibn Abbas and others say prophets not commanded to fight were killed, while every prophet commanded to fight was victorious. BURSEVI (ابن عباس والحسن): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Ḥasan say only prophets not commanded to fight were killed, while every prophet commanded to fight was granted victory. ALUSI-FULL (بعض المحققين): The preferred answer limits the promised messengers to those commanded to fight; their exposure to death is not incompatible with divine wisdom. [7 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(الحسن); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس وغيره); TABRISI(الحسن); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس والحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس وغيره); BURSEVI(ابن عباس والحسن); ALUSI-FULL(بعض المحققين)+]
- 2:61/q56/p2 Their deaths raised their rank — MAWARDI-FULL: One theological explanation is that God allowed unbelievers to kill some prophets so they could attain otherwise unreachable high ranks; this was not abandonment by God. QURTUBI-FULL: God's allowing disbelievers to kill prophets honors them and raises their rank, as with believers slain in God's cause; it is not abandonment. BURSEVI: Allowing prophets to be killed honors them and raises their rank, as with believers killed in God’s path; it is not abandonment. [3 notes: MAWARDI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; BURSEVI]
- 2:61/q56/p3 They completed revelation before death — TABRISI: Ṭabrisī's preferred view is that God may not allow a prophet's death before he completes his assigned revelation, since that would undermine the obligations and their benefits; once he has delivered it, God may allow his killers and need not prevent it. IBNASHUR-FULL: The text says prophets rather than messengers because enemies cannot overcome messengers without undermining the purpose of revelation; the claim that Jews killed Jesus conflicts with that wisdom, since God ended his mission after its aim was achieved. [2 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL; TABRISI+]
- 2:61/q56/p4 Divine protection does not preclude all prophetic deaths — ALUSI-FULL: An atheist alleges a contradiction between Israelite prophet-killing and God's promise to help His messengers. ALUSI-FULL: The distinction fails because 2:87 also says that some messengers were killed. [2 notes: ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q57 What is implied by the prophet plural used here?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · rhetoric]
turns on: Whether the plural forms imply number here or converge when definite.
- 2:61/q57/p1 The plurals differ in pattern — AKHFASH: Both النبيين and الأنبياء are forms used by Arab speakers for the plural of prophet. MAWARDI-FULL: Nabī has the plural form nabāʾ, used by Abbas ibn Mirdas in a verse praising the Prophet. IBNATIYYA-FULL: The dominant plural أنبياء resembles defective forms such as walī/awliyāʾ and ṣafī/aṣfiyāʾ. SAMIN-DURR: The second cited poetic use places a النبي at a mountain, while نبيئة is reported as a disparaging diminutive for Musaylima; أنبياء follows the regular plural pattern of weak فعيل nouns. ALUSI-FULL: The plural نبيين is challenged because a passive participle is not normally given the sound masculine plural; a reply says the word is active, or that its original sense has faded so the common meaning permits the plural. [5 notes: AKHFASH; MAWARDI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:61/q57/p2 The definite plurals mean the same — IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو علي): Abu Ali calls the greeting report's chain weak; he says the Prophet's silence when praised as خاتم النبآء strengthens its weakness, since the plural and singular follow the same pattern. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: When definite, النبيين and الأنبياء do not differ in meaning; without the article, the sound plural suggests few and the broken plural suggests many. ELMALILI: Nabī derives from nabaʾ and originally means one who brings news by revelation from God; it is equivalent to prophet, with plurals anbiyāʾ and nabiyyīn. [3 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو علي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ELMALILI]
- 2:61/q57/p3 The sound plural can suggest few — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: When definite, النبيين and الأنبياء do not differ in meaning; without the article, the sound plural suggests few and the broken plural suggests many. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The author treats al-nabiyyīn in al-Baqara as a plural of paucity and al-anbiyāʾ in Āl ʿImrān as a plural of abundance, underscoring the latter’s greater condemnation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT: When a sound masculine plural appears alongside a broken plural of abundance, he says it indicates paucity; without that pairing it can denote either few or many. [3 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMARRAI-ASRAR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 2:61/q57/p4 The broken plural can suggest many — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: When definite, النبيين and الأنبياء do not differ in meaning; without the article, the sound plural suggests few and the broken plural suggests many. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The author treats al-nabiyyīn in al-Baqara as a plural of paucity and al-anbiyāʾ in Āl ʿImrān as a plural of abundance, underscoring the latter’s greater condemnation. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT: When a sound masculine plural appears alongside a broken plural of abundance, he says it indicates paucity; without that pairing it can denote either few or many. [3 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMARRAI-ASRAR; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]

## 2:61/q58 How is nabī and its plural read?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · readings]
turns on: Nāfiʿ's transmission, hamza ease, the al-Aḥzāb exceptions, and the disputed correction report.
- 2:61/q58/p1 Hamzated nabīʾ — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari notes an Arabic form without hamza and a hamzated form in which people say dāniʾ for mean or vile. TAB-FULL (الأعشى): A line by al-Aʿshā uses hamzated dāniʾ, evidence for the hamzated Arabic form. TAB-FULL: Nabī is originally hamzated from anbaʾa, ‘to inform’; the form munbiʾ shifted to nabī by changing hamza to yāʾ, like samīʿ and baṣīr from their corresponding forms. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari cites Abbas ibn Mirdas’s line, whose form khatim al-naba supports a hamzated singular nabi. TAB-FULL (some scholars): Some derive nabī and nubuwwa without hamza from nabwa, an elevated place, and say the original sense is a visible, raised road; they report not hearing the word hamzated. TAB-FULL (القطامي): Al-Qutami’s line uses nabiyan for a road, cited by the unhamzated etymology as evidence that nabī means a manifest road. ZAJJAJ (أهل المدينة): The agreed reading of “nabiyyīn,” “anbiyāʾ,” and “barīʾa” drops hamza; a group of Medinan readers pronounce hamza in these forms throughout the Qur’an. ZAJJAJ: “Nabī” may derive from “anbaʾa,” with hamza dropped through frequent use, or from a verb meaning to rise, giving the sense of high rank. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The hamzated form of النبيين derives from informing or announcing, as in the cited expressions about reporting truth and naming the angels. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The unhamzated form is supported by the heaviness of hamza in Arabic speech; the cited Prophetic wording and Quraysh's non-hamzating illustrate this. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The unhamzated form may derive from nabwa, an elevated place, because the Prophet reports from the exalted realm and conveys God's message. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: Arabic often drops hamza in words whose origins contain it; the author gives النبي، الخابية، البرية، الذرية، and الروية as parallels. FARISI-HUJJA (المسيبي وقالون): Al-Musayyibī and Qālūn report that Nāfiʿ hamzas النبيّ forms throughout the Qurʾān except al-Aḥzāb 50 and 53, where adjacent same-type kasra hamzas meet. FARISI-HUJJA (ورش عن نافع): Warsh reports Nāfiʿ hamzas all forms but drops the second hamza when two meet, whether alike or different, as in the two al-Aḥzāb phrases. FARISI-HUJJA (الباقون): All remaining readers leave hamza out of these forms. FARISI-HUJJA: He rejects derivation from النباوة, or both proposed roots, because Sibawayh’s diminutive and the agreed forms تنبأ and النبيئة establish hamza as the root consonant. FARISI-HUJJA: Agreement on تنبأ مسيلمة and النبيئة shows that the root has hamza, despite both hamza and weak-letter readings in the transmitted forms. THALABI: The famous reading has al-nabiyyīn with shadda and no hamza; Nāfiʿ alone uses hamza and lengthens it. THALABI: With hamza, nabīʾ means “informer,” from the Arabic root n-b-ʾ, illustrated by the verse “when she informed him of it.” THALABI (الكسائي): Al-Kisāʾī says nabī without hamza means “road”; a messenger is named for guiding people, and a poem uses nabī in this sense. MAWARDI-FULL: The common reading leaves nabī unhamzated; Nafiʿ alone reads al-anbiyāʾ and al-nabiyyīn with hamza. WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو عبيدة): Most Arabs omit the hamza in النبي; he says the Arabs likewise omit it in four words whose original forms contain hamza. JISHUMI (نافع): Nāfiʿ reads nabiyīn andnabiyūn with hamza, restoring the original form; the other readers omit hamza, the lighter and more widespread reading. JISHUMI (رسول الله): A report says a man addressed the Prophet with nabīʾ, with hamza, and he instructed him to say nabī without hamza. BAGHAWI-FULL (نافع): Nafiʿ alone pronounces the hamza in “nabī” and its related forms, giving the sense of one who announces. IBNATIYYA-FULL (نافع): Nafiʿ hamzates النبيئين wherever it occurs except the two Ahzab passages, 33:50 and 33:53, where he omits lengthening and hamza; the remaining readers omit hamza throughout. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Hamzating derives nabīʾ from anbaʾa, 'to inform': nabīʾ means an informer, like samīʿ meaning one who makes heard; the plural nabāʾ supports the derivation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الشاعر): A poetic line addressed to the Seal of the Prophets uses النبآء as a plural form supporting the hamzated derivation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو علي): Abu Ali cites Sibawayh's نبيئة in a construction with نبوته and the universal use of تنبأ to argue that the root's final radical is hamza. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Those who do not hamzate differ: some derive nabī from hamza but ease the hamza, while others derive it from naba yanbū, meaning to appear; the prophet guides to the manifest path of guidance and salvation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الزهراوي): Al-Zahrawi records نبوء for 'appeared', making a conspicuous road نبيء with hamza. IBNATIYYA-FULL (رجل): A report says a man greeted the Prophet as 'Nabīʾ of God' with hamza; the Prophet answered that he was God's prophet without hamza. TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh's evidence that Arabs say nabīʾat suʾ and tanabbaʾa supports the hamzated root and excludes derivation from nabāwa. TABRISI: As evidence against rejecting hamza, the author says a poet praised the Prophet with the plural and no known objection; a fault in the singular would also affect the plural. TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh's report that all Arabs say tanabbaʾa with hamza supports deriving nabiʾ from the hamzated root and rules out nabāwa. TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj allows nabi to derive either from anbaʾa with hamza dropped through frequent use or from nabā, to rise. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: Nāfiʿ uses hamza in nabiyyīn and related forms except in two places in al-Aḥzāb, where two successive same-kind kasra hamzas meet. QURTUBI-FULL (نافع): Nāfiʿ reads al-nabiyyīn with hamzah throughout the Qur'an except two verses in al-Aḥzāb, where he leaves it unlengthened and unhamzated; the other readers omit hamzah throughout. QURTUBI-FULL: The two exceptions to Nāfiʿ's hamzated reading are 33:50 and 33:53, where he reads without lengthening or hamzah because two kasrah hamzahs meet; other readers omit hamzah everywhere. QURTUBI-FULL: The hamzated form derives from anbaʾa, 'inform': nabīʾ is an active participle, plural anbiyāʾ, with nabāʾ also attested as a plural. QURTUBI-FULL (العباس بن مرداس): Al-ʿAbbās b. Mirdās's praise of the Prophet as the seal of nabāʾ supports the hamzated form. QURTUBI-FULL: Those omitting hamzah either derive the word from the same root with a softened hamzah or from nabā yanbū, 'to rise'; nabwah is elevation. QURTUBI-FULL: Without hamzah, nabī can mean a path; a messenger is called nabī because people are guided by him as by a road. QURTUBI-FULL (شاعر): An unnamed poet uses nabī for a path on the ground, supporting the unhamzated lexical sense. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو علي): A report where the Prophet rejects nabīʾ with hamzah and accepts nabī without it has a weak chain, according to Abū ʿAlī; the Prophet's uncorrected hearing of a hamzated poem also weakens it. NASAFI-FULL (نافع): Nafi' pronounces the word for prophets with a hamza, as in its related forms. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Zuhayr's hamzated reading derives أدنأ from lowliness; the unhamzated form may also have originated with hamza and been softened. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (نافع): Nafi hamzates forms of نبي; Qalun replaces and assimilates the hamza in two connected readings in al-Ahzab, while most readers omit hamza. SAMIN-DURR (أبو عبيد): Abu Ubayd says nearly all readers, ordinary speakers, and most Arabs omit the hamza, and relates a report in which the Prophet corrects a man who says يا نبيء الله. SAMIN-DURR (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda says Arabs replace hamza in النبي، البرية، and الخابية; he adds الذرية from ذرأ, while Sibawayh's تنبأ مسيلمة shows that the hamza is original. SAMIN-DURR: The author will not reject Nafi's canonical reading on the basis of a speculative report; if the report is sound, يا نبيء الله may have been stopped because it suggested an expelled person, not because hamza itself was wrong. SAMIN-DURR: Most readers leave the hamza out of النبي and its derivatives; Nafi of Medina pronounces it throughout except two places in al-Ahzab, with Qalun reported to omit it in connected reading there. SAMIN-DURR: With hamza, النبي derives from نَبَأ (news): it means one who informs about God or one informed by God; its plural نبآء supports this derivation, as in al-Abbas ibn Mirdas's verse. SAMIN-DURR (العباس ابن مرداس): Al-Abbas ibn Mirdas's line uses نبآء with the hamza visible, supporting derivation of النبي from نبأ. SAMIN-DURR (أبو علي): Abu Ali cites Sibawayh calling the verified pronunciation of نبيا and برية poor, since the usual Arabic practice is to ease the hamza. SAMIN-DURR: For readers without hamza, the preferred account is that they ease an underlying hamza, matching the hamzated reading and forms such as تنبأ مسيلمة. SAMIN-DURR: A second derivation for the non-hamzated form takes it from نبا ينبو, to rise or appear; the Prophet's rank is elevated and his position manifest. SAMIN-DURR: Qalun has no reported disagreement at these النبي positions; the hamza remains underlying and is omitted only in connected reading, returning at a pause. DURR-FULL (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar says the Prophet, Abu Bakr, Umar, and the caliphs did not use the hamza in nabī; he calls hamza a later innovation. ALUSI-FULL (نافع): Nafiʿ reads النبيين with hamza, as he does النبي and النبوة. ALUSI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A man addressed the Prophet as نبيء الله with hamza; the Prophet replied that he was نبي الله without hamza. QIRAAT-ER: The hamzated nabīʾīn reading derives the word from informing rather than elevation, making the etymological distinction explicit. [62 notes: TAB-FULL(some scholars,الأعشى,القطامي); ZAJJAJ(أهل المدينة); IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA(الباقون,المسيبي وقالون,ورش عن نافع); THALABI(الكسائي); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو عبيدة); JISHUMI(رسول الله,نافع); BAGHAWI-FULL(نافع); IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو علي,الزهراوي,الشاعر,رجل,نافع); TABRISI(الزجاج,سيبويه); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو علي,العباس بن مرداس,شاعر,نافع); NASAFI-FULL(نافع); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(نافع); SAMIN-DURR(أبو عبيد,أبو عبيدة,أبو علي,العباس ابن مرداس); DURR-FULL(ابن عمر); ALUSI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,نافع); QIRAAT-ER; SAMIN-DURR+]

- 2:61/q58/p3 The correction report favors unhamzated address — TABRISI (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report has the Prophet answer a man who called him nabīʾ Allah by saying he is nabī Allah. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو علي): A report where the Prophet rejects nabīʾ with hamzah and accepts nabī without it has a weak chain, according to Abū ʿAlī; the Prophet's uncorrected hearing of a hamzated poem also weakens it. DURR-FULL (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports that when a Bedouin called the Prophet nabīʾ Allah, he corrected the form and said he was nabī Allah. DURR-FULL (حمران بن أعين): Hamran b. Aʿyan separately reports a Bedouin's address as nabīʾ Allah and the Prophet's correction to nabī Allah.
