- 2:61/q58/p4 The correction report is weak — FARISI-HUJJA: Agreement on تنبأ مسيلمة and النبيئة shows that the root has hamza, despite both hamza and weak-letter readings in the transmitted forms. TABRISI: The author suspects some transmitters judged the report's chain weak. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو علي): A report where the Prophet rejects nabīʾ with hamzah and accepts nabī without it has a weak chain, according to Abū ʿAlī; the Prophet's uncorrected hearing of a hamzated poem also weakens it. SAMIN-DURR: The author will not reject Nafi's canonical reading on the basis of a speculative report; if the report is sound, يا نبيء الله may have been stopped because it suggested an expelled person, not because hamza itself was wrong. [4 notes: FARISI-HUJJA; TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو علي); SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:61/q58/p5 The two transmitted forms are discussed — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The ḍamma reading of both letters preserves their original vowels: the pronoun was originally همو before the preposition. SAMIN-DURR (الحاكم): Al-Hakim grades the correction report authentic according to the conditions of the two Ṣaḥīḥ collections, though neither collection includes it. ALUSI-FULL (أبو زيد): Abu Zayd traces نبأت to leaving the earth; the objection may have been to the ambiguity of “expelled by God,” not to the reading itself, and a prophet's use is not automatically permitted for people. [3 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; SAMIN-DURR(الحاكم); ALUSI-FULL(أبو زيد)]

## 2:61/q59 What is the etymology of nabī?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · meaning]
turns on: Evidence from tanabbaʾa, the plural, poetry, and roots meaning news, elevation, or path.
- 2:61/q59/p1 From nabaʾ, news — TAB-FULL: Nabī is originally hamzated from anbaʾa, ‘to inform’; the form munbiʾ shifted to nabī by changing hamza to yāʾ, like samīʿ and baṣīr from their corresponding forms. ZAJJAJ: “Nabī” may derive from “anbaʾa,” with hamza dropped through frequent use, or from a verb meaning to rise, giving the sense of high rank. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The hamzated form of النبيين derives from informing or announcing, as in the cited expressions about reporting truth and naming the angels. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو زيد): Abū Zayd explains نبأت from moving from one land to another, but distinguishes this travel verb from the etymology of نبي. FARISI-HUJJA: Al-Fārisī derives نبي from نبأ, news, because a prophet reports from God, rather than from the travel verb. FARISI-HUJJA: He rejects derivation from النباوة, or both proposed roots, because Sibawayh’s diminutive and the agreed forms تنبأ and النبيئة establish hamza as the root consonant. FARISI-HUJJA: Agreement on تنبأ مسيلمة and النبيئة shows that the root has hamza, despite both hamza and weak-letter readings in the transmitted forms. THALABI: With hamza, nabīʾ means “informer,” from the Arabic root n-b-ʾ, illustrated by the verse “when she informed him of it.” MAWARDI-FULL: One etymology derives nabī from nabaʾ, report, because a prophet reports from God; the source links this root to the verse about what was reported in Moses' scriptures. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Hamzating derives nabīʾ from anbaʾa, 'to inform': nabīʾ means an informer, like samīʿ meaning one who makes heard; the plural nabāʾ supports the derivation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو علي): Abu Ali cites Sibawayh's نبيئة in a construction with نبوته and the universal use of تنبأ to argue that the root's final radical is hamza. TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh's evidence that Arabs say nabīʾat suʾ and tanabbaʾa supports the hamzated root and excludes derivation from nabāwa. TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh's report that all Arabs say tanabbaʾa with hamza supports deriving nabiʾ from the hamzated root and rules out nabāwa. TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj allows nabi to derive either from anbaʾa with hamza dropped through frequent use or from nabā, to rise. QURTUBI-FULL: The hamzated form derives from anbaʾa, 'inform': nabīʾ is an active participle, plural anbiyāʾ, with nabāʾ also attested as a plural. QURTUBI-FULL: Those omitting hamzah either derive the word from the same root with a softened hamzah or from nabā yanbū, 'to rise'; nabwah is elevation. NASAFI-FULL: Nabī may derive from informing, as one who tells from God, or from rising; nubuwwa is an elevated place. SAMIN-DURR (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda says Arabs replace hamza in النبي، البرية، and الخابية; he adds الذرية from ذرأ, while Sibawayh's تنبأ مسيلمة shows that the hamza is original. SAMIN-DURR: With hamza, النبي derives from نَبَأ (news): it means one who informs about God or one informed by God; its plural نبآء supports this derivation, as in al-Abbas ibn Mirdas's verse. SAMIN-DURR (العباس ابن مرداس): Al-Abbas ibn Mirdas's line uses نبآء with the hamza visible, supporting derivation of النبي from نبأ. SAMIN-DURR: For readers without hamza, the preferred account is that they ease an underlying hamza, matching the hamzated reading and forms such as تنبأ مسيلمة. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali derives nabīʾ from nabaʾ, informing of an unseen matter the reporter could not know unaided. ALUSI-FULL (أبو زيد): Abu Zayd traces نبأت to leaving the earth; the objection may have been to the ambiguity of “expelled by God,” not to the reading itself, and a prophet's use is not automatically permitted for people. QIRAAT-ER: The hamzated nabīʾīn reading derives the word from informing rather than elevation, making the etymological distinction explicit. [24 notes: TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA(أبو زيد); THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو علي); TABRISI(الزجاج,سيبويه); QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(أبو عبيدة,العباس ابن مرداس); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ALUSI-FULL(أبو زيد); QIRAAT-ER; SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:61/q59/p2 From elevation or rising — TAB-FULL (some scholars): Some derive nabī and nubuwwa without hamza from nabwa, an elevated place, and say the original sense is a visible, raised road; they report not hearing the word hamzated. ZAJJAJ: “Nabī” may derive from “anbaʾa,” with hamza dropped through frequent use, or from a verb meaning to rise, giving the sense of high rank. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA: The unhamzated form may derive from nabwa, an elevated place, because the Prophet reports from the exalted realm and conveys God's message. Against — FARISI-HUJJA: He rejects derivation from النباوة, or both proposed roots, because Sibawayh’s diminutive and the agreed forms تنبأ and النبيئة establish hamza as the root consonant. MAWARDI-FULL: A third etymology derives nabī from elevation because prophets hold a high rank. JISHUMI (الكسائي): One etymology derives nabī from nabwa, an elevated place; al-Kisāʾī gives “path” as another derivation, since people are guided by a prophet. BAGHAWI-FULL: Another derivation takes “nabī” to mean elevated, from a high place, with the word retaining its original form. TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh's evidence that Arabs say nabīʾat suʾ and tanabbaʾa supports the hamzated root and excludes derivation from nabāwa. TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh's report that all Arabs say tanabbaʾa with hamza supports deriving nabiʾ from the hamzated root and rules out nabāwa. TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj allows nabi to derive either from anbaʾa with hamza dropped through frequent use or from nabā, to rise. QURTUBI-FULL: Those omitting hamzah either derive the word from the same root with a softened hamzah or from nabā yanbū, 'to rise'; nabwah is elevation. NASAFI-FULL: Nabī may derive from informing, as one who tells from God, or from rising; nubuwwa is an elevated place. SAMIN-DURR: A second derivation for the non-hamzated form takes it from نبا ينبو, to rise or appear; the Prophet's rank is elevated and his position manifest. SAMIN-DURR: On the نبا derivation, النبي can be an active participle meaning elevated or a passive participle meaning God raised him above creation; its sound changes yield أنبياء. QIRAAT-ER: The hamzated nabīʾīn reading derives the word from informing rather than elevation, making the etymological distinction explicit. [14 notes: TAB-FULL(some scholars); ZAJJAJ; IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; MAWARDI-FULL; JISHUMI(الكسائي); BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI(الزجاج,سيبويه); QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; QIRAAT-ER; FARISI-HUJJA-]
- 2:61/q59/p3 From a road or path — TAB-FULL (some scholars): Some derive nabī and nubuwwa without hamza from nabwa, an elevated place, and say the original sense is a visible, raised road; they report not hearing the word hamzated. TAB-FULL (القطامي): Al-Qutami’s line uses nabiyan for a road, cited by the unhamzated etymology as evidence that nabī means a manifest road. THALABI (الكسائي): Al-Kisāʾī says nabī without hamza means “road”; a messenger is named for guiding people, and a poem uses nabī in this sense. MAWARDI-FULL: A second etymology takes nabī from “path”; the Prophet is called nabī because it is the way to him, supported by al-Qutami's line. JISHUMI (الكسائي): One etymology derives nabī from nabwa, an elevated place; al-Kisāʾī gives “path” as another derivation, since people are guided by a prophet. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Those who do not hamzate differ: some derive nabī from hamza but ease the hamza, while others derive it from naba yanbū, meaning to appear; the prophet guides to the manifest path of guidance and salvation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الزهراوي): Al-Zahrawi records نبوء for 'appeared', making a conspicuous road نبيء with hamza. QURTUBI-FULL: Without hamzah, nabī can mean a path; a messenger is called nabī because people are guided by him as by a road. QURTUBI-FULL (شاعر): An unnamed poet uses nabī for a path on the ground, supporting the unhamzated lexical sense. SAMIN-DURR: A further etymology derives النبي from الطريق, since a prophet is God's path to creation through whom people learn their Creator; the first cited poem uses نبيا to mean a road. [10 notes: TAB-FULL(some scholars,القطامي); THALABI(الكسائي); MAWARDI-FULL; JISHUMI(الكسائي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الزهراوي); QURTUBI-FULL(شاعر); SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:61/q59/p4 From departure or movement — FARISI-HUJJA (أبو زيد): Abū Zayd explains نبأت from moving from one land to another, but distinguishes this travel verb from the etymology of نبي. FARISI-HUJJA: Al-Fārisī derives نبي from نبأ, news, because a prophet reports from God, rather than from the travel verb. ALUSI-FULL (أبو زيد): Abu Zayd traces نبأت to leaving the earth; the objection may have been to the ambiguity of “expelled by God,” not to the reading itself, and a prophet's use is not automatically permitted for people. [3 notes: FARISI-HUJJA(أبو زيد); ALUSI-FULL(أبو زيد)]
- 2:61/q59/p5 The etymology is disputed — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Zuhayr's hamzated reading derives أدنأ from lowliness; the unhamzated form may also have originated with hamza and been softened. ALUSI-FULL (نافع): Nafiʿ reads النبيين with hamza, as he does النبي and النبوة. [2 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(نافع)]
- 2:61/q59/p6 The stated interpretation — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İslâm bilginleri): One proposed etymology derives nebī from nebve or nebâve, meaning high, noble, or honorable, because prophethood and its revelation bear transcendent superiority. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İkinci görüş): Another proposed etymology derives nebī from nebeʾ, “news,” because a prophet conveys information and knowledge from God to people. [2 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İkinci görüş,İslâm bilginleri)]

## 2:61/q60 What is a prophet here?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
turns on: The lexical messenger description versus the broader theological definition.
- 2:61/q60/p1 A prophet conveys God's message — DURR-FULL (الحاكم): Al-Hakim authenticated the report about the Prophet's distinction between nabīʾ and nabī. DURR-FULL (الذهبي): Al-Dhahabi rejects the Abu Dharr report as munkar and unsound. ELMALILI: Nafiʿ reads the word in its original form as nabiyyīn. SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT: He observes that accounts of the Children of Israel’s sins use the prophets term, contrasting النبيين in al-Baqara with الأنبياء in Āl ʿImrān. [4 notes: DURR-FULL(الحاكم,الذهبي); ELMALILI; SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 2:61/q60/p2 The stated interpretation — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İslâm bilginleri): Nearly all Islamic scholars define a prophet as a person chosen from humanity, given superior capacities, and assigned to convey the divine message to a community or all people. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Nebī, plural anbiyā, denotes chosen servants who mediate between God and people by conveying divine commands and prohibitions; Turkish commonly uses the Persian-derived peygamber for both nebī and rasūl. [2 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İslâm bilginleri)]

## 2:61/q61 How do nabī and rasūl relate?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q61/p1 The terms overlap, with a reported distinction — ELMALILI: Nabī is broader than rasūl—every rasūl is a nabī but not every nabī is a rasūl—although the Qur'an sometimes uses the terms synonymously. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: The etymological disagreement does not amount to a substantive disagreement over the technical definition of prophet; scholars also differ over whether nebī and rasūl mean the same or different things. [2 notes: ELMALILI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q62 Is prophethood bestowed or acquired?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q62/p1 Prophethood is God's gift — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İslâm bilginleri): Scholars agree prophethood is a bestowed divine rank, not something acquired through effort; some read the “wisdom” of 2:269 as revelation and cite it as evidence. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Bazı bilginler): Some scholars interpret the wisdom in 2:269 as revelation and use it as evidence that prophethood is divinely bestowed. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: The interpretation of Ibrahim 14:11 and Quranic statements that God sends people as messengers also show that prophethood is bestowed, not earned. [3 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Bazı bilginler,İslâm bilginleri)]

## 2:61/q63 What does faith in prophets require?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q63/p1 Faith in prophets is fundamental — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İslâm bilginleri): Faith in prophets is one of Islam’s six articles of faith; scholars who group belief into three fundamentals—divinity, prophethood, and afterlife—hold that denying prophethood removes a believer in God from the religion. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İslâm bilginleri)]

## 2:61/q64 What traits define prophets?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q64/p1 Prophets have defining truthful and trustworthy traits — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İslâm bilginleri): Scholars identify five defining prophetic traits: truthfulness, trustworthiness, sinlessness, keen judgment, and complete delivery of the divine message. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Prophets faithfully convey the truth; rare minor slips, called zalla, are corrected by revelation, and their intelligence enables them to understand and explain divine teaching accurately. [2 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İslâm bilginleri)]

## 2:61/q65 How does prophethood relate to human nature?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q65/p1 Prophets remain human servants — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Islam takes a middle course by treating prophets as God’s chosen messengers and servants rather than divine beings, unlike Christian deification of Jesus. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Prophets first believe in the truths they preach and carefully apply religious, moral, and legal rulings in their own lives. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Outside their prophetic mission, prophets share ordinary human experiences such as eating, marrying, owning property, illness, and death; attributing extraordinary qualities in these matters is impermissible. [3 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q66 Why does humanity need prophets?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q66/p1 Humanity needs prophetic guidance — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: When people leave prophetic guidance they fall into false belief, moral corruption, greed, oppression, and violence; God mercifully sends prophets to rescue humanity and show the way to happiness in both lives, with Muhammad last. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: The commentary traces modern Western departure from prophecy from deism through positivism and Marxism to atheism, warning that modern capabilities magnify threats to human values and existence and demonstrate prophecy’s necessity. [2 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q67 Why is Muhammad the final prophet?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q67/p1 Muhammad is the final prophet — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Earlier lost or altered scriptures required new revelation and prophets, but the preserved Quran leaves no need for another prophet after Muhammad; the Quran is final scripture and Muhammad the seal of prophets. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q68 What number and first prophet are reported?  [ٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ · theology]
- 2:61/q68/p1 Adam is first; later numbers are disputed — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Adam was the first human and first prophet; the Quran provides no count of later prophets and names twenty-eight, while whether Luqman, Dhu al-Qarnayn, and Uzayr were prophets or saints is disputed. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q69 Why does the verse add 'without right'?  [بِغَيْرِ ٱلْحَقِّ · rhetoric]
turns on: Emphasis, absence of any charge, and definite versus indefinite legal right.
- 2:61/q69/p1 The killing was wholly unjust — TAB-FULL: Killing prophets without right means killing God’s messengers without His authorization, while denying their missions and prophethood. THALABI: A report says the Jews killed seventy prophets in one hour early in the day; 112 Israelite worshippers ordered right and forbade wrong, and were all killed by day’s end. WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): The phrase بغير الحق grammatically modifies the killing and means that the killing was unjust. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: The charge of killing the prophets refers to siding with those who committed the killings; killing them without right means killing them unjustly. BAGHAWI-FULL: “Without right” means without any crime. BAGHAWI-FULL: Although killing prophets is always wrongful, “without right” describes the killing, as “judge with truth” describes judgment without implying that judgment divides into just and unjust kinds. TABRISI: Killing the prophets without right means without any offense, as in the killings of Zechariah and John. TABRISI: God allowed prophets to be killed so they could attain ranks unavailable otherwise; allowing murder of believers and righteous people likewise does not mean God has abandoned them. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Ḥaqq can mean a crime or offense in the phrase “without right.” IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن الأنباري): Bi-ghayri l-ḥaqq can mean without any crime or offense. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: A third view describes their killing as unjust, just as God’s judgment is described as right without implying He judges otherwise. RAZI-FULL: One answer to why the text says without right is that people may commit a wrong act believing it justified through a doubt, but these people knew their killing was wrong and did it anyway. SAMIN-DURR: بغير الحق is either a circumstantial phrase describing the killers as acting wrongly, or an adjective to an omitted verbal noun meaning an unlawful killing. SAMIN-DURR: باء بكذا can also mean acknowledge or admit; the cited prophetic report means accepting the favor and binding oneself to it, and poetry uses بؤت بحقها. SAMIN-DURR (الراغب): Al-Raghib says interpreting بؤت بحقها as I admitted its right does not follow the word's lexical implication. BIQAI-FULL: Because a prophet is protected in religion and worldly life, killing him is never truly justified; the phrase stresses the absence of fully clear right, even if killers claim a pretext such as suspecting false prophethood. BIQAI-FULL: The blame concerns shedding blood without complete clarity, consistent with the prohibition against killing a protected person except by right. BIQAI-FULL: A claimed suspicion, such as believing someone falsely claimed prophethood, does not excuse killing without certainty. ALUSI-FULL: The apparent syntax makes بغير الحق a contested modifier governed by both disbelief and killing; al-Bahr attaches it to the nearby killing alone. ALUSI-FULL: A more apparent reading takes الحق generically, meaning no right at all; the parallel wording in 3:112 supports this and means the killings were unjust even in their own view. ALUSI-FULL: The phrase may simply expose the escalating ugliness of their conduct—killing a prophet, killing a group, and doing so without right—which best fits the ordinary sense of prohibition regardless of the killer's beliefs. IBNASHUR-FULL: Zechariah, John’s father, was killed by Herod while trying to save his son from execution during Jesus’s prophethood. [22 notes: TAB-FULL; THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن الأنباري); RAZI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الراغب); BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL+]
- 2:61/q69/p2 It intensifies condemnation — WAHIDI-BASIT (قيل): Another view treats بغير الحق as emphatic, since killing a prophet could only be unlawful; the text gives ‘hearts that are in the chests’ as a parallel. IBNATIYYA-FULL: بغير الحق intensifies the atrocity and sin: although no prophet could rightly be killed, the phrase rules out a conceivable excuse; no prophet committed a crime warranting execution, and God allowed some to be killed as an honor raising their rank, like martyrs among believers. TABRISI: The qualifier 'without right' does not imply that prophets could ever be killed justly; it emphasizes that their killing was sheer injustice, like the cited Quranic and poetic examples. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: A second view treats bi-ghayri l-ḥaqq as emphatic, as in the statement that hearts in breasts grow blind. QURTUBI-FULL: 'Without right' magnifies the enormity of the killing; it does not imply any prophet could deserve execution, since none did an act requiring it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: بغير الحق attaches to وتقتلون and is either a circumstantial phrase or a qualifier of an omitted verbal noun; both analyses are emphatic. SAMIN-DURR: Another explanation treats بغير الحق as a description of the killing's injustice to the prophets, intensifying the condemnation of the crime. ELMALILI: The added phrase “without right” emphasizes the heinousness and deliberate character of killing prophets, since it plainly violated divine judgment; it also alludes to their hostile intent against the Seal of the Prophets. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The author reads indefinite ḥaqq in 3:112 as killing the prophets without any right or cause whatsoever, intensifying the condemnation; definite al-ḥaqq in 2:61 refers to the recognized category of right. [9 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(قيل); IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; ELMALILI; SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:61/q69/p3 No valid charge even by their standards — WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأنباري): The prophets had committed no offense that could warrant their blood or expel them from prophethood; a prophet can never lawfully be killed. KASHSHAF-FULL: Although prophet-killing is inherently unjust, 'without right' means even by their own standards the prophets had committed no offense deserving death; they only advised them. RAZI-FULL: By contrast, the indefinite phrase without any right denies that there was any justification of any kind. NASAFI-FULL: Killing them without right means even by their own standard; the Torah did not warrant their killing, and the phrase is a circumstantial accusative describing wrongful killing. SAMIN-DURR (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari asks why the verse adds بغير الحق when prophets could only be killed unlawfully; he answers that the killers themselves had no justifying case against them. BURSEVI: Shuʿayb, Zechariah, and John are named among the prophets they killed; “without right” stresses that even they did not regard the killings as justified. IBNASHUR-FULL: بغير الحق means without a valid ground in their own law; this indicts them by their scripture, although killing prophets can never be justified. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The author reads indefinite ḥaqq in 3:112 as killing the prophets without any right or cause whatsoever, intensifying the condemnation; definite al-ḥaqq in 2:61 refers to the recognized category of right. [8 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأنباري); KASHSHAF-FULL; RAZI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري); BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL; SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:61/q69/p4 Known right versus no right at all — RAZI-FULL: The definite phrase without the right refers to the recognized legal grounds for killing among Muslims, as in the Prophetic report listing three cases in which Muslim blood is lawful. RAZI-FULL: By contrast, the indefinite phrase without any right denies that there was any justification of any kind. BURHAN: The same accusation appears with the definite phrase in al-Baqara and without the article in Al Imran, illustrating variation in definiteness. BURHAN: Al Imran uses indefinite حق in a generic conditional statement; al-Baqara's definite الحق concerns a familiar rationale used by the people being described. ALUSI-FULL: One explanation of “without right” is that they knew the murders were unjust: worldly desire, caprice, and excess drove them; on this reading the definite article points to a known right. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The definite الحق in 2:61 means the known right that could authorize killing; the passage introduces its contrast with indefinite حق in 3:112 without yet explaining that form. SAMARRAI-ASRAR: The author reads indefinite ḥaqq in 3:112 as killing the prophets without any right or cause whatsoever, intensifying the condemnation; definite al-ḥaqq in 2:61 refers to the recognized category of right. [7 notes: RAZI-FULL; BURHAN; ALUSI-FULL; SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:61/q69/p5 Unjust human killing versus God's taking life — RAZI-FULL: A third answer distinguishes God's just taking of life from people killing without right, forestalling the objection that God also kills. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]

## 2:61/q70 How is the final causal clause parsed?  [ذَٰلِكَ بِمَا عَصَوا۟ وَّكَانُوا۟ يَعْتَدُونَ · grammar]
- 2:61/q70/p1 Bimā marks cause and the verbs form the causal construction — AKHFASH: In بما عصوا, ما عصوا functions as a noun meaning “their disobedience.” TAB-FULL (أعشى بني ثعلبة): Al-Tabari cites al-Aʿsha’s use of بما as evidence that it expresses the cause or reason for an event. NAHHAS (الأخفش): He interprets بما عصوا as ‘because of their disobedience’; كانوا يعتدون is coordinated with عصوا. WAHIDI-BASIT (الأخفش): He parses ما with the verb as a verbal noun: the disbelief and killing occurred through disobedience, abandonment of commands, and failure to obey. QURTUBI-FULL (الأخفش): Al-Akhfash interprets bimā as 'because of their disobedience'; disobedience opposes obedience, while transgression exceeds limits, especially in injustice and sins. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: ما in بما عصوا is a verbal noun marker, so the phrase means because of their disobedience. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: اعتداء is not coordinated with عصيان to preserve the verse endings and suggest transgression had become their habitual, continual act. SAMIN-DURR: The final و of عصوا assimilates into the following و because the preceding vowel is fatḥa, with no lengthened vowel to block assimilation; اهتدوا وإن تولوا illustrates the same pattern. SAMIN-DURR: ما is a verbal noun particle and باء is causal, so the clause means because of their disobedience; عصوا is a relative clause with no syntactic case. SAMIN-DURR: عصوا derives from عصيوا: the ياء took fatḥa after a vowel change, then was deleted as the first of two quiescent letters, leaving the pattern فعوا. SAMIN-DURR: كانوا يعتدون is an accusative predicate of كان, and that clause coordinates with the relative clause of ما. SAMIN-DURR: يعتدون originates as يعتديون and undergoes the same deletion and sound change as يتقون, yielding the pattern يفتعون. [12 notes: AKHFASH; TAB-FULL(أعشى بني ثعلبة); NAHHAS(الأخفش); WAHIDI-BASIT(الأخفش); QURTUBI-FULL(الأخفش); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:61/q71 How do disobedience and transgression relate to the earlier offenses and punishment?  [ذَٰلِكَ بِمَا عَصَوا۟ وَّكَانُوا۟ يَعْتَدُونَ · interpretation]
turns on: Restated punishment cause versus the earlier sins leading to disbelief and killing.
- 2:61/q71/p1 Earlier sins led to disbelief and murder — TAB-FULL: Tabari explains that the punishment came because of their disobedience, unbelief, and transgression. TAB-FULL: The punishment followed their disobedience to God’s command and crossing the limits into what He prohibited. IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatada warns them to avoid disobedience and aggression, the causes of earlier peoples' destruction. WAHIDI-BASIT: Here disbelief and disobedience coincide and each entails the other, though disobedience is not disbelief in every context; alternatively, an omitted noun makes it the ill effect of their disobedience. WAHIDI-BASIT (كثير من المفسرين): Many exegetes restrict the disobedience and transgression to violating the Sabbath; others take them generally as sins, exceeding God’s commands, and violating His prohibitions. BAGHAWI-FULL: Their disobedience means they exceeded God's commands and committed His prohibitions. KASHSHAF-FULL: The demonstrative may point back to disbelief and killing as the result of persistent disobedience and transgression, which hardened hearts until they dared deny signs and kill prophets. KASHSHAF-FULL: Alternatively, it may refer to the disbelief and killing together with their other disobedience. TABRISI: After listing God's favors to the Israelites, the passage recounts their ingratitude and disobedient choice. BAYDAWI-FULL: Minor sins lead to major sins, just as minor acts of obedience lead toward greater acts. NASAFI-FULL: The repeated that may refer to sins generally, or specifically to Sabbath transgression; alternatively unbelief and killing arose from persistent disobedience and excess that hardened their hearts. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: من الله may attach to باءوا if باء means return, suggesting they turned away from God through disobedience; alternatively it adjectivally describes غضب, the clearer analysis with the meanings deserve or settle. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Prior disobedience and transgression emboldened them to reject signs and kill prophets, since sins pave the way to disbelief. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The cited verses describe deeds darkening hearts and connect the claim that hearts are veiled to God's curse and their disbelief. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another interpretation maps disobedience to breaking the covenant and transgression to killing many prophets. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another gloss describes transgression as long-lasting disobedience and persistence in it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The poet's lines illustrate how small beginnings can stir great consequences, fitting the claim that earlier disobedience grew into disbelief and murder. SAMIN-DURR: عصيان originally denotes firmness, while اعتداء is تجاوز derived from عدا يعدو; the verse leaves both verbs' objects unstated to include every form of disobedience and transgression. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Disobedience means doing what is forbidden; transgression means exceeding the limits of what is permitted or commanded, and this is an additional cause of their punishment. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says anger falls on one who defies the Majestic through disobedience, so the text next states their deeds. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says prohibitions imply that people may violate them and commands that they may obey, since commands and bans display the distinction between the obedient and disobedient rather than compel what nature already does. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says their disobedience brought the humiliation God announced, and that corrupting the God-created bodies of human beings is an especially grave form of corruption. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines disobedience as violating a command. BURSEVI: Repeated minor sins lead to major sins, just as persistence in small acts of obedience leads toward greater acts. ALUSI-FULL: The following phrase gives the cause behind their disbelief and killings: earlier disobedience and transgression lead one sin to another, with the first cause emphasized because it might seem unexpected. ELMALILI: They made rebellion habitual and continually exceeded limits; persistence in lesser sins leads to major sins, persistence in major sins to disbelief, which enables every atrocity. IBNASHUR-FULL (الفخر): Al-Fakhr holds that requesting another blessing for a known purpose is not itself disobedience. IBNASHUR-FULL: The author rejects the sin-and-pardon interpretation as weak: ‘go down’ grants no favor, and the request gives no evidence of sin, since asking to move from one favor to another for a familiar purpose is not disobedience. IBNASHUR-FULL: The second ذالك may repeat the first reference to punishment, adding disobedience and transgression as further causes; repetition can serve instead of conjunction. IBNASHUR-FULL: Alternatively, the second ذالك may refer to disbelief and killing the prophets, which caused the punishment; persistent small sins lead into greater ones. ASAD-NOTES: The auxiliary kanu signals continuity or repeated persistence in their wrongdoing. [31 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); WAHIDI-BASIT(كثير من المفسرين); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(الفخر); ASAD-NOTES]
- 2:61/q71/p2 Their sins caused punishment — TAB-FULL: Tabari explains that the punishment came because of their disobedience, unbelief, and transgression. TAB-FULL: The punishment followed their disobedience to God’s command and crossing the limits into what He prohibited. IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatada warns them to avoid disobedience and aggression, the causes of earlier peoples' destruction. THALABI: “That was because they disobeyed and transgressed” means they went beyond God’s command and committed His prohibitions. THALABI: Without hamza, the word may reflect deletion of hamza for ease because it is frequent, or mean elevated, from nabūʾa, as in the cited line about a side lifted from the bed. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says the name is declined because it is masculine and was given to a masculine place-name, like other short names. TABRISI: Nabi derives from nabaʾ, news, because a prophet reports from God. TABRISI (المازني): Al-Mazini challenges al-Farra's claim that deletion is poetic necessity because the line could instead say fa-lyadun minni. SAMIN-DURR (ابن عطية): Ibn Atiyya argues the report is weak because the Prophet did not object when al-Abbas recited يا خاتم النبآء, and the singular should not differ from the plural. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Because their request came from arrogance and excess rather than need, they were not granted it. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Disobedience means doing what is forbidden; transgression means exceeding the limits of what is permitted or commanded, and this is an additional cause of their punishment. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says anger falls on one who defies the Majestic through disobedience, so the text next states their deeds. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali says their disobedience brought the humiliation God announced, and that corrupting the God-created bodies of human beings is an especially grave form of corruption. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali defines transgression as deliberately crossing a limit without excuse, because the person had been given latitude and the boundary was set. ALUSI-FULL (الرازي): Al-Razi argues that the request could not be sinful because God answered it, since answering a sinful request would itself be sinful and prophets cannot do that. ALUSI-FULL: The following phrase gives the cause behind their disbelief and killings: earlier disobedience and transgression lead one sin to another, with the first cause emphasized because it might seem unexpected. IBNASHUR-FULL: The emphatic إن treats them as doubters because their request is long delayed and their longing makes them suspect they may not obtain it. IBNASHUR-FULL: The second ذالك may repeat the first reference to punishment, adding disobedience and transgression as further causes; repetition can serve instead of conjunction. IBNASHUR-FULL: Alternatively, the second ذالك may refer to disbelief and killing the prophets, which caused the punishment; persistent small sins lead into greater ones. [19 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج); TABRISI(المازني); SAMIN-DURR(ابن عطية); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ALUSI-FULL(الرازي); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:61/q71/p3 Sabbath violation is specified — WAHIDI-BASIT (كثير من المفسرين): Many exegetes restrict the disobedience and transgression to violating the Sabbath; others take them generally as sins, exceeding God’s commands, and violating His prohibitions. KASHSHAF-FULL (قيل): Another view specifies their transgression as violating the Sabbath. NASAFI-FULL: The repeated that may refer to sins generally, or specifically to Sabbath transgression; alternatively unbelief and killing arose from persistent disobedience and excess that hardened their hearts. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Another explanation identifies the transgression as their violation of the Sabbath prohibition. [4 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(كثير من المفسرين); KASHSHAF-FULL(قيل); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q71/p4 Repeated wrongdoing caused their fate — WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Their disbelief and killing resulted from the evil consequence of committing sins and transgressing God's command. ALUSI-FULL: The causal باء introduces an interpreted verbal noun; the nominal wording highlights repeated disbelief and killing over time or vividly recalls their ugly acts. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Some listed sins occurred generations after Moses, but the verse recalls them alongside the wilderness complaint to warn Muslims and the Prophet’s Jews against repeating ancestral sins for worldly gain. [3 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ; ALUSI-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q72 What does transgression mean here?  [يَعْتَدُونَ · meaning]
- 2:61/q72/p1 Crossing God's limits into wrongdoing — TAB-FULL: Transgression is crossing a boundary God set for His servants and passing beyond it. IBNATIYYA-FULL: يعتدون means transgressing limits; transgression applies broadly to crossing boundaries and is conventionally used for injustice and sins. TABRISI: Iʿtidāʾ is crossing the limit God set for His servants, or any limit of one thing into another. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: ʿUdwān is more severe wrongdoing. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj defines transgression as exceeding the proper measure in anything. RAZI-FULL: They transgressed means they acted unjustly, crossing from right into falsehood. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قيل): Transgression is crossing God's boundary from what is right into falsehood. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (شاعر): A second poetic line says people may underestimate evil even as it grows. BIQAI-FULL: Their transgression was an ingrained habit that continued and emboldened them to commit grave acts. [9 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج); RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(شاعر,قيل); BIQAI-FULL]

## 2:61/q73 How is the verse read inwardly?  [* · inward]
- 2:61/q73/p1 The request signifies descent into bodily desires — SULAMI (قيل): People fall into two groups: those freed from self-direction and content with God's decree keep increasing, while those left to their own choices remain tossed about until they perish. SULAMI: The rebuke means they exchanged God's primordial choice for them for disobedient asking and desire; God's word does not change. SULAMI (الواسطي): Al-Wasiti says they abandoned manna and quails, which came without effort, for food that suited their appetites and ordinary nature. QASHANI: The Israelites’ one food is spiritual nourishment through knowledge, gnosis, and wisdom. QASHANI: Their request to Moses means asking God to permit the soul’s base desires, low pleasures, stale amusements, and other ego-centered satisfactions. QASHANI: Egypt signifies the city of the body; humiliation is the condition following pursuit of desires and greed for possessions, while misery means lasting need and dwelling in the lower realm. QASHANI: Incurring divine wrath signifies deserving distance from and expulsion by God; their downfall follows from being veiled from His signs and manifestations. QASHANI: On a second inward reading, killing the prophets means attacking the heart’s prophets without a sound warrant, through falsehood, disobedience to heart and intellect, and transgression against their guidance. BURSEVI (التأويلات): The lower self asks its heart, figured as Moses, for bodily produce: animal desires and physical pleasures; the answer is to exchange the perishing for the enduring. BURSEVI (التأويلات): The inward Egypt is the lower body, reached by descending from the high stations of the spirit; its base demands bring beast-like degradation. BURSEVI (التأويلات): Inward disbelief means denying unseen inspirations and spiritual unveilings; killing prophets means nullifying divinely opened reports of the unseen and denying their secrets. BURSEVI (التأويلات): These losses result from disobedience and breaking the covenant of striving in worship; transgression is seeking something other than God. BURSEVI: In the inward reading, the servant has a will, but God’s will is better; “descend” means leaving surrender and divine governance for self-directed choice. BURSEVI: Choosing and planning for oneself alongside God’s governance brings humiliation and poverty. BURSEVI (التأويلات): The inward analogy says Israel could not endure one heavenly food because of low nature, just as the lower human self cannot be content with one spiritual nourishment from God. BURSEVI (النبي عليه السلام): The Prophet says he is unlike others because he spends the night with his Lord, who feeds and gives him drink, modeling the soul’s endurance of spiritual nourishment. ALUSI-FULL: The spiritual lesson is that they refused God's decree, gratitude, and patience; tyranny and abasement took hold, worldly love filled their hearts, and they fell from their high station. ALUSI-FULL: In the mystical reading, the one food is wisdom and knowledge, while earthly growth symbolizes vile desires and low pleasures arising from the base self in the body's Egypt; tasting them brings humiliation, poverty, and ruin. ALUSI-FULL: The mystical cause of this demand is veiling from divine signs, darkening hearts through sin, and heedlessness of the Beloved that replaces the sought goal with other things; the author asks for well-being. [19 notes: SULAMI(الواسطي,قيل); QASHANI; BURSEVI(التأويلات,النبي عليه السلام); ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:61/q74 How do reciters combine variants in this passage?  [* · readings]
turns on: Word-by-word Egyptian method versus transmitter-by-transmitter Syrian method.
- 2:61/q74/p1 Recitation methods vary — NASHR: Combining by word repeats each word with a foundational or word-specific variant, stopping when possible or joining to the last face; for two-word differences, the reader stops after the second word and completes the options. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:61/q74/p2 Word by word combination — NASHR: The Egyptian word-by-word method is more reliable for exhausting variants and easier to learn, but it detracts from the flow and beauty of recitation. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:61/q74/p3 Each transmitter to a stop — NASHR: The Syrian method reads one transmitter’s face to a permissible stop, then repeats to that stop with the next reader; the author learned both methods and combines them, prioritizing the next reader most compatible with the first and observing good performance. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:61/q74/p4 A combined method — NASHR: The Syrian method reads one transmitter’s face to a permissible stop, then repeats to that stop with the next reader; the author learned both methods and combines them, prioritizing the next reader most compatible with the first and observing good performance. [1 notes: NASHR]

## 2:61/q75 What larger lesson or link is drawn from the verse?  [* · interpretation]
- 2:61/q75/p1 The verse warns against ingratitude and recalls Israel's history — BIQAI-FULL: The history warns this Muslim community against following the People of the Book; the next comprehensive verse begins with the believers among this community before treating the other religious communities. ALUSI-FULL: The episode resembles their earlier resistance after hearing God's speech, followed by their destruction and then the gift of life. IBNASHUR-FULL: Gratitude means displaying a blessing’s intended effects; failing to do so merits its removal and replacement with its opposite. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: To return to their homeland as an independent, honorable society, they needed fidelity to God and Moses, acceptance of his teaching, and willingness to endure material hardship. [4 notes: BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:61/q76 What wilderness narrative does Biqāʿī quote from the Torah?  [* · reports]
- 2:61/q76/p1 Biqāʿī quotes a continuous departure and wilderness account — BIQAI-FULL: The Qur'anic sequence of Israel's stories is not chronological; the author says the Torah's important correspondences explain its arrangement. BIQAI-FULL: The author says he consulted a current ninth-century Jewish copy of the Torah's fourth book, in an Arabic translation marked with reading times, and compared it with the meaning alongside a Jewish reader. BIQAI-FULL: The Torah itinerary places Israel's departure from Rameses, also called Ain Shams in a variant, on the fifteenth of Nisan after Passover, with a variant dating it one day after Passover; Egyptians were burying their firstborn as Israel left armed. BIQAI-FULL: The cited Torah says Egypt was occupied with burying its slain firstborn and that the Lord had taken vengeance on the Egyptians' gods as Israel departed. BIQAI-FULL: The Torah itinerary continues along the Red Sea coast, with variant names for the sea and several wilderness encampments through Rithmah. BIQAI-FULL: At Rephidim there was no drinking water; the itinerary gives a variant name for the station. BIQAI-FULL (موسى): Moses commands Israel to remember its liberation from Egyptian slavery through the annual festival of unleavened bread, observed for seven days, and to teach its meaning to their children. BIQAI-FULL (موسى): Moses says the observance is a sign and memorial because God brought Israel out with a mighty hand, and it should be kept year after year. BIQAI-FULL (موسى): The Torah passage commands consecrating every firstborn male animal to God, redeeming it with a lamb or slaughtering it, and redeeming each firstborn son. BIQAI-FULL (موسى): When a child asks why the rite is performed, the parent is to recount that God brought Israel out of bondage and killed Egypt's firstborn, explaining the sacrifice and redemption of Israel's own firstborn. BIQAI-FULL: The Torah says God avoided the nearby Palestine road lest Israel see battle and flee back to Egypt, leading them through the Sea of Reeds wilderness instead. BIQAI-FULL: Israel left armed, while Moses carried Joseph's bones because Joseph had made Israel swear to take them when God remembered and brought them out. BIQAI-FULL: God led them by a cloud pillar in daylightand a fire pillar at night for shade and light, and neither pillar left the people. BIQAI-FULL (الرب): God tells Moses to camp by the sea so Pharaoh will think Israel is lost, then says He will harden Pharaoh's heart, gain glory through him and his army, and teach Egypt that He is Lord. BIQAI-FULL: Pharaoh regrets releasing Israel and pursues with six hundred thousand chosen riders and Egyptian forces; Israel sees the pursuers and panics. BIQAI-FULL (بنو إسرائيل): Israel cries to Moses that Egypt had enough graves and that slavery there would have been better than dying in the wilderness. BIQAI-FULL (موسى): Moses tells the people not to fear: they will witness God's deliverance, will not see the Egyptians again, and God will fight for them while they remain calm. BIQAI-FULL (الرب): God tells Moses to command Israel to move and to lift and strike the sea so the people can cross dry; He will harden Egypt and gain glory through Pharaoh's forces. BIQAI-FULL: The divine pillar moves behind Israel between their camp and Egypt's, with darkness for the Egyptians and light for Israel, preventing approach throughout the night. BIQAI-FULL: The Lord drives the sea back with a hot wind all night, leaving dry ground and walls of water on both sides as Israel crosses. BIQAI-FULL: Egypt's chariots and horses pursue Israel into the sea; at dawn God terrifies the army and disables its chariots, prompting the Egyptians to recognize that the Lord fights for Israel. BIQAI-FULL (الرب): God commands Moses to stretch his hand over the sea; the waters return and drown Pharaoh's forces, with no survivor. BIQAI-FULL: Israel is saved, sees the dead Egyptians and God's great act against them, then fears God and believes and trusts the words of Moses. BIQAI-FULL (موسى وبنو إسرائيل): Moses and Israel sing praise to God as the glorious, eternal warrior who drowned Pharaoh's army; their song describes divine power, rescue, and kingship forever. BIQAI-FULL: The song says surrounding peoples trembled, Israel was led toward the sacred mountain, and God would reign forever; afterward they traveled three days through Shur without water and grumbled at bitter Marah. BIQAI-FULL: God promised evening meat and morning bread so Israel would know that he was their Lord. BIQAI-FULL: Quail covered the camp at evening, and morning dew surrounded it before manna appeared. BIQAI-FULL: The manna appeared as flaky material on the ground, resembling frost. BIQAI-FULL: Moses identified the manna as God's bread and instructed each person to gather one measure according to need for every member of the household. BIQAI-FULL: Although some gathered much and others little, the measured amounts left no surplus for the first or shortage for the latter. BIQAI-FULL: They reached the wilderness of Sin between Elim and Sinai on the fifteenth day of the second month after leaving Egypt. BIQAI-FULL: The Israelites grumbled against Moses and Aaron, longing for Egypt's meat and bread and accusing them of bringing the people into the wilderness to die of hunger. BIQAI-FULL: God promised bread from heaven and daily gathering as a test of whether Israel would keep his commands. BIQAI-FULL: God commanded a double collection on the sixth day. BIQAI-FULL: Moses and Aaron told the people that God had brought them from Egypt and that they would see his glory the next morning; their complaint had reached him. BIQAI-FULL: Moses said God would give meat in the evening and bread in the morning, and that the people's complaint was against God rather than his messengers. BIQAI-FULL: Aaron brought the congregation near; God's glory appeared in a cloud, and God said he had heard their grumbling. BIQAI-FULL: Some disobeyed Moses by keeping manna overnight; it became wormy and foul, angering Moses. BIQAI-FULL: The measure is glossed as ten jarīb, that is, ten waybas. BIQAI-FULL: Manna was gathered each morning and melted when the sun grew hot. BIQAI-FULL: On the sixth day each person gathered two measures; Moses explained that the next day was a holy Sabbath of rest. BIQAI-FULL: The people were to bake and cook what they needed and keep the remainder cold for the following day. BIQAI-FULL: The saved manna did not spoil; Moses said to eat it on the Sabbath, when none could be gathered in the field, and to gather on the other six days. BIQAI-FULL: Some went out to gather on the seventh day but found none; God rebuked their refusal to accept his commands, and the people rested. BIQAI-FULL: Israel called the food manna; it resembled a coriander seed and tasted like honey. BIQAI-FULL: The fourth book describes manna as coriander-like and white like the mahā, then ground, pounded, cooked into a dish, and tasting like oil-kneaded bread. BIQAI-FULL: Israel ate manna for forty years, until reaching inhabited territory and Canaan. BIQAI-FULL: Al-Biqāʿī cites the Torah's wilderness account: the tabernacle was Israel's qibla, with cloud and night fire signaling when to camp or travel and shading them from the sun. BIQAI-FULL: This segment completes the wilderness narrative: silver horns summoned Israel to the meeting tent and called commanders with one blast; the ark went ahead and the cloud shaded travelers from the sun. BIQAI-FULL: When the people grumbled, fire from God burned the camp's outskirts; Moses prayed and it subsided, so the place was named Burning. BIQAI-FULL: A mixed crowd craved meat and recalled Egypt's fish, cucumbers, melons, leeks, onions, and garlic; they complained that only manna remained to eat. BIQAI-FULL: Moses said he could not carry the whole nation alone and asked God to kill him rather than let him see further harm. BIQAI-FULL: God appointed seventy elders and shared Moses' spirit with them; a wind brought quail, then a severe plague struck the people while they ate, naming the site Graves of Lust. BIQAI-FULL: After the quail plague, the people left Graves of Lust, reached Hazeroth, and remained there seven days. [54 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الرب,بنو إسرائيل,موسى,موسى وبنو إسرائيل)]

## 2:61/q77 What inimitability arguments cite this verse's wording?  [* · theology]
turns on: Competing theories of inimitability and objections using this wording.
- 2:61/q77/p1 Verbal variety is disputed evidence — ALUSI-FULL: The passage lists apparent differences in Qur'anic statements, including denial that people will ask one another alongside a later description of them asking, and two formulations limiting what prevents belief. ALUSI-FULL: Examples of verbal variation include wool described once as ṣūf and once as ʿihn, and the ordering of poverty and humiliation in the cited phrase. ALUSI-FULL: Further alleged inconsistencies include calling the Prophet a father, varying the material from which Adam was created, repetitions in wording and meaning, and explaining that three plus seven days equals ten complete. ALUSI-FULL (عثمان): Uthman is reported to have said that the Qur'an contains grammatical irregularities that the Arabs will set right with their tongues. [4 notes: ALUSI-FULL(عثمان)]
- 2:61/q77/p2 The proposed criterion is challenged — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): An objection argues that even freedom from all such defects would not establish inimitability, since sermons and poetry show the same feature and the claim scales to short surahs. ALUSI-FULL: The author objects that the diversion theory makes the miracle the act of diversion rather than the Qur'an itself, contrary to the Muslims' prior consensus. [2 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:61/q77/p3 Divine diversion from rivalry — Against — ALUSI-FULL: He further argues that if each Arab was diverted individually, producing a like text would be customary for each; ordinary speech is not eloquent, so the theory wrongly entails that the Qur'an itself is not eloquent. ALUSI-FULL (الأستاذ أبو إسحاق الأسفراييني والنظام): Abu Ishaq al-Isfarayini and al-Nazzam say the miracle is that God diverted eloquent Arabs' motives from attempting a rival; al-Murtada instead says their necessary knowledge for competing was removed. Against — ALUSI-FULL: The author objects that the diversion theory makes the miracle the act of diversion rather than the Qur'an itself, contrary to the Muslims' prior consensus. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الأستاذ أبو إسحاق الأسفراييني والنظام); ALUSI-FULL-]
- 2:61/q77/p4 Agreement with reason or subtle meaning — Against — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): A proposed criterion makes the Qur'an miraculous through agreement with reason and subtle meaning; the author rejects it as common among eloquent speech and says it also applies to non-miraculous prophetic speech and the Torah and Gospel. [0 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)-]
- 2:61/q77/p5 Pre-eternity of speech — Against — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another view locates inimitability in the Qur'an's pre-eternity; the author objects that this would make all divine attributes miraculous and that an unobservable eternal speech cannot be the object of a challenge. [0 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)-]

## 2:61/q78 How does the verse relate to the Torah's food account?  [* · links]
- 2:61/q78/p1 Numbers 11 parallels the complaint and quail account — IBNASHUR-FULL: The verse alludes to the Torah's account: after leaving Sinai/Horeb for Paran in year two and traveling toward Hebron, Israelites remembered free fish and produce in Egypt, complained about manna, and wept; God was angered, Moses sought pardon, and God sent quail, which they stored for a month. ASAD-NOTES: Moses asks whether they would trade freedom for the meager comforts of Egyptian captivity; in Sinai they longed for Egypt's comparative security, as Numbers 11 and Moses' next sentence indicate. STUDYQURAN: Numbers 11:4–5 parallels Israel's craving for foods remembered from Egypt, including cucumbers, onions, and garlic. STUDYQURAN: Numbers 11:31–35 says quails were given, but those who craved them were struck down for greed toward God's provision. [4 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL; ASAD-NOTES; STUDYQURAN]
